**The gap this closed was not in the plan at all.** `bindings/python` is a workspace *member* but never a *default* member — the root package is itself a member, so Cargo scopes bare commands to it alone. That is deliberate, it is what keeps `cargo publish` a one-package operation ([D-098](s13-decision-register.md#d-098)), and `tests/packaging_tests.rs` pins it. Its cost went unnoticed for three phases: **nothing in CI ever compiled `macrame-py`.** `ci.yml`'s clippy is scoped to `macrame-db` by that same default, and the only thing that built the binding was `wheels.yml`, on tags. A pull request could break every file P1–P4 wrote and all four checks would go green. The lint job names `-p macrame-py` explicitly, and deliberately does not pass `--features extension-module`: the binding manifest states the crate must build without it, and a check that quietly required it would make that statement untestable.

Three properties are tested rather than trusted to review, because each fails silently: that the suite runs through `run_suite.py` rather than bare pytest ([D-107](s13-decision-register.md#d-107) — replacing it reads as tidying), that the publish job still needs the suite, and that the `requires-python` floor is a version some job actually runs. All four tripwires were verified by injection.

The `abi3` job is the one nothing else covers. Every other job builds and runs on a single interpreter, so none of them tests the claim that funds the small wheel matrix: *one wheel serves CPython 3.10 through whatever ships next* ([D-106](s13-decision-register.md#d-106)). It builds on 3.10, asserts the artifact is tagged `abi3` rather than for one version, and runs the whole suite through 3.13 against it. If pyo3's `abi3-py310` feature were ever dropped, every other job here would stay green while the matrix silently became wrong by a factor of five.

<a id="d-109"></a>D-109 — the type stub is **hand-written**, and it is checked against the extension in both directions rather than trusted (0.7.0). [D-100](s13-decision-register.md#d-100), [D-104](s13-decision-register.md#d-104), [D-107](s13-decision-register.md#d-107), [§14.15](s14-python-bindings.md#1415-the-stub-and-what-keeps-it-true).

`pyo3-stub-gen` exists and was the obvious choice. It costs a build step, a proc-macro attribute on every `#[pymethods]` block, and generated output that still needs hand-editing wherever the Python signature is more precise than the Rust one — which here is most of the interesting ones. A generator writes `Optional[Any]` for a timestamp; the boundary accepts `str | datetime | None`, refuses naive datetimes, and answers with an aware UTC `datetime`. That difference *is* the value of the stub, and none of it survives generation.

The same argument applies to the open interval (`None`, never a sentinel datetime), to embeddings (`bytes | Sequence[float]`, one fast path and one convenient one), and to `astar`'s `heuristic` — `Callable[[str, str], float]`, which a generator would render as `Any` because pyo3 sees a `PyObject`.

**A stub is documentation, so it has documentation's failure mode: it is not executed, and nothing notices when it stops being true.** The specific defect is narrow and predictable — a method added in Rust and never stubbed. It works perfectly at runtime; the symptom is `"Database" has no attribute "…"` in somebody else's editor, months later. So `tests_py/test_stubs.py` parses the `.pyi` with `ast` and compares it against the live module in both directions, class by class and member by member.

Exception attributes cannot be compared that way. The mapping layer sets them with `setattr` on the *raised instance*, so `hasattr(NotFoundError, "id")` is correctly False, and the class carries no trace of what a raised error will hold. They are compared against `errors.rs` instead — the source that writes them, and the file that changes when a `DbError` variant gains a field. That alone would be two documents agreeing with each other, so a third test pushes every variant through `_raise_db_error` and asserts the raised object really carries what the stub promises: **stub → source → runtime**, closed.

What the name comparison cannot see is a wrong *type*: `-> int` where the runtime answers a `datetime` satisfies every check above. Only a checker reads annotations, so `mypy --strict` over `python/macrame` runs in CI ([§14.14](s14-python-bindings.md#1414-ci)), measured clean. Both halves are needed and neither subsumes the other.

`py.typed` ships beside the stub, and both are asserted against the *installed* package rather than the repository — being packaged is decided at install time, and maturin copies the `python-source` directory by default rather than by declaration. Without the marker a checker ignores the stub entirely and every call silently type-checks as `Any`, which is the failure that looks most like success.


<a id="d-110"></a>D-110 — the Rust suite is gated by a **classifier**, not a retry loop, and the quarantined step's budget is raised because of it (0.8.0). [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030), [D-107](s13-decision-register.md#d-107), [R15](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#r15), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy).

`ci.yml` answered R15 with `for attempt in 1 2 3`, twice, and `main` went red on two consecutive documentation-only commits (runs `30706318073` and `30706474231`) with a four-line diagnostic: attempt 1 failed, attempt 2 failed, attempt 3 failed, property tests failed three times. Nothing in it says whether libSQL died or a property found a real defect, which are the two things a maintainer needs to tell apart before doing anything at all.

**Two separate defects, and the count was the smaller one.** The budget was calibrated on the wrong step: *"R15 has always passed on re-run"* is a statement about the main suite, where `RUST_TEST_THREADS = "1"` applies. The quarantined binaries are quarantined precisely because serialising does not save them — `doctrine_property_tests` alone measured **9 crashes in 15 runs** at 0.7.0. Three attempts against p≈0.6 goes red about 22% of the time, which is the 2-in-9 observed. But raising the number would have left the real defect untouched: **a retry loop cannot distinguish noise from a result**, so it re-runs genuine failures twice for nothing, and re-running a flaky assertion until it passes is how a flaky assertion becomes permanent.

[D-107](s13-decision-register.md#d-107) solved exactly this for the Python suite and the lesson was never brought back. `scripts/run_rust_suite.py` classifies each run as `CRASH`, `FAILED`, `INCOMPLETE`, `TEARDOWN` or `BUILD`, and retries **only** `CRASH`. The two files are deliberately shaped alike and deliberately do not share an implementation: pytest is one process with one summary, cargo is one process per target with one summary each, which is the distinction D-107 exists to record.

**What makes the classification possible is a fact about the crash, and it was measured rather than assumed.** cargo announces targets on stderr and the targets print on stdout; the two streams cannot be reinterleaved afterwards. They do not need to be. A target killed mid-run has *already printed its `running N tests` line* — libtest emits it before the first test, and the surviving output is what `.cargo/config.toml` describes. So a crash removes a target's **summary** without removing its **section**, sections stay positionally in step with the announced targets even across a crash, and the crashed target can still be named. That is also precisely the reporting hazard: the missing summary makes the run return a *smaller pass count with zero failures*, observed at 0.7.0 as "308 passed, 0 failed" with eight tests silently absent.

**Verified by injection in both directions**, as [D-107](s13-decision-register.md#d-107) was: an injected `std::process::abort()` reports `CRASH`, is retried, and names `bench_control_tests`; an injected `panic!` reports `FAILED` on attempt 1, names `injected_panic_must_be_reported_as_failed`, and does not retry.

**And injection was not sufficient, which is the part worth recording.** Both injections were run locally, both were correct, and the first CI run then reported `BUILD` on a suite in which **all 27 targets passed**. `ci.yml` sets `CARGO_TERM_COLOR: always`; cargo therefore wraps every status line in SGR escapes (`[1m[92m     Running[0m tests/foo.rs`), the `^\s*Running` pattern matched nothing, and the target list came back empty. Locally the variable is unset and cargo turns colour off for a non-tty, so the defect could not appear where it was tested. **Injection proves the classifier reads a real cargo run; it cannot prove it reads a run shaped the way the workflow shapes one.**

Two fixes, and the second matters more than the first. Escapes are now stripped from both streams before any pattern sees them — stripped once rather than tolerated in each regex, because "every future pattern must also allow for colour" is a rule that holds until the next pattern. And the branch that misfired was wrong independently of colour: it read `if not targets` and called that a build failure, when the stdout sections were sitting right there saying otherwise. **An empty target list is not evidence that nothing ran; it is evidence that nothing was parsed, and those differ exactly when this file has a bug.** `BUILD` now requires no announcements *and* no test output, target naming degrades to `target#N` instead of failing, and a run with no output and exit 0 is `INCOMPLETE` rather than anything green — a build failure reporting exit 0 was self-contradictory and the log said so.

`--self-test` classifies nine fixtures including the coloured shape, asserts the coloured and uncoloured runs produce the *same detail line* rather than merely both landing somewhere, and runs as its own CI step before the suite. It compiles nothing. A gate that can only be exercised by the thing it gates is a gate that gets exercised once. Six further classifications — including a summary deleted from the middle of a run, to confirm alignment names the right target and not its neighbour — were checked against a captured real run rather than a hand-written fixture.

**The budget then moves, and only then.** The quarantined step goes to six attempts (≈5% red instead of ≈22%). Raising it was rejected while the loop existed, and the rejection was right at the time: more attempts also meant more chances to launder a real failure into a pass. The classifier removes that consequence — `FAILED` returns on attempt 1 and is never retried — so six attempts now buys tolerance for the one outcome known to be noise and nothing else. The main suite stays at three, and `tests_py/run_suite.py` stays at three; the number is a property of how often a given step faults, not a house style.

**Observed in use, 2026-08-02, and one claim in it was measured false.** The quarantined step exhausted all six attempts once, and the exhaustion message said *"that is more than R15 has needed here — treat it as real."* It was not: `integrity_property_tests`, the binary that died, then faulted **1 in 6 runs in isolation** with exit `0xC0000005`. At p≈0.6 a run of six has about a 5% chance, so a budget of six produces that message roughly one run in twenty with nothing wrong, and telling the reader it must be real would train them to disbelieve it. The message now says what `.cargo/config.toml` has always advised instead — run the named binary alone; clean alone means R15, red alone means real. The budget itself is unchanged and is working: a later run of the same step passed on **attempt 4 of 6**, which three attempts would have reported as a failure.

**Rejected.** *Five retries alone* — lowers a rate and preserves the property that genuine red is indistinguishable from noise, which is the actual defect. *`continue-on-error`* — deletes the signal instead of reading it. *Dropping the property binaries* — they are the only model-based checks of the doctrine invariants ([D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030)). *Summing pass counts across targets* — the exact thing the reporting hazard defeats. *Folding a build failure into `INCOMPLETE`* — an empty target list satisfies every "all green" check vacuously, so `BUILD` is classified first and named separately.

<a id="d-111"></a>D-111 — `libsql` **stays at 0.9.30**: R15 reproduces on `0.10.0-pre.4`, on both sides of the Python boundary (0.8.0). [D-092](s13-decision-register.md#d-092), [D-095](s13-decision-register.md#d-095), [D-110](s13-decision-register.md#d-110), [R15](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#r15).

R15 is expensive: it costs `RUST_TEST_THREADS = "1"`, the `property-tests` quarantine, the crash-retry half of two suite gates, a Python suite forbidden `pytest-xdist`, the longest row in the risk register, and — until [D-110](s13-decision-register.md#d-110) — a red `main` on documentation-only commits. All of it becomes deletable if the fault is gone upstream. `0.9.30` has not moved since the fault was reported against it on 2026-07-31; `0.10.0-pre.4` (2026-06-02) had never been compiled here. So it was probed rather than guessed at.

**It builds and it passes.** `0.10.0-pre.4` resolves and compiles unchanged, and the full suite is **305 passed across 27 targets on the first attempt**. Nothing in this crate depends on anything the 0.10 line moved, which is worth knowing on its own: whenever 0.10 goes stable, the upgrade is a version bump and not a port.

**And the fault is still there.** Measured in one session on one machine, so the arms are comparable to each other:

| arm | 0.9.30, recorded 0.6.0/0.7.0 | 0.9.30, this session | `0.10.0-pre.4`, this session |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rust `control` — 48 concurrent opens, 15 s × 10 | 2/10 | **0/10** | **1/10** |
| Rust `claim` — one long-lived `Database`, 15 s × 10 | 0/10 | — | **0/10** |
| Python probe — 48 opens from 48 threads × 12 | 2/12 | — | **1/12** |
| full suite | 305 passed | 305 passed | 305 passed |

**The row that matters most is the one that looks like good news.** `0.9.30` faulted **0/10** this session against the 2/10 recorded for it at 0.6.0. Against p≈0.2 a clean run of ten happens about 11% of the time, so this is noise behaving exactly as noise does — but it is decisive for method. Had the probe run only the new engine and seen a clean arm, the honest-looking conclusion would have been *fixed*. Had it compared 1/10 against the *historical* 2/10 baseline, the conclusion would have been *improved*. Run side by side, the old engine scored better than the new one, and the only defensible reading is that **ten runs cannot distinguish these rates at all**. This is the same argument `examples/r15_soak.rs` was built around — a clean arm means nothing without a control — applied one level up, to the comparison between engines rather than between arms.

So the question this probe can answer is binary, and it answers it: **the fault is observed directly on `0.10.0-pre.4`, on both sides of the boundary.** Any non-zero rate keeps every mitigation, so no further runs would change the decision, and bounding the *rate* to the precision that would distinguish 1/10 from 2/10 needs a sample far larger than the decision is worth.

**Two things found on the way.** `bindings/python/Cargo.toml` pins `libsql` **independently** of the root — deliberately, and its comment says so — so a dependency move is a two-manifest edit. It is self-detecting rather than silent: the first attempt here failed with `Engine(#[from] libsql::Error)` mismatching across two resolved versions, which is a compile error and the right failure. And the whole probe ran in a detached `git worktree`, so the working tree never held a dependency change that was never going to be committed.

**Rejected.** *Moving to `0.10.0-pre.4` anyway* — it is a pre-release, the fault it would be adopted to fix is still present, and the mitigations would all have to stay. *Reading the clean `0.9.30` arm as a rate change* — the sample cannot support it, in either direction. *Skipping the Python arm because the Rust arm settled the decision* — the transparency of the boundary is a measured property ([D-095](s13-decision-register.md#d-095)), not one to re-derive from a mechanism argument on a new engine. *Deleting any mitigation* — nothing measured here licenses it.

<a id="d-112"></a>D-112 — macOS joins the Rust matrix, the Node 20 actions are bumped to their **first** `node24` major rather than to the newest, and the absence of a supply-chain gate is recorded as a choice (0.8.0). [D-108](s13-decision-register.md#d-108), [D-110](s13-decision-register.md#d-110), [R15](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#r15), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy).

**macOS.** `README.md` promises "Windows desktop, Linux, or macOS" and `ci.yml`'s matrix was `[ubuntu-latest, windows-latest]`. `python.yml` added `macos-latest` at P7, so the only macOS evidence this project held arrived **through pyo3** — a strange shape for a crate that is itself the product, and a README claim resting on a binding's CI. The matrix gains `macos-latest`.

It buys a measurement worth having independently: **the R15 rate on Apple silicon is unknown.** Every figure in `.cargo/config.toml` and in the [R15](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#r15) row comes from one Windows machine, and the fault has never been observed on a non-Windows runner — which is not the same as knowing it is absent, and nobody has looked. [D-110](s13-decision-register.md#d-110)'s classifier is what makes the answer readable rather than noise: a macOS fault will report `CRASH` and name its target instead of arriving as a smaller green.

**The action bumps, and why not the newest.** `actions/checkout@v4`, `setup-python@v5`, `upload-artifact@v4` and `download-artifact@v4` all declare `runs.using: node20` — **read out of each action's own `action.yml` rather than inferred from the deprecation banner**, which matters, because the same read showed three others needed nothing: `Swatinem/rust-cache@v2` and `PyO3/maturin-action@v1` are already `node24` on their current major, and `pypa/gh-action-pypi-publish@release/v1` is a composite action with no Node runtime at all.

Node 24 arrived at a **different major for each** of the four, which is the fact that decides this entry: `checkout` v5, `setup-python` v6, `upload-artifact` v6, `download-artifact` v7. Each is bumped to exactly that one. The newest are v7 / v7 / v7 / v8, and taking them would additionally buy an ESM migration, `download-artifact`'s new hash-mismatch enforcement, `upload-artifact`'s direct-upload semantics and `checkout`'s fork-PR blocking for `pull_request_target` / `workflow_run` — none of which this repository uses, and **none of which can be tested anywhere except on CI itself**. The stated problem is a deprecated runtime; these four solve it exactly.

The one skipped major with a real breaking change was checked rather than waved past: `download-artifact` v5 changed the output path for single-artifact downloads **by ID**, and `wheels.yml` names no artifact — it takes `path: dist` with `merge-multiple: true` across all of them — so it does not apply.

**No supply-chain gate, and that is a decision.** `cargo-deny` and `cargo-audit` are not adopted. Ten direct dependencies, all mainstream; nothing has been observed that such a gate would have caught, and adding one is a *policy* about acceptable licences and advisory response times rather than a fix for a defect. Recorded here so its absence reads as a choice rather than an oversight — the same reason [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089) records indices with no reader.

**Rejected.** *Bumping every action to its newest major* — takes untestable behavioural change to solve a runtime problem. *`macos-latest` behind `continue-on-error`* — that is [D-110](s13-decision-register.md#d-110)'s rejected `continue-on-error` again: a platform whose failures cannot fail the build is not in the matrix, it is in the log. *Dropping the macOS claim from `README.md` instead* — the crate does build and run there; the gap was in evidence, not in capability. *Pinning actions by commit SHA* — defensible, and a supply-chain policy decision that belongs with the `cargo-deny` question rather than smuggled in beside a runtime bump.

<a id="d-113"></a>D-113 — a decision that names a release is **executable**, and the register is read as data by the suite (0.8.0). [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030), [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087), [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy).

[D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087) and [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089) both read *"Scheduled for 0.7.0"*. 0.7.0 shipped as the Python bindings release with neither in it, and **nothing anywhere went red.** `doc_link_tests` was satisfied the whole time — it checks that anchors resolve, and both anchors resolved perfectly. The defect is not that someone forgot; it is that **a scheduled decision is prose, and prose is not executed.** This plan exists because of those two sentences.

`no_decision_still_awaits_a_release_that_has_shipped` in `tests/doc_sync_tests.rs` parses the register for scheduling phrases that name an actual version, compares each against `CARGO_PKG_VERSION`, and fails when a named version is at or below the current one unless the entry closes that release out by name: `DELIVERED in 0.8.0`, or `RESCHEDULED from 0.7.0`.

**Two narrowings, both taken from [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088)'s lesson** that a tripwire over prose which fires on prose gets disabled — and a disabled tripwire is worse than none, because its name goes on implying coverage. First, **a version must look like a version**: the phrase alone is not enough, because this register contains "deferred until something measures it" ([D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047)) and "deferred to the release that implements erasure" ([D-084](s13-decision-register.md#d-084)), which are honest deferrals with no date to miss — firing on them would instruct a maintainer to invent a release number in order to go green. Second, **the marker is all-caps**, a token ordinary prose cannot produce. Matching "delivered" case-insensitively would be satisfied by almost any entry discussing delivery, so the check would pass by coincidence rather than by intent — exactly the defect [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030) found in `audit_current()`, where a query reducing to a constant zero certified every corruption as clean.

**Verified by measurement, in the order that makes it mean something: red first.** On the unmodified register it failed naming precisely `D-087` and `D-089` and nothing else, with the two guard tests green beside it. The pattern test pins both directions — four scheduling forms that must fire, four sentences that must not — and a third test pins the marker arm, so *shipped* and *overdue* are both asserted rather than only the state that happens to hold today.

**The two entries are resolved by saying what happened, not by deletion.** Neither shipped, so neither carries `DELIVERED`; both now record that they missed 0.7.0 and name **0.8.0** — B2 for the interning, B4 for the index drop. That is a true statement, and it greens the suite honestly rather than by suppressing the finding. **It also arms the tripwire for the next boundary**: when B7 bumps `CARGO_PKG_VERSION` to 0.8.0, both entries come due again and the build stops unless the work landed and the marker is written. The failure mode this catches cannot be committed twice.

Rescheduling D-089 turned up a second error in the same sentence's neighbourhood: this entry and `README.md` had both come to describe the two unread indices as *added in v8*. They are in the **v7 baseline**, in `ddl::CREATE_INDICES`, created on every fresh database; v8 is what will remove them. Corrected in both places.

**Three things the test found that the design had not, and all three are corrections to it rather than to the register.**

**A per-entry marker disarms the tripwire permanently.** The first version accepted a bare `DELIVERED` anywhere in the entry. But an entry that missed 0.7.0 and now names 0.8.0 carries *two* claims, and one unqualified marker would settle both — so the mechanism built to catch a missed release would itself have waved through the next one. The marker is therefore keyed to the version it closes. Closing 0.7.0 says nothing about 0.8.0, and each entry comes due again at each boundary by itself.

**The original sentence must not be reworded, which is [Doctrine III](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-iii) applied to this file.** The obvious way to green the test was to edit *"Scheduled for 0.7.0"* out of D-087 and D-089. That is rewriting an assertion in place. This register is a ledger of belief and the doctrine that governs `links` governs it too: a belief is superseded, never overwritten. Both sentences stand exactly as written, and a later sentence in the same entry records that the release passed without them. Making the test green by destroying the evidence that it was ever wrong would have been the worst available outcome, and it was the easiest one.

**The test fired on this entry.** D-113 explains the failure by quoting it, and was duly reported as a decision overdue for a release it has no stake in. A register that records its own history will go on quoting the schedules it records, so this is a class rather than an instance: a phrase immediately preceded by a quotation mark is a citation, not a commitment. The rule is deliberately dumb — no attempt to decide *which* quotations are citations, because that is the cleverness [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088) warns produces a test nobody trusts. The gap it leaves is stated in the source: a genuine schedule written as the first words inside quotation marks would be missed. No entry has ever been written that way, and both real claims here are preceded by a space.

**Scope, stated so the gap is not read as coverage.** Only `s13-decision-register.md` is scanned. Scheduling language in a README row, a plan document or a source comment is not. The register is where decisions are authoritative; widening the scan is a larger change than this tripwire, not a free one.

**Rejected.** *A successor entry linking back, as the delivery signal* — the plan's alternative, and it needs a cross-entry graph where a marker in the entry needs nothing; the entry that made the promise is the right place to close it. *Case-insensitive "delivered"* — passes by coincidence, see above. *A marker not tied to a version* — settles every future claim the entry makes, which is the failure being guarded. *Leaving the test red until B1–B4 land*, which the plan proposed — a red `main` is what [D-110](s13-decision-register.md#d-110) had just finished fixing, and "red on purpose" is indistinguishable from red by accident to everyone who did not write it. *Deleting the two sentences* — the failure this test exists to catch is a deferral that leaves no trace.

<a id="d-114"></a>D-114 — `Subgraph`, `NodeData` and `EdgeRef` have private fields and a public accessor surface; the break is taken once, with the representation unchanged (0.8.0). [D-007](s13-decision-register.md#d-007), [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047), [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087), [D-101](s13-decision-register.md#d-101), [D-113](s13-decision-register.md#d-113), [§5.4](s5-modules.md#54-graphsubgraphrs-and-graphalgorithmsrs--native-in-memory-analytics), [Appendix A](appendices.md#appendix-a--public-api-normative).

Through 0.7.0 all three types had public fields, and the three `BTreeMap`s were the most widely read structure the crate exposes. Every detail of the representation was therefore public API — the `BTreeMap`, the `String` keys, the fact that adjacency is two maps at all — none of it a promise anyone meant to make. [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087)'s interning cannot begin while `EdgeRef::node` is a public `String`, so this is the change that has to come first.

**Measured, because [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087) overstates the cost and the plan understated it.** The register said the three types are *"read directly by every algorithm"*. They are not: `algorithms.rs` reaches adjacency **only** through `out_edges()` / `in_edges()`, which return borrowed slices — 0 sites on either map. But the plan then concluded the file was "ten mechanical substitutions", counting only `.nodes`. It also reads `EdgeRef` fields off those slices, at 15 further sites, and those move too once `EdgeRef` is closed. The real figure is **26 sites in one file**, all mechanical, none of them a design question. Across the tree: 57 `.nodes`, 11 `.out_adj`, 9 `.in_adj`, 13 files, 5 struct literals.

**`NodeData` and `EdgeRef` are closed now rather than later, and that is the whole point of doing this separately.** Leaving their fields public would have made B1 cheap and then broken the API twice more — B2 changes `EdgeRef::node` to `u32`, B3 changes `NodeData::content` to `Option`. A break taken once is the premise; a break taken once *per field* is the thing being avoided.

**The Python surface does not move, and the binding still does.** [D-101](s13-decision-register.md#d-101) made `Subgraph` opaque at P4.2 for an unrelated reason — peak memory on conversion — so `PyNodeData`, `PyEdgeRef` and `PySubgraph` already present methods rather than fields, and **not one `#[pymethods]` signature changes here**. The opacity decision pre-paid for this break. What did change is the ~20 lines behind those methods, which read the Rust fields directly; they now call accessors. That distinction is worth stating because the plan recorded only the first half, and "the Python surface does not move" was read as "the binding needs no work".

**`add_edge` becomes public rather than being replaced by a way to reach the maps.** Three test fixtures, one property-test generator and one diagnostic built adjacency by hand — each pushing into both maps and each doing its own `back.node = source` — which is five copies of an invariant the type already had one function for. They now call it. That is a genuine improvement extracted from a mechanical change, not a consolation for one.

**Zero behavioural diff, measured rather than asserted.** `examples/fixture_matrix_diag.rs` was run against the pre-B1 commit in a detached worktree and against this one, and every structural column is identical on all four fixtures — node counts, edge counts, shortest-path counts, and `estimated_bytes` at 339,638 / 2,168,375 / 324,653 / 30,744,680. Suites: 308 (`metrics`), 319 (`property-tests`), 344 Python. The property suites are what cover "same partitions, same distances": they compare against brute-force oracles, so a changed answer fails them by construction.

**Two documents reproduced the field list verbatim**, so two tests now hold the surface: no document may advertise a field this decision made private, and every public method on `Subgraph` must appear in the block `quickref.md` quotes. Both verified by injection. **The first version of the field check was too wide and went red on correct documentation** — it searched the whole file for `pub title:`, `pub weight:` and so on, which `ConceptUpsert` and `EdgeAssertion` legitimately declare, sharing five names. Scoped to the three declarations. That is [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088)'s lesson arriving for the third time in this release, inside a test whose own comment warns about it.

**Rejected.** *`pub(crate)` fields* — keeps `algorithms.rs` untouched and leaves the *external* surface just as broken, which is the surface B2 needs closed. *Leaving `NodeData` and `EdgeRef` public* — see above; it converts one break into three. *An `impl Index` or a `nodes()` returning `&BTreeMap`* — re-publishes the representation through a different door and makes interning impossible again. *Deriving `Deref` to the map* — same, with less honesty about it.

<a id="d-115"></a>D-115 — `EdgeRef` is interned to 24 bytes against a per-`Subgraph` string pool; the win is **5.8×–6.8×**, not the 7.1×–9.5× the plan projected (0.8.0). **DELIVERED in 0.8.0** for [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087). [D-007](s13-decision-register.md#d-007), [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047), [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063), [D-073](s13-decision-register.md#d-073), [D-114](s13-decision-register.md#d-114), [§5.4](s5-modules.md#54-graphsubgraphrs-and-graphalgorithmsrs--native-in-memory-analytics), [§9](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#9-performance-budgets).

`EdgeRef` was `{String, String, f64, String, String}` — 104 bytes of struct and around 250 of payload. It is now `{u32, u32, f64, u32, u32}`, **`size_of` 24, measured**, with every string an index into a pool held by the `Subgraph`. A **reachability** improvement in [D-073](s13-decision-register.md#d-073)'s category — graphs that did not fit the byte budget start fitting — not a speed one. [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063)'s CPU finding is not disputed and nothing here rests on it.

**Only the edges are interned, and that is the design rather than a shortcut.** `nodes` stays a `BTreeMap<String, NodeData>`. Node payload was never the target: the cost that scales is per *edge entry*, and there are two per edge. Keeping the node map also keeps iteration order structural, which is what makes the next paragraph true.

**D-063's determinism warning does not land, and the gate that would catch it if it did was written first.** D-063 said the real cost of interning is that *"determinism stops being structural and becomes procedural"*. It does not, because pool indices are not observable: node order still comes from a `BTreeMap` keyed by id, and adjacency order is still the order edges were added. Pool indices are handed out first-seen and nothing reads them except the resolver. `node_order_does_not_depend_on_construction_order` was landed **before** the representation moved, as D-063 asked ("a test … not a comment saying to sort"), and it is unchanged by this entry.

**That test's first version was vacuous and its own guard caught it.** It built the same graph in two databases with edges *inserted* in reverse order, assuming row order follows insertion order. It does not: the walk goes through `idx_lc_traversal_cover` ([D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042)), so rows arrive in index order however they were written, both databases scanned identically, and the test would have passed against any implementation — including the one it exists to catch. It now varies the **construction order of a hand-built graph** through `insert_node` / `add_edge`, which is where first-seen assignment would actually bite, and cross-checks against the loader.

**The plan's arithmetic was optimistic and the exit gate is why we know.** It required the table be reproduced *"on the real type, not on `size_of` arithmetic"*, and the two disagree:

| id length | B/edge at 0.7.0 | plan projected | **measured, all-in** | plan ratio | **measured** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 | 342 | 48 | **59** | 7.1× | **5.8×** |
| 26 (ULID) | 378 | 48 | **62** | 7.9× | **6.1×** |
| 64 | 454 | 48 | **67** | 9.5× | **6.8×** |

48 is `2 × size_of::<EdgeRef>()` and counts the pool as free. It is not: **[D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063)'s objection that an id table "stores every id a second time, partly cancelling the memory win" is right, and the amount is about 20%.** The win survives comfortably, and it is 5.8×–6.8×, not 7.1×–9.5×. On the four real fixtures `estimated_bytes` falls 339,638 → 190,134 (1.8×), 2,168,375 → 432,791 (5.0×), 324,653 → 174,023 (1.9×) and 30,744,680 → **4,386,282 (7.0×)** — the spread being density, exactly as the model predicts.

**The same diagnostic says where interning stops helping.** At 20 edges per node with 8-byte ids, edges are 80% of the budget with empty `content` and **5%** at 20 KB of document text. Interning wins on topology-heavy graphs; on content-heavy ones the answer is [B3](../Macrame%20Implementation%20Plan%20v0.8.0-v0.9.0.md), not this.

**A quadratic loader was introduced and caught by an existing test.** The first version charged the marginal pool cost by calling `estimated_bytes()` before and after every edge — O(pool) per row, so loading became O(E²). That is precisely the defect [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047) diagnosed and fixed, re-introduced by the change meant to make loading cheaper. `loading_scales_linearly_in_the_number_of_edges` failed, which is what it is for. `intern` now returns the marginal cost, the pool keeps a running total, and `add_edge` returns what it added, so the budget check is O(1).

**Reading an edge now needs the graph**: `e.node(&g)`, `e.edge_type(&g)`, `e.valid_from(&g)`, `e.valid_to(&g)`; only `weight()` stands alone, an `f64` being already 8 bytes. That is the visible cost, and it is why [D-114](s13-decision-register.md#d-114) had to privatise these fields first — a public `node: String` cannot become a `u32`. **The Python surface is untouched and the Python suite needed no edits**, which was the stated test of whether B1 privatised enough. `PyEdgeRef` now owns resolved strings rather than wrapping an `EdgeRef`, because a `#[pyclass]` outlives the graph and cannot borrow from it.

**Rejected.** *Interning the node map as well* — makes iteration order procedural for no gain, since node payload is not what scales. *A pool per field* — four maps to save nothing; they dedupe against each other for free in one. *Interning the weight* — an `f64` is 8 bytes and a pool of them costs more than it saves. *Recomputing pool bytes per row* — measured quadratic, see above. *Reporting `2 × size_of` as the win* — it is what the plan did, and it overstates by ~20%.

**Rejected, on making the cross-graph invariant enforceable.** The handle is tied to the `Subgraph` it came from, and nothing in the type system says so; the invariant is documented on `EdgeRef` in the standard Rust phrasing for an unenforced logic error, alongside the guarantee that licenses that phrasing — bounds-checked indexing, no `unsafe`, no undefined behaviour.

*A `graph_id: u32` tag on the edge.* The obvious middle path to prevent cross-graph resolution. It takes the struct to 28 bytes, almost certainly 32 with alignment — a 33% memory regression to catch an unobserved bug, which directly contradicts this item's purpose of reducing memory to increase reachability.

*A `#[cfg(debug_assertions)]` tag.* Tempting as a zero-release-cost check, and it breaks something concrete: `estimated_bytes()` reads `size_of::<EdgeRef>()`, so the byte budget would differ between debug and release, and the tests pinning the loader's arithmetic against `estimated_bytes()` would assert a number production does not use. Hard-coding the constant to fix that decouples the accounting from the type — precisely the coupling [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047)'s fix established to stop the two drifting apart.

*Removing `PartialEq`.* Within one graph, index equality is exactly the comparison a caller wants, and it is both cheaper and stricter than comparing five strings. The hazard is named in the invariant instead, because it is the one that needs no accessor call and therefore escapes the "do not query it against another graph" framing entirely.

**Lifetime branding is the pre-registered escape hatch.** A branded index makes the invariant a compile error and costs a generic parameter on `Subgraph` and on every signature that mentions one. Not paid for today, against a bug that has not been observed; recorded here so that if it is ever hit, the fix is chosen rather than invented.

<a id="d-116"></a>D-116 — `NodeData::content` becomes `Option<String>` and is **not loaded by default**; `None` means *not loaded*, never *empty* (0.8.0). [D-007](s13-decision-register.md#d-007), [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087), [D-096](s13-decision-register.md#d-096), [D-114](s13-decision-register.md#d-114), [D-115](s13-decision-register.md#d-115), [§5.4](s5-modules.md#54-graphsubgraphrs-and-graphalgorithmsrs--native-in-memory-analytics), [Appendix A](appendices.md#appendix-a--public-api-normative).

`load_subgraph_with` always hydrated `content` — its rustdoc said so — and **no algorithm reads it**. `dijkstra`, `astar`, `scc`, `k_core`, `louvain` and `modularity` touch topology and weight only. So the byte budget, which exists to stop a dense neighbourhood exhausting memory, was being spent on document text nothing would look at. `TraversalBuilder::content(bool)` turns it on; the default is off, and `Database::load_subgraph` — which has no builder — never loads it.

**Complementary to [D-115](s13-decision-register.md#d-115), not an alternative.** Interning wins when edges dominate; this wins when nodes do. `examples/budget_density_diag.rs` measures the split at 20 edges per node and 8-byte ids: edges are **80%** of the budget with empty content, **30%** at 2 KB per concept and **5%** at 20 KB. Against those measured components the default load now carries 238,802 bytes where it carried 4,238,802 at 20 KB per concept — **17.8×** — and 638,802 → 238,802 at 2 KB. The two decisions together are why they are in one release.

**`Option`, not `""`, and this is the same refusal [D-096](s13-decision-register.md#d-096) made for the open interval.** A sentinel that is a *valid value of the type* cannot be told apart from the real thing. A concept with genuinely empty text and one whose text was not requested are different facts, and they differ exactly when a caller is deciding whether to go back to the database. An empty string would have made that decision silently and wrongly.

**Decided inside the break, which [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087) could not do.** Its rejection list says the fix is "a load option, not a silent change of what the type contains" — right for an additive release. `NodeData` is being reshaped anyway ([D-114](s13-decision-register.md#d-114)), so the option and the type change land together and a caller cannot be silently wrong about which they got.

**The claim is settled by test, not by argument.** `content_is_absent_by_default_and_no_algorithm_notices` loads the same fixture twice, once each way, and asserts all six algorithms return identical answers — *and* that `estimated_bytes()` differs by more than 2×. The second assertion is what stops the first passing vacuously on a fixture whose content happened to be empty, which is the failure mode [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088) keeps producing.

**In Rust this is a behaviour change without a compile break**, so it belongs in the release note by name: `load_subgraph` and `load_subgraph_with` no longer populate `content` unless asked. In Python it **is** a break — `NodeData.content` is `str | None` — and B7 carries the stub, `s14`, the README example and the `content=` keyword that will let a Python caller ask for it. **Until B7 there is no way to request content from Python at all**; that gap is named in `tests_py/test_read_path.py` rather than left to be discovered.

**Rejected.** *An empty string for absent* — see above; indistinguishable from the real thing. *Loading content and discarding it in Rust* — keeps the I/O, which is most of what this avoids; the column is replaced by `NULL` in the query so the engine never reads the text off disk. *A separate `load_subgraph_with_content` method* — two loaders differing in one flag, and the flag belongs with the other traversal options. *Making it a parameter of `load_subgraph`* — a fifth positional argument on the documented main entry point, to express something only the filtered form needs.

<a id="d-117"></a>D-117 — the migration ladder gains a rung kind: `suspends_foreign_keys`, with `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` inside the transaction (0.8.0). [D-032](s13-decision-register.md#d-032), [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036), [D-084](s13-decision-register.md#d-084), [§5.1](s5-modules.md#51-connectionrs--the-handle-the-pragmas-and-the-write-actor).

**This is scope the plan named before implementation because a probe refuted the specified shape.** [D-084](s13-decision-register.md#d-084) specifies the `concepts` rowid rung as a `links`-style rebuild. `links` has no inbound foreign keys; `concepts` has two (`links.source_id`, `links.target_id`), and `examples/concepts_rebuild_probe.rs` was written to find out whether the rung is writable at all. On libSQL 0.9.30, **four approaches fail**:

| # | approach | result |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `PRAGMA foreign_keys = OFF` inside the rung's transaction | **silently ignored** — `execute` returns `Ok` and the value reads back `1`. The pragma is a no-op inside a transaction, and [`apply_step`] wraps every rung in `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` |
| 2 | `DROP TABLE concepts` with keys on | `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed`, **with or without** the delete guard — the guard is not the obstacle |
| 3 | `PRAGMA defer_foreign_keys = ON`, designed for this | every statement succeeds, `foreign_key_check` reports **0 violations**, and **COMMIT fails**: SQLite counts deferred violation *events*, and re-adding an equivalent parent does not decrement the counter |
| 4 | rename-around, `legacy_alter_table` on and off | the drop of the orphan fails both ways |

The fifth works: toggle the pragma **outside** the transaction. So the ladder has to know which rungs need that, and a rung says so with this flag.

**Atomicity is not weakened.** The rung is still one transaction and one commit with the `user_version` stamp inside it — [D-032](s13-decision-register.md#d-032)'s property is untouched. The pragma is per-*connection*, and the migration connection is created in `open()` and discarded on failure, so a crash between the toggle and the reset cannot leave a long-lived connection with enforcement off. It is restored on **every** path including the error one, deliberately: the guarantee should not depend on the caller's disposal habits.

**Suspension is not permission, and that is the whole safety argument.** A flag that turns enforcement off is only acceptable if something else turns verification on. `apply_step` runs `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` **inside** the transaction before committing, and any row it reports fails the rung — so a rung that suspends enforcement and leaves a violation rolls back rather than committing a database nobody checked.

**Both arms are tested, and the failing one is pinned to the check rather than to failure in general.** `a_suspending_rung_can_rebuild_a_table_with_inbound_keys` performs the drop-and-rename that is impossible with enforcement on, then asserts enforcement is back. `a_suspending_rung_that_leaves_a_violation_is_refused` orphans a row and requires the rung to fail, `user_version` to stay put, and enforcement to be restored despite the failure — asserting on the specific wording, because a DDL statement failing for its own reasons would also produce an error mentioning foreign keys and the test would then pass while proving nothing.

**Landed with no rung using it, and the rung followed in the same release.** The mechanism went first because it is the part the probe changed, it is testable on its own, and a schema rung is a bad place to be debugging the ladder underneath it. `concepts-rowid-pk-and-unread-indices` ([D-119](s13-decision-register.md#d-119)) is the rung that sets the flag, and it remains the only one.

**Rejected.** *Doing it inside the rung body* — measured to be silently ignored, which is worse than failing. *`defer_foreign_keys`* — the documented tool for this, and it does not work here; recorded so nobody re-derives it. *Skipping `foreign_key_check`* — turns the flag into a supported way to write a corrupt database. *Restoring the pragma only on success* — makes a correctness guarantee depend on the caller discarding the connection.

<a id="d-118"></a>D-118 — schema v8 drops `idx_annotations_label` and `idx_lc_tgt_active`; the unread set becomes **empty** rather than known-bad, and the win is measured at **7.9%** off `assert_edge` (0.8.0). **DELIVERED in 0.8.0** for [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089). [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059), [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), [D-113](s13-decision-register.md#d-113), [D-117](s13-decision-register.md#d-117), [D-119](s13-decision-register.md#d-119), [§4.2](s4-schema.md#42-links-assertion-history-and-current-belief-materialization), [§4.5](s4-schema.md#45-analytics-annotations--the-second-derivative-table-054-d-041).

[D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089)'s registry found two indexes that no query in the crate seeks on — an index write per insert, forever, buying nothing, one of them on the hottest write path. It recorded them, scheduled removal for 0.7.0, and 0.7.0 had no schema work to put it in. [D-113](s13-decision-register.md#d-113) is what turned that stale sentence into a failing test. The `v7 → v8` rung drops both.

**The performance claim is measured, because [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088) says this project does not ship one that is not.** `examples/tgt_index_cost_probe.rs` runs the same `star_of_stars` load twice — once against the v8 index set, once with `idx_lc_tgt_active` restored, which is exactly the v7 shape since that index is the only difference on this table — alternating the arms across five repeats so a warming disk does not land on one of them:

| arm | 3,999 `assert_edge` calls | per edge |
|---|---|---|
| v7 (`idx_lc_tgt_active` present) | 1,010.3 ms | 252.6 µs |
| v8 (dropped) | 931.0 ms | 232.8 µs |

**−7.9%, or 19.8 µs per assertion.** Two orders of magnitude smaller than [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059)'s index win, and unlike that one it is free — the index had no reader to lose.

**The test changes shape, and that is the real deliverable.** `the_unread_indices_are_the_two_already_known` pinned the *set*: a tripwire against a third joining them, which accepts the two. `the_unread_index_set_is_empty` asserts the standard D-089 was actually arguing for — an index with no reader is a red test, full stop. The `NoReader` variant deliberately survives its last instance, under `#[allow(dead_code)]`: recording a new index as unread is how the test is made to fail, so the category has to stay available for that failure to be expressible. A vacuity guard asserts the registry is not itself empty, since an empty registry would satisfy an empty unread set.

**Rejected.** *Keeping them and revisiting later* — this is the release with a rung, and "later" is what produced the stale schedule in the first place. *Deleting the `NoReader` variant along with its last instance* — turns a red test into a compile error somewhere else, and then into a temptation to skip the registry entry entirely. *Asserting the win without measuring it* — the whole of D-088.

<a id="d-119"></a>D-119 — `concepts` gains `rowid_pk INTEGER PRIMARY KEY` and `concepts_fts` gains its third trigger, installed inert; taken pre-1.0 because [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036) closes the door on it (0.8.0). **DELIVERED in 0.8.0** for [D-084](s13-decision-register.md#d-084). [D-022](s13-decision-register.md#d-022), [D-032](s13-decision-register.md#d-032), [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036), [D-051](s13-decision-register.md#d-051), [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071), [D-117](s13-decision-register.md#d-117), [D-118](s13-decision-register.md#d-118), [D-120](s13-decision-register.md#d-120), [§4.1](s4-schema.md), [§4.6](s4-schema.md), [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search).

`concepts_fts` is external-content with `content_rowid`, and through v7 `concepts` was `id TEXT PRIMARY KEY`, so the rowid the index keys on was **implicit**. v8 makes it a column: `rowid_pk INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, id TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE`. `id` stays a valid foreign-key parent and `ON CONFLICT(id)` still works, but SQLite permits one primary key per table, so this **is** a primary-key change — the change [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036) forbids outright after 1.0 and classes as needing a major version with a purpose-built ETL. Pre-1.0, [D-032](s13-decision-register.md#d-032) makes it a baseline re-issue. Taken now or never.

**The justification is not the one D-071 recorded, and [D-120](s13-decision-register.md#d-120) is why.** The stated hazard was that `VACUUM` renumbers implicit rowids and 0.9.0's archival would make `concepts.rowid` sparse. Measurement found that `VACUUM` on this engine renumbers a sparse implicit rowid only for a table with **no index at all**, and v7's `concepts` carried the `id` autoindex — so v7 was never at risk. The rung is still right, for the honest reason: SQLite *documents* the renumbering as permitted, and an index's correctness should not rest on an engine optimisation that happens to decline to exercise the permission.

**The third FTS trigger ships inert, and both halves are pinned.** `trg_concepts_fts_delete` cannot fire today: `trg_concepts_guard_delete` is an unconditional `BEFORE DELETE` that aborts, so no statement reaches `AFTER DELETE`. It is installed because 0.9.0's archive session is what makes that guard conditional, and the moment it does the index would go silently stale without it. Installing the capability in a rung that is already rebuilding the table costs nothing. ~~It also means **0.9.0 needs no migration of its own**.~~ **Corrected in place, 2026-08-06 — see [D-126](s13-decision-register.md#d-126): it does not.** This trigger is C2's step 3; step 2, making `trg_concepts_guard_delete` marker-gated, needs a trigger-only `v8 → v9` rung that a baseline re-issue cannot deliver. The entry above is otherwise unchanged: this sentence was a claim about a *later* release, made at the moment the rung shipped, and it was the one part of D-119 that no gate could check. `the_fts_delete_trigger_is_installed_and_inert` asserts existence *and* unreachability, because the inertness is a property of a *different* trigger and that dependency is exactly the thing worth a test.

**The rung's order has two traps in it.** Triggers and `concepts_fts` come down *before* the table is touched: recreating triggers while the old FTS table was still present would bind them to an index about to be dropped, and dropping `concepts_fts` while triggers still named it is the schema-reparse failure the `links` rung hit from the other direction. And `rowid` is copied into `rowid_pk` **by value** rather than left to auto-assign — on today's dense numbering the two agree, which is what makes it look redundant; it is what makes the rung correct on a file whose rowids are not dense, and it preserves row identity rather than merely row order.

**One honest cost, recorded rather than discovered.** `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` reports violations across the whole database, so a v7 file that *already* held an orphaned `links` row — reachable only if it was written with enforcement off — fails to migrate. That is the right outcome and it is not silent: `a_v7_database_with_a_pre_existing_orphan_is_refused` pins the message naming both the check and the table, and pins that `user_version` stays at 7.

**The v7 fixture is hand-written, against this file's own advice, and the reason is specific.** The v6 fixture could be built by re-issuing today's baseline and *removing* one CHECK, so the copy was nearly the real thing. v8 changes a primary key and re-keys the FTS index onto a column that did not exist, so no subtractive route exists: a fixture assembled from `ddl::` constants would be a v8 database wearing a v7 stamp, and the rung would be tested against a table that had already had its change. `links` rows are present in every case, because a `concepts` with nothing pointing at it exercises none of what [D-117](s13-decision-register.md#d-117) exists for — and `the_v8_rung_needs_the_suspension_and_links_rows_prove_it` pins exactly that, so the flag cannot quietly become unnecessary without saying so.

**Rejected.** *Deferring to the release that implements erasure*, which is what D-084 decided — D-036 makes the change unavailable by then, and the two are the same decision seen from opposite sides of a deadline. *Leaving `id` as the primary key and adding `rowid_pk` as a plain column* — then it is not the rowid, and `content_rowid` still points at an implicit one. *Shipping the delete trigger in 0.9.0 alongside the archive session* — that is a second rung, on a release this plan promises will have none. *Discovering the rowids by `SELECT` and reassigning them* — a rung is a statement about the past, and copying the value is what makes it one.

<a id="d-120"></a>D-120 — `VACUUM` renumbers a sparse implicit rowid **only for a table with no index**, so [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071)'s hazard was never live; found by a test's control arm (0.8.0). [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071), [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), [D-119](s13-decision-register.md#d-119), [§4.6](s4-schema.md), [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search).

`vacuum_preserves_a_sparse_rowid_pk` was written as [D-119](s13-decision-register.md#d-119)'s exit gate, and given a control arm on the principle that a test asserting *nothing moved* proves an outcome and not a mechanism — if `VACUUM` never renumbered anything, the assertion would hold against the v7 schema too. The control was the v7 shape, `id TEXT PRIMARY KEY` with an implicit rowid, seeded with the same gaps. **It did not move**, and the gate was therefore vacuous as written.

`examples/vacuum_rowid_probe.rs` measured six shapes on libSQL 0.9.30, made sparse both by never-occupied gaps and by deletion:

| shape | result |
|---|---|
| `id TEXT PRIMARY KEY` (one column) | **preserved** |
| `id TEXT PRIMARY KEY` (several columns) | **preserved** |
| `id TEXT UNIQUE`, no primary key | **preserved** |
| no constraints, one secondary index | **preserved** |
| **no primary key and no index at all** | **RENUMBERED** — `[1, 4, 5, 9, 100, 1000000]` → `[1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]` |
| `rowid_pk INTEGER PRIMARY KEY` (the v8 shape) | **preserved** |

The discriminator is not the primary key. It is **whether the table carries any index at all**.

**What this costs the story, stated plainly.** [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071) concluded the hazard did not arise *because the delete guard keeps rowids dense*, and warned that archival would make it real. That reasoning was sound and the conclusion was right, but for an additional reason nobody had measured: `concepts` has always carried the `id` autoindex, so archival would **not** have made it real either. Two accidents stacked, neither chosen, and `vacuum_does_not_disturb_the_fts_index` could not have told you if either stopped holding.

**It does not make [D-119](s13-decision-register.md#d-119) wrong; it makes its justification honest.** The rung is no longer "fixes a live bug" — it is "removes a dependence on an undocumented optimisation". SQLite's documentation says `VACUUM` *may* change the rowids of a table with no explicit `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY`; resting a search index's correctness on an implementation detail that currently declines to exercise that permission is precisely the class of accident this register exists to convert into a decision.

**The control arm is now the no-index shape**, which is the narrowest thing that still proves the engine reassigns rowids at all — and if it ever stops, the gate fails and says so rather than passing quietly.

**Rejected.** *Deleting the control once it complicated the story* — it is the only reason the story is known to be wrong. *Keeping the v7 shape as the control and marking the test `#[ignore]`* — a disabled test is [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088)'s failure mode arriving by a different door. *Reverting the v8 rung on the grounds that the hazard is not real* — the permission in the documentation is real, and 1.0 closes the door.

<a id="d-121"></a>D-121 — below the log floor is **not** a corruption; *before recorded history* and *the cold file is gone* are told apart from `seq_id`, with no marker and no rung (0.8.0). [D-024](s13-decision-register.md#d-024), [D-026](s13-decision-register.md#d-026), [D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043), [D-049](s13-decision-register.md#d-049), [D-052](s13-decision-register.md#d-052), [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069), [D-096](s13-decision-register.md#d-096), [R14](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#r14), [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots), [§14.9](s14-python-bindings.md).

**Reproduced against 0.7.0 as published, through the binding**, and the reproduction is the argument:

| ledger | `reconstruct("2020-01-01…")` |
|---|---|
| empty database | `MaterializedState` — 0 concepts, 0 edges |
| one concept, one edge, never archived | `ReplayCorruptError: archive database file "…\kb_archive.db" does not exist` |

**The defect is the transition.** Before the first write the question has a correct answer; after it the same question raises the class meaning *the ledger is damaged*, naming a file the caller never created — the binding derives `kb_archive.db` from the database path. This is [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069)'s rule broken in the same way `InvalidTimestamp` and `InvalidId` broke it: an error that names the wrong subject sends a caller to fix the wrong thing.

**It is worse than the reproduction shows, and the third row is the one that matters.** The floor is `MIN(recorded_at)` — *transaction* time, crate-stamped at write. On a database written today, **every** `ts` before today was below the floor, including instants a caller would reasonably think were well inside their data's own lifetime. A concept asserted `valid_from = 2026-01-01` and written on 2026-08-02 made `reconstruct("2026-06-01")` report a damaged ledger. Not a pre-genesis curiosity; an ordinary question.

**Mechanism.** `hot_log_is_complete` returned `false` when `ts` preceded `MIN(recorded_at)`, and the branch below assumed the delta must therefore be in cold storage. The hot file recorded nothing about whether it had ever been archived — the horizon lives in `cold.archive_horizon`, inside the file whose existence is the question.

**The objection that kept it, and why it was the way out rather than the reason to stay.** `s14` recorded the behaviour as a rough edge and declined to smooth it over in the binding, on the ground that *translating it into an empty state would mean claiming a real missing archive is also nothing to worry about*. That is exactly right, and it is a statement that two situations were being conflated — not a reason to keep conflating them.

They are now distinguished **from the hot file alone**. `transaction_log.seq_id` is `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT`, so ids are allocated `1, 2, 3, …` and never reused; a rolled-back transaction leaves no gap, which [D-049](s13-decision-register.md#d-049) established by measurement after this project had assumed the opposite; and `trg_txlog_guard_delete` confines deletion to an archive session. Therefore `MIN(seq_id) = 1 AND COUNT(*) = MAX(seq_id)` **iff** no row has ever been removed — `COUNT` distinct ids inside `[1, MAX]` with `COUNT = MAX` is all of `1..=MAX`, so the implication runs both ways and the test is exact rather than suggestive. It does not depend on the archive removing a contiguous block, which it does not: `archive()` removes *superseded* rows scattered through the sequence, which leaves interior gaps; removal from the front raises `MIN`; removal from the end cannot happen because the newest row per entity is never archivable.

**One bit, deliberately.** It answers *was anything ever removed*, and nothing about when or what. The rejected alternative — a hot-side marker written by `archive()` recording *archived at* and *horizon*, so the error could say *"this database was archived on 2026-06-01; pass the archive path"* — would carry more, and needs a rung. The plan deferred it to 0.9.0 as a second rung in a release already taking one. It is not merely deferred now: the branch that needed it does not need it, and ~~0.9.0 should adopt the marker only if it wants the richer *message*, not to make the decision correct.~~ **That door is now closed — corrected 2026-08-06, see [D-132](s13-decision-register.md#d-132).** 0.9.0 went to collect the richer message and found it free: the marker would carry the archive *timestamp*, while the hot log already yields how many rows went (`MAX(seq_id) - COUNT(*)`) and how far back what remains reaches (`MIN(seq_id)`) — the two facts a caller facing an unreachable cold file actually needs. The marker is refused outright rather than deferred again, which under [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036) is what "not in 0.9.0" means anyway.

**`hot_log_is_complete` becomes `hot_log_reach` returning a three-way enum.** The `bool` was the defect's shape: two answers for three cases, so the missing one had to be attached to whichever neighbour was closer, and *the delta is elsewhere* was closer than it looked. `Covers` / `PredatesRecordedHistory` / `NeedsArchive` makes the third case something a reader can see.

**`MaterializedState` gains `predates_recorded_history`.** An empty state has two meanings — *everything was retired by then* is a fact about the data, *the ledger had not started* is a fact about the question — and both arrive as zero concepts and zero edges. This is [D-096](s13-decision-register.md#d-096)'s rule about sentinels applied one level up: the empty state is a valid value, so it cannot double as the marker for its own special case. The field is `#[serde(default)]`, which costs nothing and is not what makes it safe — [D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043)'s container carries `SCHEMA_VERSION`, and [D-119](s13-decision-register.md#d-119)'s bump to v8 had already refused every snapshot written before this release. The two items landing in one release is what made an additive field free; a later one may not be so lucky.

**The gates, and the one that was pinning the defect.** `test_cold_db_reconstruct_missing_archive_error` built a never-archived database, handed it a path to a file that had never existed, and required an error — so it was asserting the bug, and it went red on the fix. Replaced by `a_missing_archive_is_an_error_when_rows_were_actually_archived`, whose fixture supersedes a concept, archives, checks rows really moved, and *then* deletes the cold file: R14's case, earned rather than assumed. `reconstructing_below_the_log_floor_is_not_a_corruption` asserts the reproduction table and the transaction-time boundary. `nothing_below_the_log_floor_is_ever_a_corruption` generates the history *and* the instant, because the claim is universally quantified over `t` and one instant is the wrong shape for it. Every one of them asserts the flag **false** on a fold that had rows, or a `reconstruct` that always returned nothing would satisfy them all. Verified by injection: restoring the old branch fails the unit gate and the property gate, and leaves the missing-archive gate green — which is the correct pattern, since that one guards the half that did not change.

**Rejected.** *The hot-side marker* — needs a rung, and the branch turned out not to need it (above). *Returning the empty state whenever the archive file is absent* — that is the objection `s14` raised, and it makes a deleted archive silently report an empty world. *Making `Database::reconstruct` pass `None` when the derived archive path does not exist* — moves the same conflation up a layer and makes the free function's contract depend on which caller reached it. *An `EmptyBecauseNoHistory` error variant* — the question has an answer, and an answer is not an error. *Leaving it, since a caller can catch `ReplayCorruptError`* — the class means the ledger is damaged, and a caller who learns to ignore it will ignore it when it is true.

<a id="d-122"></a>D-122 — Louvain stays **phase-one-only**, and the reason it gave before was measured false; the aggregation phase is declined because at these sizes it turns a correct answer into a merged one (0.8.0). [D-039](s13-decision-register.md#d-039), [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063), [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), [D-115](s13-decision-register.md#d-115), [§5.4](s5-modules.md#54-graphsubgraphrs-and-graphalgorithmsrs--native-in-memory-analytics), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy).

`louvain` implements local moving and not the aggregate-and-recurse phase. The rustdoc justified that by graph size — *"the aggregation phase would matter on graphs far larger than the byte budget admits"* — and [D-115](s13-decision-register.md#d-115)'s interning raised what the budget admits by 5.8×–6.8×, so the justification had to be re-argued against the new ceiling rather than re-asserted.

**The stated reason is false.** `examples/louvain_aggregation_probe.rs` builds `clustered` directly into a `Subgraph` — affordable because [D-114](s13-decision-register.md#d-114) made `insert_node` and `add_edge` public — and finds the ceiling at **4,096 cliques / 49,152 nodes / 544,767 edges, 35.6 MiB at 68 B/edge** under the 64 MiB budget the benches use. Two-phase diverges from phase-one at **6,144 nodes**, comfortably inside the budget, and the gap widens with size:

| cliques | nodes | Q(truth) | Q(1 phase) | Q(2 phase) | ΔQ | k(1p) | k(2p) | two-phase vs truth |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 | 96 | 0.86841 | 0.86841 | 0.86841 | +0.00000 | 8 | 8 | exact |
| 32 | 384 | 0.96146 | 0.96146 | 0.96146 | +0.00000 | 32 | 32 | exact |
| 128 | 1,536 | 0.98473 | 0.98473 | 0.98473 | +0.00000 | 128 | 128 | exact |
| 512 | 6,144 | 0.99054 | 0.99054 | **0.99235** | +0.00181 | 512 | 256 | merges 2 cliques |
| 2,048 | 24,576 | 0.99200 | 0.99200 | **0.99617** | +0.00417 | 2,048 | 512 | merges 4 cliques |
| 4,096 | 49,152 | 0.99224 | 0.99224 | **0.99715** | +0.00491 | 4,096 | 1,024 | merges 4 cliques |

**So the gate as B6 specified it would have said "ship it", and shipping it would have been wrong.** B6's criterion was *take aggregation only if Q differs materially*. Q does differ materially, and it grows with size. But the last three columns say what ΔQ cannot: **phase-one recovers the ground truth exactly at every size**, and two-phase earns its higher Q by **merging whole cliques** — two per community at 512, four at 4,096, never splitting one, and scoring above the ground truth itself.

That is the modularity resolution limit (Fortunato & Barthélemy): on a large graph the objective prefers a partition coarser than the true one, because a single bridge between two cliques is enough to make merging them look profitable once the graph is big enough for `(Σtot·k_i)/2m²` to shrink. It is a property of Q, not of any algorithm. **Optimising Q harder therefore moves away from the answer, and a Q comparison cannot be the criterion for a change whose whole effect is to optimise Q harder.** The scope limit survives, on a better argument than the one it had and for the opposite reason: not *aggregation would change nothing here*, but *aggregation would change something, and the change is wrong*.

**Two controls, because the arms are easy to confound.** The probe's local-moving step is a transcription of `louvain` onto a flat adjacency structure, so a Q difference could have been the reimplementation rather than the feature; **control 1** asserts at every size that the transcription's level-0 partition is the same *grouping* the crate's `louvain` returns, comparing induced partitions rather than labels. **Control 2** is the fixture: `clustered` has a known ground truth, which is what makes "merged" distinguishable from "found". Without the second, ΔQ > 0 reads as a discovery.

**Closed, not left open.** [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063) names an unmeasured open question as "the worst of the three states", so the finding ships as a test rather than as a probe nobody re-runs. `modularity_prefers_a_merged_partition_over_the_true_one_at_scale` needs no two-phase implementation in the crate: it builds 512 cliques, asserts `louvain` recovers the truth exactly, and asserts that the **merged partition outscores the truth**. That is the fact underneath the decision, and if it ever stops holding the decision is worth revisiting. Its failure message names the probe.

**What would reopen it.** A fixture with genuine hierarchy — communities of communities, where the coarse structure is real rather than an artifact of the objective. `clustered` is a chain of uniform cliques and cannot exhibit that, so this measurement is about `clustered` at scale and says so. If such a fixture is added, the question comes back, and the criterion should be agreement with ground truth rather than Q.

**Rejected.** *Shipping aggregation on the ΔQ number* — the number is real and the inference from it is not. *Adding a resolution parameter (γ) so a caller can tune the limit away* — a second knob whose correct value nobody can supply, on an API whose current answer is exact. *Keeping the old rustdoc sentence* — it is measured false, and a justification that is wrong for a conclusion that is right is worse than none, because it is what the next person will re-check against. *Leaving the question open pending a hierarchical fixture* — [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063) again; the fixture would be new scope, and the question as asked has an answer.

<a id="d-123"></a>D-123 — absent `content` crosses to Python as `None`, and `load_subgraph` gains the `content=` keyword that makes the default overridable (0.8.0). [D-096](s13-decision-register.md#d-096), [D-101](s13-decision-register.md#d-101), [D-102](s13-decision-register.md#d-102), [D-109](s13-decision-register.md#d-109), [D-116](s13-decision-register.md#d-116), [D-121](s13-decision-register.md#d-121), [§14.4](s14-python-bindings.md#144-timestamps-and-value-types), [§14.9](s14-python-bindings.md#149-status), [§14.10](s14-python-bindings.md#1410-the-read-path-and-the-subgraph-handle).

[D-116](s13-decision-register.md#d-116) took `concepts.content` out of the default load and left the Python surface with a gap it named rather than hid: `NodeData.content` returned `None` and **there was no way to ask for the text at all**. A default nobody can override is not a default, it is a removal. `load_subgraph(..., content=False)` closes it, keyword-only, alongside `edge_types` / `min_weight` / `now`.

**`str | None`, not `""`.** The empty string is a *valid value of the type*, so it cannot mark the absence of one: a concept whose text really is empty and one whose text was not fetched are different facts, and they differ exactly when a caller is deciding whether to go back to the database. That is [D-096](s13-decision-register.md#d-096)'s refusal of the open-interval sentinel, one layer along and for the same reason, which is why `content` is documented in [§14.4](s14-python-bindings.md#144-timestamps-and-value-types) beside it rather than in a section of its own. `test_content_is_none_not_empty_string` pins both arms on one graph: a concept written with `content=""` reads back `""` when asked for and `None` when not.

**`False` matches the crate rather than softening it.** Defaulting the binding to `True` would have avoided a visible break and made the binding a second source of truth about what a load costs — the thing [D-101](s13-decision-register.md#d-101)'s opaque handle exists to prevent. `traverse` is untouched: it hydrates by `attribute_mode`, which asks *which* text, not *whether* ([D-102](s13-decision-register.md#d-102)).

**What did not cross is the more interesting half.** [D-115](s13-decision-register.md#d-115) interned `EdgeRef` against a per-`Subgraph` string pool — a representation change to the largest structure the crate materialises — and **no `#[pymethods]` signature moved**. `graph.rs` swapped field reads for [D-114](s13-decision-register.md#d-114)'s accessors and `PyEdgeRef` resolves through the pool at construction; that is all. The reason is [D-101](s13-decision-register.md#d-101): the binding returns a handle rather than converting the graph to a dict, so there was no copy whose layout had to track the change. A decision taken in 0.7.0 for API-shape reasons paid for a 0.8.0 representation change nobody had planned for when it was taken.

**One plan assumption was wrong and is corrected here.** B7 specified that *"`to_dict()`'s node entries change shape when content is absent"* and that the method should emit an explicit `None` rather than omit the key. It does not and it need not: `to_dict()` returns `{id: NodeData}`, not nested plain dicts, so absent content is `NodeData.content is None` — a property access, not a key lookup — and the `KeyError` hazard the step was guarding against does not exist. The stub now says so, since the shape is not obvious from the signature `dict[str, Any]`.

**The stub is still hand-written, and 0.8.0 is the argument for it, not against.** [D-109](s13-decision-register.md#d-109) refused `pyo3-stub-gen` because a generator writes `Optional[Any]` precisely where the interesting distinction lives. `content: str | None` with *"`None` means not loaded, never empty"* is that distinction, and it is the sentence a caller needs. `test_stubs.py` compares the stub against the extension's `dir()` in both directions, and both halves were re-verified by injection here: an invented member is caught (`NodeData: in the stub only: ['invented_member']`) and a dropped one is caught (`MaterializedState: not in the stub: ['predates_recorded_history']`).

**The version bump is not cosmetic.** `0.8.0` across `Cargo.toml`, `bindings/python/Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock` and the README's `macrame-db = "0.8"`. A wheel built against schema v7 opens a v8 database and refuses it by design, so a wheel that reports the old version is a wheel whose compatibility claim is wrong. `test_packaging.py` reads `macrame.__version__` from the *installed* extension, which is only meaningful if the wheel was rebuilt — the step 0.7.0 missed once, so it is stated as a gate rather than assumed.

**The README example is run verbatim before the tag.** 0.7.0's raised `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed` when first checked, because it asserted an edge to a concept it never wrote. Extracted from the markdown and executed in a clean directory: `{'entanglement': 1.0, 'quantum': 0.0}`.

**Rejected.** *Regenerating the stub* — [D-109](s13-decision-register.md#d-109), and `content` is the example that makes the case. *Returning `""` for absent content* — a sentinel that is a valid value of the type. *Defaulting `content=True` in Python only* — a binding that disagrees with the crate about a default is a second source of truth. *Adding `content` to `traverse`* — a different question, already answered by `attribute_mode`.

<a id="d-124"></a>D-124 — the 0.8.0 "R15 regression" is retracted: it was a stale baseline read against an underpowered measurement, and the fault rate now lives in exactly one file (0.8.0). [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063), [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), [D-092](s13-decision-register.md#d-092), [D-110](s13-decision-register.md#d-110), [D-111](s13-decision-register.md#d-111), [D-119](s13-decision-register.md#d-119), [D-121](s13-decision-register.md#d-121), [R15](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#r15), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy).

During B5 this project recorded, in three places, that the quarantined `property-tests` gate had gone red on `main`, that `doctrine_property_tests` was faulting "far above its recorded rate", and that 0.8.0 should not be tagged until the cause was found. **None of that was true, and the way it became true-looking is the part worth keeping.**
