README said *"~50/50"*. `ci.yml` sized a six-attempt retry budget on p ≈ 0.6, from `doctrine_property_tests` alone at n = 15. `run_rust_suite.py` told the reader to expect a red step *"roughly 1 run in 20"*, which is 0.6 carried through six attempts. At 93% per attempt, six in a row is **65%**.

## Why that is not the finding

Because the step is not red 65% of the time in CI. `ci.yml` records 2 red in 9 runs on `main`, which implies p ≈ 0.78 on the runner. Both numbers are real and neither is wrong.

So the five published figures — 45%, 60%, 75%, 78%, 93% — were never competing estimates of one constant. **They are five machines under five loads.** `.cargo/config.toml` had most of the evidence for this already: the same executable scored 75% and 45% in consecutive n = 20 experiments, and it drew the right conclusion about the *noise band* without drawing the further one that the quantity is not a property of the crate at all. The standing instruction is now to quote the figure you measured, name the machine and the load beside it, and not carry it anywhere.

**The retry budget is deliberately unchanged.** At any rate in this range no budget makes the step a gate, and more attempts only buy more chances to launder a real failure into a pass — the reason it was capped when the classifier was written, and the reason still holds.

## The flaky test, which is the part with a lesson in it

Pass 1 of this study read **85 crashes and 26 failures in 120 runs**. All 26 were the same test — `bulk_import_is_atomic_per_chunk_not_overall`, asserting `committed < n`, where `n` is the count of good rows.

That assertion says *at least one good row shared the failing chunk*, which is true only when the violation does not land first in its chunk. [D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146) made where the boundaries fall a fact about the machine, so sometimes it does. **This is the test written to stop pinning boundary positions, pinning one.** The doc comment above it names the exact hazard and explains why a second test exists to cover it; the assertion three lines down then assumed it away. It has never fired before because 402 rows at a fixed 90 always left a 42-row tail.

Corrected to `committed < edge_count` — a prefix committed and not the whole batch — with whole-chunk rollback left where it can be stated without naming a size.

**And a flaky test biases a crash-rate measurement downward.** `classify()` reports FAILED before it looks for a crash, so a run where one binary crashed *and* another failed is counted as a failure and the crash is invisible. Pass 1's 71% was not a quieter session than pass 2's 93%; it was the same number with a bug standing in front of it. Any crash rate measured on a suite that contains a flaky test is a lower bound.

**The study's own instrument lied too, and it is recorded because it cost two rounds.** The harness attributed failures to `archive_window_tests` and `compat_contract_tests`, neither of which had failed: the classifier's FAILED detail names no binary, so the parser scraped the tail of the log and returned whichever binary printed last. Two flakes were hunted that did not exist. It now reads the `::error::<binary>: <test> FAILED` annotation, which is the classifier's own attribution and was there all along.

**Rejected.** *Publishing 93% as the R15 rate* — it is this machine under this load, and CI says so directly; a single figure is the error this entry is about. *Raising the six-attempt budget to fit 93%* — above; six attempts already exceed what the classifier can justify, and a budget that fits the worst machine measured is a budget that hides real failures on all the others. *Marking the quarantined step `continue-on-error`* — it would make the step unable to report anything, which is worse than a step that is red for a documented reason. *Measuring a cold arm before publishing* — runs spaced minutes apart would separate load from machine and is the obvious successor; it is named in `.cargo/config.toml` as unmeasured rather than folded into this entry as though it had been. *Treating the 26 failures as a property-test discovery* — they were one boundary-sensitive assertion in an ordinary test binary, and reading them as a finding about the engine is the mistake the study's own misattribution nearly caused.

---

<a id="d-148"></a>D-148 — R15 is a **cumulative `connect()` volume** fault, not a concurrency fault, and the diagnosis it replaces stood for six releases because one row of the reproducer stopped at 500 (0.12.1). [D-092](s13-decision-register.md#d-092), [D-095](s13-decision-register.md#d-095), [D-110](s13-decision-register.md#d-110), [D-111](s13-decision-register.md#d-111), [D-124](s13-decision-register.md#d-124), [D-147](s13-decision-register.md#d-147), [R15](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#r15), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy).

> **Every mitigation this project has shipped for R15 serialises something. Serialising does not help. It lowers the rate because it lowers the total, which is a different mechanism from the one recorded for it — and the one this entry replaces.**

**Fixture: `examples/r15_soak.rs --arm storm`, libsql 0.9.30, Windows, `--opens 48`, one file per open.** The decisive arm is release; the variable-separation matrix is debug. The rate itself stays in `.cargo/config.toml`, which remains its only home ([D-147](s13-decision-register.md#d-147)).

## What the old diagnosis was, and why it survived

From 0.6.0 this register, `.cargo/config.toml`, README and quickref all said **concurrent open**. The evidence was a reproducer that swept concurrency — 0/10 at 4, 8 and 16 simultaneous opens, 2/12 at 32, 5/12 at 128 — against a single sequential row: **500 opens, sequential, one process, 0/10**.

The sweep was sound. The sequential row was three orders of magnitude too short, and everything downstream inherited its conclusion.

## What was measured

`storm` runs the open storm with no soak database, no readers and no actor, and puts each candidate variable behind its own flag. Read the counts as **eliminated / not eliminated** and never as rates — [D-124](s13-decision-register.md#d-124) measured this instrument's noise band at ~30 points at n = 20, and these are n = 6–10.

| arm | faults | eliminated? |
|---|---|---|
| `--first-use build` (never calls `connect()`) | 0/6 | **yes** — at ~880,000 opens per run |
| `--first-use connect` (no query) | 6/6 | no |
| `--serial-opens` (`build()` under a mutex) | 6/6 | no |
| `--serial-connect` (`connect()` under a mutex) | 5/6 | no |
| `--hold` (every handle dropped in the parent) | 6/6 | no |
| `--sequential` (multi-thread runtime) | 4/6 | no |
| **`--sequential --current-thread`, release** | **2/10** | **no** |

The last row is the one that carries the entry. One task, one OS thread, `build → connect → query → drop` in a loop: no overlap, no worker migration, no concurrent teardown. It still faults, and its clean runs reached 9,792–10,416 opens.

**So 500 sequential opens is 0/10 because 500 is under the threshold, not because sequential is safe.**

The only row that eliminates the fault does so by never calling `connect()`, and it survives ~880,000 `build()` calls per run — a 100× volume margin over the arms that die. That is the whole finding: **what the fault counts is cumulative `connect()`.**

## Refined once more: distinct databases, not just calls

Every arm above opened a fresh file per iteration, so *cumulative `connect()`* and *cumulative distinct databases* moved together. `--same-file` separates them (n = 8, same configuration otherwise):

| | faults | how far runs got |
|---|---|---|
| a fresh file per open | 6/8 | faults from 2,272 |
| **one file reopened** | **1/8** | 7 clean runs reached **40,352–43,360** |

Read the counts rather than the rates — they are the robust half, and they do not depend on distinguishing 1/8 from 6/8. At ~40,000 reconnections to one database seven runs in eight are fine; against distinct databases the process is usually dead well before 20,000.

**So the dominant variable is distinct databases, with `connect()` as the call that pays for them.** Reopening one file is not immune, so this refines the volume reading rather than replacing it.

It also sharpens who is exposed, in the direction that matters: **this crate's production shape — one file, a bounded set of connections opened once — is the safest point on both axes at once.** The suite, which creates a temporary database per case, is the worst on both.

## What this explains that the old diagnosis could not

`.cargo/config.toml` has carried an unexplained anomaly since 0.5.6: every test binary is 0/15 **alone** while the serialised suite running those same binaries is 5/10. It was recorded as "the suite-level runs are finding concurrency that per-binary runs do not, and this data does not say where", with `Database::open`'s three connections named as the suspect and explicitly marked as not evidence.

There is no hidden concurrency. **The suite makes far more cumulative `connect()` calls than any one binary does.** The same reading covers the rest of the history: the three suite figures (1/20 at 0.5.4, 0/30 at 0.5.4, 5/10 at 0.5.6) track suite *size* — 171 → 221 tests — rather than thread count, and [D-147](s13-decision-register.md#d-147)'s five irreconcilable rates are five machines running the same work at five different speeds, which is five different totals per unit time.

It also retires the standing untested candidate. The multi-thread runtime was suspected because `Database::open` takes three connections that can overlap on different workers even with libtest serialised; a current-thread build scored 10/20 against 15/20 at n = 20, inside the noise band, settling nothing. `--sequential --current-thread` settles it: not the mechanism.

## What follows for the mitigations — nothing, yet

**`RUST_TEST_THREADS = "1"` stays.** It demonstrably lowers the rate; only the recorded mechanism was wrong. It plausibly lowers connections-per-unit-time, which lowers the total reached before a run ends.

**The `property-tests` quarantine stays**, and this entry strengthens its justification: a property case opens a database per generated case, which is the highest-volume shape in the repo.

**The production claim gets stronger, not weaker.** *One process, one file, a bounded set of connections opened once and never churned* is precisely the shape that never accumulates `connect()` calls, and the 500-sequential-clean figure now reads as confirmation rather than as the boundary of what was tested. The exposed party is, and always was, the test suite.

## Settled (W1.2a): there is no threshold, and the near-miss is the lesson

`--progress-file` makes the child record its running total once per round, durable *before* the fault rather than reported after it. Pooled n = 20 at one configuration:

```text
faults (17):  2144  2272  2272  6544 10176 10928 17536 ×8  19520 33856 40416
clean  (3):  42416 44864 46384   <- all three stopped on the time limit
```

**Not a fixed count.** Positions span an order of magnitude and the clean runs stopped only because time ran out — the shape of a per-`connect()` probability, very roughly 1 in 20,000–25,000 here. **So there is no number to send upstream, and the reproducer is the deliverable instead.**

**Now read the 17,536 column.** In the first session 8 of 11 faults landed on that exact value — not what noise looks like, and it reads as a hard threshold. Re-running the *identical* configuration produced it once in eight against a spread of 2,272–40,416. **It did not replicate**, and it is left in the table rather than dropped because an instrument that manufactures a convincing exact constant is worth knowing about. This is [D-124](s13-decision-register.md#d-124)'s noise band arriving in a new coordinate, and the only thing that caught it was running the same configuration twice before believing it.

## W1.3: the report is written

[`docs/libsql-issue.md`](../libsql-issue.md) replaces the 2026-07-31 report, whose stated trigger — concurrent open — this entry refutes. It carries a ~15-line single-threaded reproducer, the elimination table, the distinct-file comparison, and the non-threshold data, and it asks three specific questions rather than for a fix. **Sending it is the maintainer's action; nothing here does it.**

**Rejected.** *Putting a process-wide mutex in `open()`* — the [road map](../Macrame%20Road%20to%201.0.md) specified measuring before implementing precisely here, and the measurement refutes it: `--serial-opens` and `--serial-connect` both still fault. *Lifting the `property-tests` quarantine* — nothing here lowers the rate, and the volume reading says that step is the worst-exposed by construction. *Republishing the rate figures as a comparison between arms* — n = 6–10 against a ~30-point noise band, which is the error [D-124](s13-decision-register.md#d-124) retracted; the arms carry a boolean and the 100× margin on `--first-use build`, nothing finer. *Rewriting the six-release history of this row* — the concurrency sweep was correctly measured and correctly reported, and reading it as sloppiness rather than as an under-sampled control would lose the actual lesson, which is that a one-row arm is not a control.

---

<a id="d-149"></a>D-149 — The query planner gets **statistics**, bounded by `PRAGMA analysis_limit` so the write is schedulable (0.12.4). [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059), [D-064](s13-decision-register.md#d-064), [D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079), [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089), [D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146), [§5.2](s5-modules.md#52-graphbuilderrs--traversal-valid-time-and-attribute-fidelity), [§9](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#9-performance-budgets).

> **Every plan this crate has ever produced was costed against SQLite's built-in guesses, and the guess is "count the bound columns" — which is the defect D-042, D-059 and D-064 each found the hard way, standing as a permanent condition rather than a bug.**

**Fixture: `tests/analyze_tests.rs`, skewed out-degree — one 149-edge hub against 49 single-edge leaves.** Skew is load-bearing: uniform data is exactly where measured statistics and default guesses agree, so a uniform fixture would pass whether or not the statistics existed.

## What was wrong

`ANALYZE` and `PRAGMA optimize` appeared nowhere in `src/`. `sqlite_stat1` therefore existed in **no database Macrame had ever created**, and SQLite fell back to its defaults: assume ~1,048,576 rows, assume each bound equality column divides the search by ten.

That estimate is *structural*. It is a function of how many columns a query binds — not of what the table holds.

Now read [`tests/index_plan_tests.rs`](../../tests/index_plan_tests.rs)'s own summary of this schema's worst recurring defect: *"a covering index captures a query because it contains the columns, not because it discriminates."* D-042, D-059 and D-064 are three instances of a planner doing the only thing available to it. [`CREATE_INDICES`](../../src/schema/ddl.rs) declares two indices that both lead on `source_id`, and with no statistics they are separated by column count alone. D-059's measured numbers — 4.4 ms into an empty table, 1.06 s into a 90,000-edge hub — are what a wrong selectivity guess costs on a skewed graph, and a code graph is maximally skewed.

**The premise is asserted, not assumed.** `analyze_creates_statistics_that_did_not_exist` checks `sqlite_stat1` is empty *before* the first call. Without that half the test would pass in a world where the engine had been analysing all along, which is the world this entry claims we were not in.

## What was added

`Database::analyze()` runs `ANALYZE`; `Database::optimize()` runs `PRAGMA optimize`, which re-analyses only what SQLite believes has gone stale and is a no-op otherwise. Both are `LowPriCommand::Analyze` with their own [`CommandKind`](../../src/metrics.rs).

**Low priority is not a close call.** Statistics being seconds stale costs a plan that was already the plan a moment ago; preempting an interactive assertion costs a caller their latency bound.

`close()` calls `optimize()`, so a process that opens, works and closes keeps its statistics current with nobody arranging it. **A failure there warns and does not fail the close** — stale statistics cost plan quality and nothing else, and `close()` is where a caller learns whether their *writes* survived. Burying that answer under a much less important one would be the wrong trade.

## Why it is bounded, and why the bound lives on the connection

`ANALYZE` is a **write**: it writes `sqlite_stat1` and takes the write lock. Unbounded on a populated `links_current` that is exactly the unbudgeted hold `CHUNK_BUDGET` exists to prevent, and D-146 made hold time a *control input*, so an unbudgeted one now also perturbs the chunk controller.

`PRAGMA analysis_limit = 400` — SQLite's own recommendation — caps rows examined per index, making the cost a function of the index count (four) rather than the table size. **Measured in 0.12.23 and this sentence is wrong** ([D-166](s13-decision-register.md#d-166)): the pragma is in force and worth 3–4×, but what remains still grows with the table — 5.26 ms at 10,000 edges, 19.1 ms at 40,000, against a 3 ms budget. A constant factor, not an independence. Approximate statistics are emphatically enough here: the decision being informed is *which of two indices discriminates*, not a cardinality anyone reads.

It is set in `configure`, once per connection, rather than around each call. A limit applied only to the explicit path would leave the analysis `PRAGMA optimize` triggers internally unbounded — and that is the half that runs with nobody watching.

## Doctrine and migration: both already accommodate this

`sqlite_stat1` is derived state, rebuildable by re-running the command, carrying no assertions. That is **Doctrine VI** by construction; III and V do not reach it.

And no rung is needed. `verify` checks presence by name and its doc comment already argues for tolerating extra objects; `refuse_if_occupied` filters `name NOT LIKE 'sqlite_%'`, which `sqlite_stat1` matches. Both were checked rather than assumed.

## One thing this does not fix, said plainly

**It does nothing for the vector filter planner**, and `src/graph/vector_filter.rs` already says why: `sqlite_stat1` carries average rows-per-key, which estimates an equality predicate and not multi-hop reachability. That path measures with a capped probe and continues to. Selling `ANALYZE` as improving it would be the same category error this entry is about.

## A finding this turned up in passing

`the_analysis_limit_is_applied_where_connections_are_configured` reads the source rather than querying the database, because **the only connection a test can reach is the wrong one**: `diagnostic_conn()` does not call `configure()` at all and reports `analysis_limit = 0`. Harmless for `ANALYZE` — a read-only connection cannot analyse — but it means diagnostic reads run with a different `busy_timeout` than every other connection in the process. Recorded here, scheduled as W5.5 in the [road map](../Macrame%20Road%20to%201.0.md), and deliberately not fixed out of order.

**Rejected.** *Running `ANALYZE` inside `open()`* — it would put an unbudgeted write on the open path of every process, to fix statistics that are usually already fine. *Skipping `analysis_limit` for "accurate" statistics* — accuracy is not the constraint; a bounded hold is, and 400 rows per index answers the only question being asked. *Making `close()` fail on a failed `optimize()`* — above. *A background timer that analyses periodically* — a second scheduler beside the snapshot cadence, for work `close()` and an explicit call already cover; if a long-lived process needs it, it can call `optimize()` on its own cadence and say when. *Claiming this fixes the D-042/D-059/D-064 bug class* — it removes the blindness that generates it, and the plan-pinning registry still has to catch the instances.

---

<a id="d-150"></a>D-150 — Plan pinning needs **two fixtures and both directions**, and the direction that was missing found the review wrong on its own headline (0.12.5). [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059), [D-064](s13-decision-register.md#d-064), [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089), [D-118](s13-decision-register.md#d-118), [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy).

> **A gate that stops testing what it names does not go red. It goes quiet, and stays green, and that is worse than not having it.**

**Fixture: `tests/index_plan_tests.rs`, two of them.** `migrated` is the empty, unanalysed database this file has always used. `populated_and_analysed` is new: 260 concepts, one 150-edge hub against 60 single-edge leaves, `PRAGMA analysis_limit = 400`, then `ANALYZE`.

## Why a second fixture became necessary the moment D-149 shipped

Through 0.12.3 every assertion here ran against an empty database, and that was **faithful** — production had no statistics either, so both sides costed plans against SQLite's built-in defaults.

[D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149) ended that silently. The empty fixture still passes, still asserts a plan, and no longer asserts anything about the planner callers actually get. Nothing goes red; the gate simply stops describing production.

Both fixtures are kept and both are asserted, each named for the planner it describes. The empty one is not a leftover: **a fresh database before its first `ANALYZE` is a real state** — every process is in it between `open()` and the first `optimize()` — and the plans it gets are plans a caller runs.

**The skew is load-bearing, not decoration.** Uniform data is precisely where measured statistics and default guesses agree, so a uniform fixture would pass whether or not `ANALYZE` had ever run and would prove nothing about either. It is also what a code graph is, and what D-059's 4.4 ms → 1.06 s spread was measured on ([D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088)'s argument, applied to statistics rather than to shape).

`every_justified_index_is_the_one_the_planner_picks_with_statistics` guards its own fixture by asserting `sqlite_stat1` is non-empty first. Without that, an `ANALYZE` that silently did nothing would leave every assertion below it passing under a name claiming otherwise.

**Result worth recording: all four pinned plans survive the addition of statistics.** The declared indices discriminate, they do not merely match structurally — which is the D-042/D-059/D-064 property nobody had been in a position to check.

## The other direction, and what it caught immediately

[D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089) built this registry keyed by **index**, which catches an index no query reads. §5.2 of the [review](../Macrame%20Codebase%20Review%20v0.12.0.md) names the inverse hole: it cannot catch *a query that quietly leaves its index*, for any query no entry happens to name, because there is nothing to hang an assertion on. `QUERY_REGISTRY` is that direction — keyed by query, recording the plan each is **measured** to get.

It was wrong on its first run, and the entry that was wrong was mine.

The review's §2.1 lists five queries that scan `links` and leads with the one on every `open()`: `recorded_at_floor`'s `MAX(recorded_at)`. That was reasoned from the schema — `recorded_at` is the fifth primary-key column, so `MAX()` cannot seek — and never executed. Measured, it plans as:

```text
... | UNION ALL | SEARCH links USING COVERING INDEX sqlite_autoindex_links_1 | ...
```

**`SEARCH`, not `SCAN`.** SQLite serves the bare `MAX()` from the primary key's covering index without traversing the table. There is no unbounded startup cost to close, and the "seconds of startup on a 10M-row `links`" claim has nothing behind it.

The other four rows were confirmed the same way and do scan. So the finding survives at four queries instead of five, and **W3.1's index has to justify itself on those** — [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089) exists because an index bought on a believed benefit is an index write per insert, forever.

## Recording a defect rather than asserting the fix

Two `QUERY_REGISTRY` entries record a **scan**, with the review section that names it. That is deliberate and is not an endorsement: it puts the defect where a change has to walk past it, and it means W3 **cannot land quietly** — the indices it adds turn those rows red and the commit adding them has to come here and state the new plan. An entry that already expected the good plan would go green on its own and nobody would review the improvement.

`fragment` is a substring rather than whole-plan equality, because pinning the entire `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` string would go red on any SQLite wording change and train people to re-bless it without reading.

**Rejected.** *Replacing the empty fixture rather than adding to it* — an unanalysed database is a real state and its plans are real; dropping it trades one blind spot for another. *Pinning whole plan strings* — above. *Asserting the seek W3 will produce, so the tests are "ready"* — a test that passes before the work and after it distinguishes nothing, and the whole value here is that the improvement gets read. *Quietly editing §2.1's five to four* — the review is a dated artefact and the correction is more instructive than the corrected text; it is annotated in place with what was measured and how. *Treating the `MAX()` result as evidence that W3.1 should be dropped* — four queries still scan, and one of them is the quadratic concept-archival predicate; what changed is which query justifies the index, not whether one is justified.

<a id="d-151"></a>D-151 — The two archive indexes on `links`, justified on the queries that measured as scans rather than the one the review led with (0.12.6, W3.1/W3.2/W3.3/W3.5). [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036), [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089), [D-150](s13-decision-register.md#d-150), review §2.1/§2.2, [§4.2](s4-schema.md#42-links-assertion-history-and-current-belief-materialization). Shipped as the `v10 -> v11` rung `links-archive-indices`.

`links` carried a primary key and nothing else. The PK leads on `source_id`, and neither archive predicate seeks on `source_id`, so both scanned the ledger. Measured on the populated-and-analysed fixture, before and after, in one run of the same probe:

```text
LINKS_ARCHIVABLE (SELECT and DELETE, identical plans)
  before   SCAN links | CORRELATED SCALAR SUBQUERY 1 | SEARCH newer ...
  after    SEARCH links USING INDEX idx_links_recorded_at (recorded_at<?)
             | CORRELATED SCALAR SUBQUERY 1 | SEARCH newer ...

CONCEPTS_ARCHIVABLE (SELECT and DELETE)
  before   CORRELATED SCALAR SUBQUERY 1
             | SCAN links USING COVERING INDEX sqlite_autoindex_links_1
  after    CORRELATED SCALAR SUBQUERY 1 | MULTI-INDEX OR
             | INDEX 1 | SEARCH links USING COVERING INDEX
                          sqlite_autoindex_links_1 (source_id=?)
             | INDEX 2 | SEARCH links USING INDEX idx_links_target (target_id=?)
```

The inner supersession probe was never the problem — it binds the whole primary-key prefix and always did. It was the outer `recorded_at <` that had nothing to seek on, which is worth stating because the subquery is the part that *looks* expensive.

**W3.3 is answered by measurement and the rewrite is not taken.** The plan anticipated that SQLite might decline to use two indexes for an `OR` inside a correlated subquery, and scheduled a `UNION`-of-two-seekable-halves rewrite if so. It does not decline: `MULTI-INDEX OR` runs both arms as seeks and unions the rowids. The rewrite was measured anyway rather than assumed unnecessary, and it is **not better** — it produces a `COMPOUND QUERY` whose right half, before the index existed, SQLite served by building an `AUTOMATIC COVERING INDEX (target_id=?)` at query time. That last detail is the strongest single argument for `idx_links_target`: the planner had already concluded the index was worth having and was paying to construct a throwaway copy of it per statement. So the predicate in `archive.rs` is unchanged, which also keeps a predicate whose prose justification ([`CONCEPTS_ARCHIVABLE`](../../src/temporal/archive.rs)) is long and load-bearing from being rewritten for a benefit that does not exist.

**What justifies `idx_links_recorded_at` is not what the review said justified it.** Review §2.1 led with `recorded_at_floor`, the `MAX(recorded_at)` read on every `open()`, and predicted seconds of startup on a large ledger. [D-150](s13-decision-register.md#d-150) measured that as a covering-index seek, not a scan, and this release confirms the other side: with the new index the clock floor reads `SEARCH links USING COVERING INDEX idx_links_recorded_at` — the planner swapped which covering index answers it and the cost did not move. The index is justified on the two archive queries and on nothing else, and `QUERY_REGISTRY`'s clock-floor entry is deliberately kept pinned to the *unnamed* fragment `SEARCH links USING COVERING INDEX` so that it cannot be read as a claim that the new index serves it.

**First rung to index a frozen table, and that is the permitted case rather than an exception.** Every index rung before this one landed on `links_current`, which [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036) gives no stability guarantee at all. These land on `links`, a normative ledger table. D-036 restricts post-1.0 change on the core to additive operations and names `ADD COLUMN` and **new indexes** as the two that qualify: an index adds no column, moves no row, and changes no bitemporal semantics, and a v10 and a v11 database hold identical `links` rows. Said explicitly in `add_links_archive_indices` because the next rung to touch `links` may not have the same answer.

**`idx_links_target` is not `idx_lc_tgt_active` readmitted**, and the resemblance is the trap. The dropped one was `(target_id, valid_to)` on `links_current`, the materialization, and it was dropped because nothing sought on it. This one is `(target_id)` on `links`, the ledger, and `CONCEPTS_ARCHIVABLE` seeks on exactly that column. D-089's rule was never "no index on a target column"; it was "an index needs a named query that seeks on it", and the registry enforces the difference — the `Justification::Query` entry carries the reproduced SQL plus an `include_str!` check that the original still exists in `archive.rs`.

**The cost being accepted**, stated plainly: two extra index writes per insert into the crate's ledger table, forever, on a write path that already takes several. That is the D-089 trade, taken with the before/after in hand rather than on a believed benefit.

**The rung test asserts the plan, not the index.** `a_version_bump_must_bring_its_own_rung_test` asks each rung to assert what it is *for*, and presence-only is the weak form — a rung creating an index nothing seeks on passes it, which is precisely the D-089 failure. So the v10 -> v11 test drops both indexes, stamps the database back to v10, asserts the plan is a scan, migrates, and asserts the seek. It fails if the index exists and the planner declines it.

Rejected: *the `UNION` rewrite of `CONCEPTS_ARCHIVABLE`* (measured, unnecessary, and structurally worse — see above); *citing the clock floor as justification* (measured false twice, D-150 and here); *a composite `(target_id, valid_to)` on `links` mirroring the dropped `links_current` index* (`CONCEPTS_ARCHIVABLE` binds `target_id` alone and the extra column is payload nothing reads — the exact shape D-089 dropped); *deferring these to 0.14.0 with the rest of the performance work* (the concept-archival predicate is quadratic, and a quadratic on the archive path is a correctness-adjacent defect once a ledger is large enough that anyone runs the archive); *presence-only rung test* (above).

**W3.4, 0.12.7 — the singular write paths point at their bulk equivalents, and writing the note found a sharper edge than the one it was written for.** Review §2.3's finding is that `assert_edge` and `upsert_concept` each pay the ~0.8 ms per-transaction floor ([D-090](s13-decision-register.md#d-090)) for one row, so a thousand rows written singly spend ~0.8 s in transaction overhead before any work — and mint a thousand distinct `recorded_at` stamps for what the caller probably means as one act. The fix is documentation and not deprecation: only the caller knows whether their thousand rows are one act, which [Doctrine III](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-iii) makes a semantic question rather than a performance one. CodeRadar's original assessment reached for a *new* bulk API precisely because it did not find the existing one, which is the failure being closed.

The first draft said "use `bulk_import`" and described it as the one write with no latency bound. That is two methods conflated: **`bulk_import` is chunked** against `CHUNK_BUDGET` and atomic per chunk, while **`write_bulk_atomic`** is the one transaction under one stamp whose hold is a function of `edges.len()` ([D-081](s13-decision-register.md#d-081)). A caller told only "use the bulk one" can pick the stalling form for a batch with no atomicity requirement — turning a documented latency floor into an undocumented latency *ceiling*. So the note names both and says which is which, and the concept side says that no atomic-across-chunks path exists and none is needed to make the choice. Appendix A carries the same note at the point the singular calls appear, because that is where the decision is actually taken.

<a id="d-152"></a>D-152 — `Rehydrate` becomes its own `CommandKind`, and keeping its budget exemption was the harder half (0.12.9, W4.3). [D-012](s13-decision-register.md#d-012), [D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079), [D-131](s13-decision-register.md#d-131), review §4.4, [§5.10](s5-modules.md#510-metricsrs--what-the-actor-holds-the-lock-for). Enabled by [W4.2](../Macrame%20Road%20to%201.0.md)'s `#[non_exhaustive]`.

From 0.9.0 to 0.12.8 `LowPriCommand::Rehydrate` mapped to `CommandKind::Archive`. The code said why, and the reason was honest: rehydration is the archive path run backwards and shares its budget, and a `CommandKind` variant was a breaking addition. But a shared *budget* is not shared *attribution*, and attribution is the entire product of a metrics surface. An operator reading a long `archive` hold could not tell whether the database had archived anything at all — the two commands move rows in opposite directions across the same file boundary.

**This is the concrete evidence for review §4.4 rather than an illustration of it.** The finding is that the metrics types are unfrozen and every later addition to them is a break. The codebase had already paid that cost, silently, by shipping wrong attribution for four releases rather than take a major version. `#[non_exhaustive]` ([W4.2](../Macrame%20Road%20to%201.0.md)) removed the obstacle and this variant is the first thing it bought, which is also the argument that the attribute was worth adding rather than a precaution against a hypothetical.

**The trap, and it would have shipped quietly.** `exempt_from_budget` names the kinds `CHUNK_BUDGET` does not apply to, and `Rehydrate` was on that list **by inheritance** — it held `Archive`'s kind, so nobody had ever decided it. Giving it its own variant would have flipped it to non-exempt with no line of code saying so, and since a rehydrate is one unchunked transaction moving rows back across the file boundary, **every single one would have counted as a budget violation**. `violations()` would then have become useless on any database that rehydrates, which is precisely the failure the `Archive` exemption exists to prevent, arriving through the back door of a change made for attribution. It is exempt, now on the merits and on the record: rehydration makes the same claim about its hold that archival does — bulk movement with no latency bound, requested explicitly by the caller.

**That exposed a second copy of the exemption list, one row out of date.** The exemptions exist in `exempt_from_budget` and again as the table in `CHUNK_BUDGET`'s rustdoc, which [§8.6](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md) names as where a reader looks for the budget's scope. Two descriptions of one fact is the drift [D-035](s13-decision-register.md#d-035) exists to prevent, and here they were one row apart: `rehydrate` was exempt in code and absent from the table, so the **documented** scope of the budget was narrower than the **enforced** one, and had been since 0.9.0. Both are corrected, and `the_budget_exemptions_and_their_documented_table_agree` now asserts them in both directions. The extra-row direction is the one worth having: a missing row is a budget that silently does not apply, but an extra row promises a caller an exemption the violation counter is about to disagree with.

The variant is appended at the **end** of the enum and of `ALL`, per [`CommandKind::index`]'s rule — declaration order is a persisted contract in two languages. Python needs no binding change: `kind` is already a string and `as_str()` gives it `"rehydrate"`, and `tests_py`'s kind assertion is a subset check.

**Addendum, 0.12.15.** The test above had a defect of its own, found while W5.4 was in flight and worth recording because the failure mode is the opposite of useful: it split the source on the literal `"
///
"`, which is a string a **Windows checkout does not contain**. `core.autocrlf=true` is the platform default, every line ends `
`, the terminator never matched, `table` silently became the entire rest of `connection.rs`, and every kind was found "named" — so the test reported the two lists as disagreeing, naming `assert_edge`, when they agreed perfectly. A test that reads source must tolerate both line endings; it now takes lines, because `lines()` already does. The other three source-reading tests were checked and are unaffected: `"
}"` and `"
<a id="` still match across `
`, and `graph_tests`' `UNION
` is asserted against a runtime-built string rather than a file.

Rejected: *leaving it folded and documenting the conflation* (the documentation would have to say "read `archive` as archive-or-rehydrate", which is a metric that requires a footnote to interpret — the thing being fixed); *a `direction` field on `KindSnapshot` instead of a variant* (a second axis to carry one bit, and it would not have made `violations()` correct); *making `Rehydrate` non-exempt on the ground that it is not `Archive`* (measured consequence above: it destroys the violation counter on any rehydrating database); *a test that only checks the code list* (the drift was between the code and the prose, so a test reading one of them cannot see it).

<a id="d-153"></a>D-153 — The `biased` select's missing floor is now measured, and the first measurement hits the bound completely (0.12.10, W4.4). [D-028](s13-decision-register.md#d-028), [D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079), [D-124](s13-decision-register.md#d-124), review §4.1, [§5.1.5](s5-modules.md#515-cooperative-chunking--the-golden-rule). Evidence: `tests/actor_metrics_tests.rs::the_starvation_counter_distinguishes_a_backlog_from_a_quiet_actor`.

`run_writer_actor`'s `tokio::select!` is `biased`, so the arms poll in declaration order and high-priority work is taken whenever any is ready. Nothing bounds how long that can continue. The design has carried that property since the two-tier channel was introduced and no build has ever been able to say whether it mattered.

`MetricsSnapshot` now carries two fields. `low_starved_turns` counts turns where the actor took high-priority work while low-priority work was already queued. `low_starved_run_max` is the longest unbroken run of them — the most turns any single low-priority command has waited. **The second is the one with teeth**: a large total on a busy database says the tiers are doing their job, and only a long *run* distinguishes "prioritised" from "starved". The current run is kept but not exposed, because a live run length read at an arbitrary point in a run means nothing.

`low_queued` is the depth sampled *before* the `select!`, reused from the reading `record_turn` already takes so the two cannot disagree. That makes the counter conservative: by the time a high-priority arm fires the low queue may have grown, and work that arrived after the choice was made is never counted as having been starved by it. A low-priority turn **resets** the run rather than decrementing it, because the question is how many turns one command waited, which is a run length and not a balance.

**The measurement, and it is not a near miss.** Fixture: a 39-edge chunked `bulk_import` on the low tier, raced by 64 concurrently spawned `upsert_concept` calls on the high tier. Identical across five runs — `starved_turns=63`, `run_max=63`, `turns=70`. The run *equals* the total: the bulk import sat behind every single queued high-priority write, with no interleaving whatsoever. The run length is not bounded by anything in the crate; it is bounded by how much high-priority work the caller offers.

**What this does and does not establish.** It establishes that the mechanism is unbounded and reachable trivially — a burst of interactive writes defers background work completely, for as long as the burst lasts. It does **not** establish that a production workload hits it; 64 tasks spawned at once is a synthetic burst, and a caller writing sequentially never queues anything (the same test asserts `run_max == 0` for that case, which is the half that keeps the counter from reporting starvation on every database forever).

**No policy is added here, and that is the plan's instruction rather than this entry's conclusion.** The road map's W4.4 says to measure before adding a fairness mechanism, and its §16 rejects a forced yield outright on the stated ground that "whether the bound is ever hit in practice is a measurement nobody has taken". **That premise is now false and the rejection should be re-read, not inherited.** What is still open is whether the synthetic burst above is evidence about production or only about the mechanism — which is a judgement, not a measurement, and is left to the maintainer rather than taken here.

**Settled 2026-08-15: scheduled as W10.4 in 0.14.0**, neither shipped blind nor dropped. That wave takes a reading from a workload shaped like use rather than like a stress test, and adds the floor only if the reading earns it. The reason it is a 0.14.0 wave and not a 0.13.0 one is that this counter was added in the release that would carry the fix — a before/after taken with it alone measures the fix against itself.

Rejected: *adding a forced yield in the same release as the counter* (it would make the counter's first reading a measurement of the fix rather than of the problem, and the fix would ship with no before/after); *exposing the live run length* (meaningless at an arbitrary sample point, and it invites a caller to poll for a condition the high-water mark already records); *counting starvation from the post-select queue depth* (would attribute work that arrived after the choice to the choice); *decrementing the run on a low-priority turn* (a run is not a balance); *pinning `63` in the test* (it is a property of the machine and the scheduler — D-147's lesson — so the test asserts the counter can see the condition and this entry records the number).

<a id="d-154"></a>D-154 — `metrics` becomes a default feature, and the attempt to measure its cost is a better argument than the cost figure would have been (0.12.11, W4.5). [D-055](s13-decision-register.md#d-055), [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070), [D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079), [D-090](s13-decision-register.md#d-090), [D-093](s13-decision-register.md#d-093), [D-124](s13-decision-register.md#d-124), review §4.3. Evidence: `benches/budgets.rs`, `write_path` group.

**The argument is not that the counters are cheap.** It is that a crate organised entirely around a latency bound must not ship a default build that cannot report whether the bound is met. `violations()` is the answer to the only question this design asks about itself, and from 0.6.0 to 0.12.10 the default build could not answer it. [D-093](s13-decision-register.md#d-093) made exactly this argument for the Python wheel — where a caller cannot rebuild the extension — and won it; the Rust default sat on the wrong side of the same reasoning for six releases. `--no-default-features` remains, and is still built and tested.

The cost, stated honestly rather than as a benchmark: **11–14 relaxed atomic RMW operations per actor turn** — `record_turn` 5, `record_priority_choice` 1–3 ([D-153](s13-decision-register.md#d-153) moved this from the road map's stated 10–11), `record_hold` 5–6 — plus ~1.6 KB of counters. The clock reading is *not* part of this: it has been unconditional since 0.12.0 ([W4.1](../Macrame%20Road%20to%201.0.md) corrected the `Cargo.toml` paragraph that said otherwise).

**The measurement, and the wrong answer it gave first.** `write_path` was benchmarked with the feature on and off. One run per arm said metrics-off was **9.0% faster on `assert_edge` and 16.0% faster on `upsert_concept`, both p < 0.05**, with criterion printing *"Performance has improved."* That result is false, and two things say so before any further data:

- **The magnitude is physically impossible.** 9% of a 275 µs `assert_edge` is ~25 µs. Fourteen relaxed atomic RMWs cost tens of nanoseconds. The claim is off by three orders of magnitude.
- **The control moved too.** `control/select_1` is a bare `SELECT 1` on a separate connection that never enters the write actor, so no metrics code runs in it at all — and across the pair of sessions preceding this it shifted by up to 39%.

So the arms were re-run **alternating**, absolute times compared rather than criterion's session-to-session `change:`. Three rounds each, on a machine still under load, gave the confound in the open — within-arm spread 8–26%, sign flipping between rounds, and `control/select_1` differing by **+4.8%** between arms, *more* than `upsert_concept` did at −0.7%. A feature cannot affect a row it does not execute in, so that is the noise floor measured directly.

Six rounds each, repeated once the machine had settled, gives the bound rather than just the refutation:

| row | on (µs) | off (µs) | Δ | effect vs pooled sd | per-round sign |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `control/select_1` | 1.512 | 1.510 | −0.11% | 0.24 sd | `+-+-++` |
| `assert_edge` | 246.17 | 245.74 | −0.18% | 0.27 sd | `-++--+` |
| `upsert_concept` | 195.38 | 195.16 | −0.12% | 0.30 sd | `-+-+--` |

**All three rows move by the same amount, and the control is one of them.** Every effect is under a third of a standard deviation with the sign alternating between rounds, and the two write paths are indistinguishable from a bare `SELECT 1` that runs no metrics code at all. The absolute times also dropped from ~280 µs to ~246 µs between the noisy session and the quiet one, on identical code — which is the session drift, stated as a number.

So the cost is bounded rather than merely unresolvable: **under 0.2% of a write, or ~0.4 µs on a 246 µs `assert_edge`, and consistent with zero.** That agrees with the arithmetic — 14 relaxed atomic RMWs is tens of nanoseconds, ~0.01% of the operation, an order of magnitude below what this harness can resolve. [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070)'s session noise did exactly what the road map predicted, **and that is treated as the result.** A cost this crate's own harness cannot distinguish from a control row is not a cost worth defaulting a latency-bound crate blind for.

**The methodological finding is the one worth carrying forward, because it will recur.** Criterion's `change:` and its p-value compare the current run against the last *saved* baseline, which is a different session. The p-value is computed from within-session variance and is structurally blind to the between-session confound, so it will report `p = 0.00` and *"Performance has improved"* for a difference that is entirely session drift. That is precisely how [D-124](s13-decision-register.md#d-124) came to be retracted, and it nearly happened again here in the opposite direction — this time the noise argued *for* a decision rather than against it, which is the harder case to catch. **Any A/B across a rebuild must alternate arms and compare absolute times**; the control row exists to make the confound visible and should be read first, before the row anybody cares about.

Rejected: *reporting the single-pair 9%/16% figures* (false, and the physical sanity check refutes them before the replicates do); *trusting criterion's significance markers across a feature switch* (above); *keeping `metrics` off because a cost could not be ruled out* (it inverts the burden — the default build of a latency-bounded ledger should have to justify being unable to report its own bound, not the other way round); *removing `--no-default-features` support* (a consumer who genuinely does not want the counters should keep the option, and the path is cheap to keep tested); *dropping the now-redundant `features = ["metrics"]` in `bindings/python/Cargo.toml`* (it would make the wheel's counters a consequence of this crate's default rather than of [D-093](s13-decision-register.md#d-093), which is a decision about the wheel).

<a id="d-155"></a>D-155 — One `Tuning` struct replaces the `open*` family, and neither of the two attributes the plan specified survived contact (0.12.12, W5.1). [D-053](s13-decision-register.md#d-053), [D-062](s13-decision-register.md#d-062), review §4.5, F-30. Evidence: `tests/tuning_tests.rs`.

There were three constructors — `open`, `open_with_cadence`, `open_with_clock` — and each new knob added one more with the combinatorics of the ones before it. 0.13.0 alone wants three more (`wal_autocheckpoint`, and a page cache each for the writer and the readers), which is where naming stops being possible. `Database::open_tuned(path, Tuning { .. })` is the form that grows; the three stay, delegate, and are **not deprecated**, because `open(path)` is the right call for most callers and should not acquire a warning for being the common case.

**`#[non_exhaustive]` was specified for this struct and does not compile.** The road map's design rests on callers writing `Tuning { writer_cache_size: Some(-64_000), ..Default::default() }` so that a later field is additive. A `#[non_exhaustive]` **struct** cannot be constructed with literal syntax outside its defining crate *at all*, and the functional-update form is literal syntax — so the attribute makes exactly the expression it was added to protect an `E0639` for every external caller. The rule is not the one for enums, where `#[non_exhaustive]` only forces a wildcard arm; [`CadencePolicy`] keeps the attribute for that reason and the struct drops it. Growth therefore rests on `Default` alone, which absorbs added fields for anyone writing `..Default::default()` and breaks anyone writing an exhaustive literal — a compile error at the call site with an obvious fix, not a silent behaviour change.

**`cadence: Option<SnapshotCadence>` was also specified, and it is a trap in a `Default` struct.** In the old constructors `None` means *no cadence at all* ([D-053](s13-decision-register.md#d-053)). Carried into a struct that derives `Default`, that makes it the one field whose `None` requests a behaviour change rather than a default, and `open_tuned(path, Tuning::default())` would then silently stop writing snapshot anchors while `open(path)` kept writing them — two calls that read as synonyms, one of which quietly disables the durability mechanism. So the tri-state is written out as `CadencePolicy::{Default, Disabled, Every}`: every field's absence now means *leave it alone*, and switching the cadence off has to be asked for by name. `from_legacy` maps the old `None` to `Disabled`, and `the_legacy_none_still_means_disabled` pins that direction — an inverted mapping would hand a background task to every caller who had explicitly declined one, and nothing else in the suite would notice, because an extra anchor is not an error.

`Tuning` ships with two fields rather than five. `wal_autocheckpoint` and the two cache sizes arrive in W5.3 and W5.4, which is the additive growth this struct exists for being demonstrated on its first release rather than asserted about a future one — a field that does nothing until a later wave wires it is a documented knob that lies.

`Debug` is hand-written because `Clock` is not `Debug` and requiring that of every implementor to print a handle here would be the tail wagging the dog; the field reports present-or-absent, which is the part a reader of a dump can act on.

Rejected: *a builder with setters* (the other way to keep `#[non_exhaustive]`; the field-literal form is the legible one and the growth this needs to survive is additive fields, which `..Default::default()` already absorbs); *deprecating the three constructors* (0.13.0 is not a break, and `open(path)` should not warn); *`Option<Option<SnapshotCadence>>`* (correct and unreadable); *shipping all five fields now with three of them inert* (a knob that is documented and does nothing is worse than one that does not exist yet).

<a id="d-156"></a>D-156 — `checkpoint()` is a high-priority command that returns what SQLite reported, and getting the numbers out takes two pragmas (0.12.13, W5.2). §4.5, F-30, [D-152](s13-decision-register.md#d-152), [D-154](s13-decision-register.md#d-154). Evidence: `tests/checkpoint_tests.rs`.

WAL mode means the `.db` file alone is not the database: recent commits live in the `-wal` sidecar until a checkpoint moves them. Until now the crate had no way to ask for that, so a caller copying the file elsewhere was copying a partial database and a caller who wanted the WAL bounded had only SQLite's 1,000-page automatic checkpointer and no way to influence it. That is §4.5, and it is also why W5.2 and W5.3 are one wave: `wal_autocheckpoint: None` without an explicit counterpart is a footgun with no safe form.

**High priority, and the reason is not latency.** A checkpoint is asked for at the end of a bulk load or before taking a copy, so queueing it behind the background work it was meant to follow inverts the request. It is also the only command in either tier that is not a transaction.

**It returns a `CheckpointReport`, not `()`.** A checkpoint that moved nothing and a checkpoint that reclaimed a 400 MB WAL are the same `Ok(())`, and that difference is the whole reason the caller asked. `busy` is the field that must not be ignored: `TRUNCATE` waits for readers only as long as `busy_timeout` allows and then gives up, so a busy checkpoint is an `Ok` whose WAL is still there — and a caller who was about to copy the file away needs to know that.

**Two pragmas, which is not the obvious implementation, and the reason is measured.** A successful `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` reports `busy=0, log=0, checkpointed=0`: the counts describe the WAL *after* the operation, and after a truncation there is no WAL to describe. On a 387-frame WAL, `PASSIVE` returns `0, 387, 387` and `TRUNCATE` on the same file returns `0, 0, 0`. So the single-pragma implementation returns a report whose two counts are structurally zero on success, which is a less useful `bool` wearing a struct's clothes. `FULL` copies the frames and reports what it moved; the `TRUNCATE` that follows finds nothing left to copy and resets the file, which makes the second pass close to free — it is a file operation, not a second copy. `busy` is the union of the two, because a checkpoint blocked in either phase did not fully happen and the caller needs the pessimistic answer.

**The mode is fixed rather than a parameter.** `PASSIVE` is what the automatic checkpointer already runs, so asking for it explicitly requests something that was going to happen anyway; `RESTART` and `FULL` differ from `TRUNCATE` only in whether the file is left at its high-water size. The caller this exists for — autocheckpoint off, one checkpoint at the end — wants the WAL gone. Selecting a mode would be an additive method later, not a change to this one.

**`CommandKind::Checkpoint` is exempt from [`CHUNK_BUDGET`](../src/connection.rs), and that is the half that would have failed quietly.** Its hold is a function of accumulated WAL, which is a function of how long since the last checkpoint — not of anything the caller passed. A non-exempt checkpoint would count as a budget violation on any database large enough to want one, destroying `violations()` for exactly the callers who checkpoint. That is [D-152](s13-decision-register.md#d-152)'s trap arriving one wave later, and it was caught by the test that wave added: `the_budget_exemptions_and_their_documented_table_agree` fails on a kind added to the code list and not to `CHUNK_BUDGET`'s table, in both directions. The exemption table now has five rows, and this is the only one that is not a transaction: there is no smaller unit to chunk into, because the operation *is* the copy.

Rejected: *returning `()`* (above — the counts are the product); *`TRUNCATE` alone* (measured to zero the counts on success); *a mode parameter* (three of the four modes are not things a caller of this crate wants, and choosing between them requires reading SQLite's documentation rather than this crate's); *low priority* (it would queue behind the bulk work it exists to follow); *surfacing SQLite's `-1` counts* (`busy` already says the checkpoint did not happen, and a negative frame count is not a quantity); *running it on a timer inside the crate* (SQLite's automatic checkpointer already does that, and doing it twice takes the write lock to buy nothing).

<a id="d-157"></a>D-157 — `wal_autocheckpoint` becomes settable, the default does not move, and F-30 turns out to be a tail rather than an oscillation (0.12.14, W5.3). F-30, [D-090](s13-decision-register.md#d-090), [D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146), [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155), [D-156](s13-decision-register.md#d-156). Evidence: `tests/wal_policy_tests.rs`; measurement below.

`Tuning::wal_autocheckpoint` takes a `WalCheckpointPolicy` — `Default`, `Disabled`, or `EveryPages(u32)` — and is applied to the **write connection only**, which is the only connection in the crate that commits and therefore the only one whose autocheckpoint threshold can ever fire. Setting it on the readers would be a pragma with no path to running.

**Not `Option<u32>`, for [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155)'s reason exactly.** The plan specified `Option<u32>` with `None` disabling. In a struct that derives `Default`, a field whose `None` turns a mechanism off turns it off for everyone who did not mention it — and this mechanism is the one that keeps the WAL from growing without bound. Absence means "leave it alone" everywhere in `Tuning`. `EveryPages(0)` is deliberately *not* remapped to `Disabled` even though SQLite treats the value that way: a caller whose computed threshold came out zero has a bug, and inheriting the overload silently would turn it into an unbounded WAL.

**The default is unchanged**, which is the other half of the promise. 1,000 pages stays.

**The measurement F-30 asked for, and it partly refutes the finding's framing.** Three rounds, 6,000 concepts of 1 KB through `write_concepts`, release build:

| | longest chunk hold | mean | chunks | over budget | wall |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| autocheckpoint on (default) | **9.3–10.3 ms** | 2.40–2.44 ms | 125–130 | 24–28 | 304–321 ms |
| autocheckpoint off | **4.50 ms** | 2.08–2.20 ms | 142–153 | 18–27 | 298–339 ms |

The tail is real and reproducible: the longest hold roughly halves, and the >10 ms histogram bucket is populated only with the checkpointer on. That bucket *is* the checkpoint, landing inside a chunk transaction and being charged to it — F-30's mechanism, observed.

**But the controller does not stop oscillating, because that was never what was happening.** `over_budget` overlaps between the arms and total wall time is identical within noise. The controller works near the budget boundary either way, for reasons that have nothing to do with checkpoints: [D-090](s13-decision-register.md#d-090)'s ~0.8 ms per-transaction floor, and the one-or-two-chunk convergence cost D-146 accepted. The road map asked whether disabling autocheckpoint makes `next_chunk_size` stop oscillating; the answer is **no**, and what it actually removes is an outlier. Recorded because the wrong version of this — "F-30 fixed, the controller is now stable" — is the kind of claim that gets inherited.

**The cost is deferred, not removed**, and the number is the argument for keeping this a knob. The explicit [`Database::checkpoint`] at the end of the same fixture moved **8,400–9,100 frames in 41–45 ms** with the checkpointer off, against **~860 frames in 5.5–6.2 ms** with it on. Identical work, relocated: out of the latency-bounded path and into one hold whose timing the caller chose. That is a good trade for a bulk importer and a bad one for an interactive process, which is why the default does not move.

Rejected: *`Option<u32>` as specified* (above); *remapping `EveryPages(0)` to `Disabled`* (hides a caller's arithmetic bug behind SQLite's overload); *changing the default to disabled now that `checkpoint()` exists* (it would make every existing caller's WAL unbounded to fix a 10 ms outlier they may never have noticed); *setting the pragma on every connection* (only the writer commits); *reading the setting back in the test* (the pragma is per-connection and the writer is deliberately unnameable — the behavioural assertion on WAL size is both possible and stronger).

<a id="d-158"></a>D-158 — The page cache is two knobs, because the writer and the readers have opposite profiles (0.12.15, W5.4). §4.5, [D-091](s13-decision-register.md#d-091), [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155), R15. Evidence: `tests/cache_size_tests.rs`.

`configure()` set no `cache_size` at all, so every connection ran at SQLite's default of −2000 (2 MB) and there was no way to change it. `Tuning::writer_cache_size` and `Tuning::reader_cache_size` are now separate fields, applied per connection: the writer gets the first, and the shared [`Database::read_conn`], the snapshot cadence's connection, and — after W5.5 — each [`Database::diagnostic_conn`] get the second.

**One number cannot serve both, and the asymmetry is structural rather than a matter of taste.** There is exactly one writer, it is long-lived, and it holds the write lock while it works, so its cache is a fixed cost paid once and every page it re-reads from disk is time no other writer can use. Read-only connections are plural — `diagnostic_conn` mints a new one per call, and the R15 hazard that method documents is precisely about concurrent opens — so a value here is multiplied by however many a caller has open. A single shared number must therefore be small enough for the multiplied case, which is the wrong size for the one connection that holds the lock.

**`None` runs no pragma rather than restating −2000.** The default stays SQLite's to define; writing it out here would freeze a number this crate did not choose and would have to be revisited every time SQLite changed it. This is the one place in [`Tuning`](../src/connection.rs) where `Option` is the right shape rather than a policy enum ([D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155), [D-157](s13-decision-register.md#d-157)), and the distinction is worth naming: the two enums exist because "leave it alone" was ambiguous for a *mechanism* that could be off or default. For a *value*, leaving it alone is just not setting it.

**SQLite's units are preserved: negative is KiB, positive is pages.** `Some(-64_000)` is 64 MB; `Some(64_000)` is 64,000 pages, which at this crate's 4 KiB page size is 256 MB. Normalising the two into one "bytes" field was rejected — a caller who knows the pragma would then be silently wrong by a factor of the page size, and the crate would be redefining a well-known interface for the sake of tidiness.

**The test asserts the split, not the pragma.** Two distinct values are passed and the shared reader is asked which one it got. A single shared field, or a wiring mistake that hands the writer's value to both, produces a value that is *right everywhere* — which is exactly the bug, and is invisible to any test that sets one number and reads it back. The writer cannot be read back directly, being deliberately unnameable from outside the actor, so what is asserted is the half that would break: a large writer cache must not appear on the reader.

Rejected: *one shared `cache_size`* (above); *a bytes-normalised field* (redefines SQLite's convention for callers who already know it); *defaulting the writer to something larger than −2000* (a behaviour change for every existing caller, made on no measurement — the knob exists so a caller with a profile can make that choice with one); *restating SQLite's default when the field is `None`* (freezes a number this crate does not own).

<a id="d-159"></a>D-159 — `diagnostic_conn` is configured, and `configure` splits into the half a read-only connection can use (0.12.16, W5.5). §4.5, [D-091](s13-decision-register.md#d-091), [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149), [D-158](s13-decision-register.md#d-158), R15. Evidence: `tests/diagnostic_conn_tests.rs`.

[`Database::diagnostic_conn`] opened a connection `SQLITE_OPEN_READ_ONLY` and returned it untouched. Every other connection in the process — the writer, the shared reader, the cadence's — went through `configure()`; this one did not, so it ran with SQLite's defaults. The consequential one is **`busy_timeout`, which defaults to 0**: return `SQLITE_BUSY` immediately, against everyone else's 5 s.

**That put the shortest fuse in the process on the surface with the highest need to succeed.** The method's own rustdoc says it exists to answer questions when the typed path is already suspect, and R15 records it returning `database is locked` under concurrent opens — a mode that "looks like a fact about the database, on the method a caller reaches for when they already doubt the typed answer". A zero busy timeout is a direct contributor to exactly that, and it was never a decision; the connection was simply built somewhere else.

**The split, and what governs it.** `configure_common` runs `busy_timeout` and `cache_size` — per-connection state a reader is subject to as much as a writer. `configure_writable` runs `journal_mode`, `synchronous`, `foreign_keys`, `recursive_triggers` and [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149)'s `analysis_limit`. The line is not tidiness: `journal_mode = WAL` is a change to the *database file*, which a read-only connection cannot make, and the rest govern how writes behave, so a connection that cannot write is not governed by them. `configure` is now the two called in order.

**The two internal readers keep the full configuration.** They are opened from the same read-write `libsql::Database` handle, so the writable pragmas apply to them; dropping them for symmetry with `diagnostic_conn` would be a behaviour change made for tidiness, which is not a reason.

**`analysis_limit` stays out of reach of any test, and `analyze_tests` keeps reading the source.** That test's comment named W5.5 as what would rescue it. It does not: `ANALYZE` is a write, so the bound is in the writable half, and the only connection that has it is still the actor's private one. The test now reads `configure_writable` instead of `configure` and says why the reasoning is unchanged — worth noting, because a scheduled fix that arrives and does not fix the thing it was cited for is how a stale comment becomes a wrong one.

**Re-asserted rather than assumed: it is still read-only.** Running pragmas on a connection is the kind of change that could quietly reopen it read-write, and the whole value of this method over `read_conn()` is that the boundary is the OS's rather than a pragma its holder can turn off. `configuring_it_did_not_make_it_writable` pins both halves, including that `PRAGMA query_only = OFF` does not rescue a write.

Rejected: *running the full `configure` on it* (`journal_mode` cannot be set read-only, and the rest are inert there — it would trade a real omission for pragmas that either fail or do nothing); *leaving `busy_timeout` at 0 and documenting it* (the documentation would say "this connection fails faster than the others, on purpose", which is not true and not wanted); *giving it the writer's cache size* (it mints one connection per call, so it is on the plural side of [D-158](s13-decision-register.md#d-158)'s split); *serialising the opens inside the method* (R15's own rejection, restated in the method's rustdoc, and unrelated to configuration).

<a id="d-160"></a>D-160 — `as_of(ts)` reads `ts` on two clocks; 0.13.0 states that precisely and does not fix it (0.12.17, W5.6). §3.1, [Doctrine VIII](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-viii), [D-076](s13-decision-register.md#d-076), [D-085](s13-decision-register.md#d-085). Fix scheduled: W7.1, 0.14.0.

[Doctrine VIII](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-viii) is unambiguous: "`as_of(ts)` means valid time under current belief and returns exactly that", and "queries that mix time axes say so in their signatures". `TraversalBuilder::as_of` mixes, and does not say. What the single `ts` is compared against:

| half of the answer | column | axis |
|---|---|---|
| topology | `links.valid_from` / `links.valid_to` | **valid time**, under current belief — the contract, met |
| attributes, `AttributeMode::AtTime` | `transaction_log.recorded_at` | **transaction time** — belief as of `ts`, which is `reconstruct`'s axis |
| attributes, `AttributeMode::Current` | none; `concepts WHERE retired = 0` | valid time **now**, belief now |

So `as_of(t).attribute_mode(AtTime)` — the pairing the method's own rustdoc calls "usually what was meant" — answers *"the edges valid at `t`, labelled with what we believed at `t`"*. Both halves are individually defensible. The pairing is two questions under one timestamp, which is the conflation §3.1 names.

**The concrete cost.** A concept's title corrected today, fixing a typo made in 2020: `as_of("2020-06-01")` with `AtTime` returns the **uncorrected** title, because the correction was *recorded* after `ts`. Right answer to "what did we believe in 2020"; wrong answer to "what was true in 2020" — and the second is what the name promises. A smaller mismatch in the same direction: `AtTime` filters on `recorded_at` and on the payload's `retired` flag and never consults the concept's **own valid interval**, so a concept whose validity ended before `ts` still hydrates. The two halves of one answer do not agree about what "existed at `ts`" means.

**What no combination gives today.** *What was true at `t`, as best we now know* — the thing `as_of` is named for, applied to attributes. `Current` reads live rows with no instant at all, `AtTime` reads the transaction-time axis, and there is no valid-time read of concept attributes anywhere, because concepts are versioned in the log rather than as intervals in a `concepts_current` twin. That is why W7.1 is a design decision and not a predicate change, and why it is worth a release of notice.

**Stated, not fixed, and the reason is not caution.** Changing this changes answers callers already depend on — every `AtTime` historical read returns different text afterwards — so it is a break and belongs to a release allowed to make one. Writing the semantics down one release early is what lets W7.1 be reviewed against a stated position instead of argued in the commit that changes it, which is [D-085](s13-decision-register.md#d-085)'s own lesson: that defect survived because the mixed answer was *plausible*, and a plausible wrong answer is only caught by having written down what the right one would be.

This is the third present-tense predicate on a valid-time traversal recorded in [§5.2](s5-modules.md#52-graphbuilderrs--traversal-valid-time-and-attribute-fidelity), beside `c.retired = 0`. They are not one defect — that one is the application axis by design ([§4.1](s4-schema.md#41-concepts-and-per-model-embeddings)), this one is the transaction-time axis by accident — but the shape recurs often enough to be worth naming as a shape.

Rejected: *fixing it in 0.13.0* (a break in a release that promises none, and the replacement semantics deserve a stated position first); *renaming the parameter or the mode* (`AtTime` is accurately named for what it does — the mix is between the two halves, not inside either, so renaming moves the confusion without removing it); *erroring on `as_of` + `AtTime`* (it is the combination most callers want, and refusing the common case to signal an internal inconsistency punishes the caller for the crate's problem); *documenting it only in the rustdoc* (§3.1 raised it against the spec, so the spec is where a reader checks whether it is known).

<a id="d-161"></a>D-161 — The Python constants are exposed flat, and the `chunk_rows` docstrings carry D-143's correction (0.12.18, W6.1). [§14.16](s14-python-bindings.md#1416-parity-with-0130), [D-143](s13-decision-register.md#d-143), [D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146), [D-080](s13-decision-register.md#d-080). Evidence: `tests_py/test_write_path.py`.

`MAX_ARCHIVE_SESSIONS` and `chunk_rows::{EDGES, CONCEPTS, ANNOTATIONS, EMBEDDINGS}` were the last constants a Python caller had to reason against and could not read. `BULK_ATOMIC_WARN_HOLD` set the precedent and the argument: a number the caller is expected to compare something to belongs on the caller's side of the boundary, or the comparison gets hard-coded.

**The archive ceiling matters more than its size suggests, because it is a refusal rather than a clamp** ([D-080](s13-decision-register.md#d-080)). Session count is span divided by window, both caller-supplied, so the arithmetic that decides whether a call will be accepted is arithmetic the caller can do — but only with the constant. Without it the limit is discovered by catching `ArchiveWindowError` after the call, which is the shape D-080 explicitly chose *not* to have.

**Exposing the four ceilings without a sentence would have shipped a stale reading rather than merely leaving it unavailable.** Until 0.12.0 they were chunk sizes and dividing a batch by one gave the transaction count. Since [D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146) the loop times each chunk and sizes the next from its measured hold, so they are the **largest** size ever asked for and a populated database converges below them — the division is now a lower bound at best. The docstring says so at the Rust registration and again in the stub, because editors read stubs first.

**Flat names, not a namespace.** Rust's `chunk_rows` module has no Python shape that keeps the stub honest: a submodule needs its own `.pyi` and its own entry in `test_stubs.py`'s comparison, and a dict loses `Final[int]`, which is what `mypy --strict` reads. `CHUNK_ROWS_EDGES` is uglier than `chunk_rows.EDGES` and is checked by the two gates that already exist.

Rejected: *a `chunk_rows` submodule or dict* (above); *exposing the ceilings without the D-143 note* (ships the 0.11.0 reading under a 0.13.0 version number); *leaving them out because the adaptive loop makes them advisory* (advisory is not the same as unknowable, and the caller's alternative is a hard-coded 90).

<a id="d-162"></a>D-162 — The vector registry is readable from Python, and the dimension raises rather than returning `None` (0.12.19, W6.2). [§14.16](s14-python-bindings.md#1416-parity-with-0130), [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search), [D-037](s13-decision-register.md#d-037), [D-048](s13-decision-register.md#d-048). Evidence: `tests_py/test_vector.py`.

P4.4 shipped the vector *write* surface and the searches, and not the two reads that describe what is there. The result was an asymmetry with a practical cost: *is this model already registered?* had no answer except `register_model(name, dim)` and inspecting whether it raised — a write issued to ask a question, and one that **succeeds** in exactly the case being checked for, so the caller learns the answer only from the absence of an exception.

**Neither method caches, and that is the whole reason they are safe to expose.** [D-037](s13-decision-register.md#d-037) established that this crate keeps no registry of its own: `sqlite_master` is the set of models and `F32_BLOB(n)` in the column type is the declared width. A binding-side cache would have been the natural shortcut and would have reintroduced precisely the drift D-037 removed, one process boundary further out where nothing in the Rust suite could see it.

**`declared_dimension` raises `ModelNotRegisteredError` for an unknown model rather than answering `None`.** Symmetry with Rust is the weaker argument. The stronger one is what the value is used for: allocating a buffer of that many floats. `None` there is a zero-length buffer and a failure that surfaces somewhere else entirely, while an exception surfaces at the question. Membership is what `registered_models()` is for, and it answers with a list that can be empty.

**The shadow-table filter is asserted across the boundary, not assumed.** libSQL's DiskANN creates `*_shadow` tables in `main` that match the `embeddings_%` pattern and have no `embedding` column. The filter is the crate's, but a caller iterating `registered_models()` and calling `declared_dimension()` on each is the loop the two methods invite, and it is the loop that breaks if the filter regresses — so the Python test walks it rather than checking the list against a literal.

Rejected: *returning `dict[str, int]` from one method instead of two* (one query becomes N+1 — `PRAGMA table_info` per model — paid by every caller who only wanted the names); *`declared_dimension` returning `None`* (above); *caching the registry on `PyDatabase`* (above); *exposing the free `macrame::vector::registered_models` as a module function* (it takes a connection, and the connection is the thing [§14.6](s14-python-bindings.md#146-what-the-binding-deliberately-does-not-expose) does not hand out).

<a id="d-163"></a>D-163 — The clock reaches Python as a `_FakeClock` behind a test hook, not as a `Clock` on the supported surface (0.12.20, W6.3). [§14.6](s14-python-bindings.md#146-what-the-binding-deliberately-does-not-expose), [§14.16](s14-python-bindings.md#1416-parity-with-0130), [D-062](s13-decision-register.md#d-062), [D-027](s13-decision-register.md#d-027), defect K. Evidence: `tests_py/test_clock.py`.

[§14.6](s14-python-bindings.md#146-what-the-binding-deliberately-does-not-expose) listed `open_with_clock` among the things the binding deliberately does not expose, and the reason given was sound: a clock injected into a production ledger writes a `recorded_at` axis that no longer records anything. What that entry never weighed is the cost on the other side. `tests_py` could not influence `recorded_at` at all, so the only assertions available to it were *this is a timestamp* and *this one is later than that one* — which is **defect K's exact shape on the half that never received [D-062](s13-decision-register.md#d-062)'s fix**: the axis exists, and the tests cannot see it.

**The resolution is not to reverse that entry but to separate the two things it had merged.** What it objects to is *arbitrary time in a real ledger*. What the suite needs is *a known stamp in a test ledger*.
