Exposing `FakeClock` — a concrete fake — rather than the `Clock` trait keeps the first unreachable: a caller cannot supply their own implementation, so there is no way to attach a clock the crate did not write. The entry is qualified in place rather than deleted, and `test_clock.py` asserts the seam stays private, so a later widening has to make its own argument instead of inheriting this one.

**Underscore-prefixed, on `testing.rs`'s existing terms.** `_FakeClock` and `Database._open_with_clock` are absent from `macrame.__all__` and from the stub's public surface, and are shipped in the wheel rather than behind a Cargo feature — for the reason that module already gives, that a `testing` feature means the wheel which was tested is not the wheel which is published.

**The determinism it buys is partial, and the test suite states the limit rather than working around it.** On a database that already holds rows, `open_tuned` raises the injected clock to the newest stored `recorded_at` before the actor starts ([`Clock::raise_floor`](s5-modules.md#51-connectionrs--the-handle-the-pragmas-and-the-write-actor)); a fake set behind the ledger would otherwise abort the first concept write on `trg_concepts_monotonic_ra`. Tests wanting exact stamps start from an empty file, and one test exists to say so.

**A defect in shared code, found by writing these tests.** `to_duration` also serves `archive_windowed`'s window. A negative `timedelta` reached the caller as a **`TypeError` complaining that a `timedelta` was expected**, because `Duration` cannot represent one: the extraction failed, and the fallback then failed to read a `timedelta` as a float. `timedelta(0)` was the inverse and worse — `Duration` *can* hold zero, so it passed on the `timedelta` arm and was refused on the float arm, one rule with two answers depending on how the caller spelled it. Both now raise `ValueError` naming the sign. The existing refusal test covered `0`, `-1`, `nan` and `inf` and no `timedelta`, which is why neither showed for four releases — a reminder that a test enumerating bad values only covers the *spellings* it enumerates.

Rejected: *a `clock=` keyword on the public `open`* (puts the seam in the signature every caller reads, which is what §14.6 objects to); *exposing the `Clock` trait for Python subclassing* (a `now()` implemented in Python is called from inside the write actor with the GIL released — a re-entrancy problem, and the monotonic contract would be a caller's to honour); *a `testing` Cargo feature* (`testing.rs`'s standing argument: the tested wheel would not be the published one); *asserting `recorded_at` via `FakeClock` in Rust only and leaving Python without* (the status quo, and the thing defect K names).

<a id="d-164"></a>D-164 — `Tuning` crosses to Python as keyword arguments, not as a type (0.12.21, W6.4). [§14.16](s14-python-bindings.md#1416-parity-with-0130), [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155), [D-157](s13-decision-register.md#d-157), [D-158](s13-decision-register.md#d-158). Evidence: `tests_py/test_maintenance.py`.

`Tuning` exists in Rust to solve a problem Python does not have. Its whole purpose is that a growing set of options cannot be added to a function signature without breaking every caller — which is what `..Default::default()` and the three delegating constructors are for. Python has keyword arguments with defaults, so the same set arrives as keywords on `Database.open`: the same information, one fewer name to import, construct and keep in step with the stub.

**What must cross is not the type but the shape of its defaults.** [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155) was learned twice on the Rust side — `cadence: Option<SnapshotCadence>` and `wal_autocheckpoint: Option<u32>` both put a `None` that *disables a mechanism* into a struct deriving `Default`. The failure is easier here, not harder: a keyword's default is invisible at the call site, so a caller who never heard of the knob is exactly the caller it silently applies to. Every knob's absent state therefore leaves the mechanism alone, and a test asserts that omitting the cadence keyword leaves anchoring **on**.

**`wal_autocheckpoint` takes a string for its third state**, not a sentinel integer: `None`, `"disabled"`, or a positive page count, with `0` refused. That is [D-157](s13-decision-register.md#d-157)'s refusal restated where it matters more — SQLite reads `0` as *disable*, and the whole point of the refusal is that a caller whose arithmetic produced zero has a bug. A string cannot be produced by arithmetic, which is the property being bought.

**Two of the three knobs are not observable from Python, and the tests state that instead of working around it.** `wal_autocheckpoint` and `writer_cache_size` are applied to the write connection alone, and no Python caller can reach it; `diagnostic_conn` opens its own and reports SQLite's defaults whatever these are set to. A test asserting `PRAGMA wal_autocheckpoint == 64` there would be *false* — and, worse, would still pass on a release that stopped applying the pragma entirely. It asserts the default it genuinely sees, explains why, and names `tests/wal_policy_tests.rs` as the holder of the positive half. `reader_cache_size` is visible precisely because [D-159](s13-decision-register.md#d-159) put `cache_size` in the read-only half of `configure`.

**`CheckpointReport` crosses as a class rather than a tuple** for the reason it is a struct in Rust: two of its three numbers are both integers and both plausible, and `log_frames` (what is *left*) transposed with `checkpointed_frames` (what was *moved*) is invisible to a type checker and to any test reading one of them.

Rejected: *a `Tuning` `#[pyclass]` mirroring the Rust struct* (a second name and a second stub entry to buy what `**kwargs` already gives); *`wal_autocheckpoint=0` meaning disabled* (D-157, and the argument is stronger at a boundary where the value is more likely to have been computed); *deferring W6.4 to 0.14.0* (§7's own argument: a gap opened in the release that created the feature is the one that never becomes a convention — the constants in W6.1 had gone eight releases for want of being anybody's next task); *asserting the writer-side pragmas through `diagnostic_query`* (it reads a different connection, so the assertion would be false and would keep passing after the feature broke).

<a id="d-165"></a>D-165 — `shadow_step` is not exposed to Python, and the reason is an obligation that does not cross (0.12.22, W6.5). [§14.6](s14-python-bindings.md#146-what-the-binding-deliberately-does-not-expose), [§5.8](s5-modules.md#58-integrity--audit-and-rebuild), [D-091](s13-decision-register.md#d-091), [D-137](s13-decision-register.md#d-137).

The `lib.rs` convention block existed so a contributor deciding whether to expose something would be *standing somewhere that tells them*. `shadow_step` was neither exposed nor named there, which is the state that block was built to prevent: silence reads as "nobody has considered it", and the next person to look reasonably concludes it was an oversight.

**Unlike `raw()`, this is a judgement rather than an invariant.** `raw()` cannot be exposed — it dissolves the single-writer property the latency argument and the overlap guard both rest on. `shadow_step` is public in Rust and safe there. What does not cross is its **obligation**: the `epoch` from `ShadowOutcome::Started` must be handed back to `ShadowStep::Swap`, and a caller who loses it defeats the archive interlock and can swap a stale projection over a live one — *silently*, because the swap itself succeeds.

**In Rust that obligation is carried by two types the caller cannot fabricate.** Across this boundary they become two more `#[pyclass]`es whose only purpose is to be passed back correctly, which converts an enforced obligation into a documented convention. That is a real loss of strength for a method whose failure mode is silent corruption of derived state.

**And the seam has no Python caller.** `rebuild_current_chunked` — the loop, which cannot get the epoch wrong — is exposed. What `shadow_step` adds is the ability to do something *between* steps: pace them against a frame budget, abandon a rebuild that has run long enough, provoke the interlock in a test. None of those is a use anyone has brought to this surface.

**Recorded as revisitable, with the shape of the answer.** If a caller appears, the epoch should cross as an opaque handle rather than as an integer, so the type system carries what the convention would otherwise have to. Waiting is not caution: a type invented for a hypothetical caller is a type nobody can check against a real use.

Rejected: *exposing it with a documented obligation* (above — a convention where there is currently a type); *exposing it with the epoch as an `int`* (the weakest version: a fabricable value in the one place fabrication defeats the interlock); *leaving it unrecorded* (the state W6.5 exists to end — the convention block's whole purpose is that a decision like this is visible where it would be made).

<a id="d-166"></a>D-166 — `analysis_limit` is a constant factor, not a bound; D-149 overstated it and §8 caught it by asking for a measurement (0.12.23, §8 acceptance). [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149), [D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146), [§9](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#9-performance-budgets). Evidence: `examples/analyze_hold.rs`.

[D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149) argued that `PRAGMA analysis_limit = 400` "caps the rows examined per index, making the cost a function of the index count (four) rather than the table size", and that this is "what lets this be scheduled as ordinary low-priority work". The argument was made by construction and never observed. §8's acceptance list asked for it **measured, not asserted** — and the measurement disagrees.

| edges | the crate's own hold | `ANALYZE`, limit off | `ANALYZE`, limit 400 |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10,000 | 5.26 ms | 18.4 ms | 6.01 ms |
| 40,000 | 19.1 ms | 78.6 ms | 19.4 ms |

**The pragma is in force, and this is how that gets established at all.** The connection running `ANALYZE` is the write actor's, which no test can reach — `tests/analyze_tests.rs` reads the source for exactly this reason. So the example builds a ledger through `Database`, records the actor's own hold from `metrics()`, then re-opens the same file as a plain libSQL connection and times `ANALYZE` with the limit off and on. The crate's hold tracks the **capped** arm within a few percent and is nowhere near the uncapped one. Worth 3.1× at 10,000 edges and 4.1× at 40,000.

**What it does not do is remove the table from the equation.** Over that 4× range the capped time grew 3.2×, which is very nearly linear. `analysis_limit` damps the constant; it does not change the shape. D-149's mechanism is real and its stated strength is not.

**The consequence was already visible and nobody had looked.** [`CommandKind::Analyze`](s5-modules.md#510-metricsrs--what-the-actor-holds-the-lock-for) is **not** among the budget-exempt kinds, so a 19 ms hold against a 3 ms budget is counted: `metrics().budget_violations()` returns `("analyze", 1)` after a single call on a 40,000-edge ledger, and always would have. The instrument built for this worked; the reading was never taken. That is the argument for §8's "measured, not asserted" wording in general, and it is worth stating because the same phrasing appears against other items.

**The position this leaves, stated rather than repaired.** `ANALYZE` is one indivisible statement — it cannot be chunked the way [D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146) chunks the bulk paths — so the hold is what it is. It remains low-priority work, preemptible *between* commands and not within one, and `optimize()` is still what `close()` calls because it does nothing when nothing has moved. What changes is that the rustdoc, the constant's documentation and this register now say ~19 ms rather than implying ~constant, so a caller scheduling `analyze()` on a large ledger is deciding with the real number.

Rejected: *lowering `analysis_limit` until the hold fits the budget* (the limit governs statistical quality, and the budget is missed by 6× — the setting that met it would be sampling too little to separate the two `source_id`-leading indices, which is the entire purpose); *making `Analyze` budget-exempt* (it would silence the one signal that caught this, and exemption is for work that is *inherently* unchunkable **and** deliberately accepted — the second half was never argued for this kind); *chunking `ANALYZE`* (there is no such statement — `ANALYZE <index>` per index would be four holds of a quarter the size at best, and the shape stays linear); *deleting D-149* (its decision was right and its mechanism is real; only the strength claim was wrong, so it is corrected in place and cited here).

<a id="d-167"></a>D-167 — A method that loses its rustdoc takes a neighbour's summary with it, and only the appendix was guarded (0.12.24, §8 acceptance item 10). [D-156](s13-decision-register.md#d-156), [§5.8](s5-modules.md#58-integrity--audit-and-rebuild). Evidence: `tests/doc_sync_tests.rs`.

Closing §8's last item meant reading [`Database::checkpoint`]'s rustdoc, and it opened with *"Rebuild `links_current` from `links` and verify zero drift (§5.8)."* That line is `rebuild_current`'s. W5.2 (0.12.13) inserted `checkpoint` immediately above `rebuild_current`, and the one-line doc comment stayed where it was — at the head of the new method's block. **Two methods were wrong from one edit**: `rebuild_current` had no documentation at all, and `checkpoint`'s summary — the single line `cargo doc` shows in the method index — described a different operation.

**The existing gate could not see it.** `every_public_database_method_appears_in_appendix_a` asks whether each method is *named* in Appendix A. `rebuild_current` still was. *Presence in a document* and *presence of a doc comment* are different facts and only the first was guarded, which is a gap of the same shape this file was written to close.

**A second staleness surfaced in the same rustdoc.** It claimed `checkpoint` "runs `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)`". It has run `FULL` and then `TRUNCATE` since the day it shipped — [D-156](s13-decision-register.md#d-156) records why, since a truncating checkpoint reports `busy=0, log=0, checkpointed=0` on success and cannot describe its own work. The decision register had the mechanism right and the method's own documentation did not, which is the direction that reaches a caller.

**The gate is scoped rather than global.** `RUSTFLAGS="-W missing_docs"` reports **266** undocumented public items in this crate, so turning that lint on is a project, not a gate, and not one to land in the commit that found this. `every_public_database_method_has_a_doc_comment` covers the handle's methods — the surface that already has a normative document behind it and where the standard was already "documented", with nothing enforcing it. `raw` and `shadow_step` are exempt, the same two the appendix check exempts and for the same written reasons.

**Verified by injection**, which is this suite's convention for a gate: deleting `rebuild_current`'s doc block again fails the test naming it and its line, and restoring the block passes.

Rejected: *`#![warn(missing_docs)]` crate-wide* (266 items, and it would bury this finding under a mechanical sweep); *checking that the doc's first line mentions the method's own name* (would fail on every legitimately-phrased summary and get relaxed the first time it cried wolf — this file's own stated standard); *fixing the two rustdocs and adding no test* (the failure was invisible for eleven releases precisely because nothing looked).

<a id="d-168"></a>D-168 — `Analyze` stays budget-counted; the exemption cannot be decided while the kind is shared with `optimize()` (0.12.25). [D-166](s13-decision-register.md#d-166), [D-152](s13-decision-register.md#d-152), [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149), [§5.10](s5-modules.md#510-metricsrs--what-the-actor-holds-the-lock-for). **Settled in 0.13.24 by [D-197](s13-decision-register.md#d-197): the kind was split and both halves stayed counted, each for its own reason.** Evidence: `tests/actor_metrics_tests.rs`.

[D-166](s13-decision-register.md#d-166) measured `analyze()` at **19.1 ms on a 40,000-edge ledger** against a 3 ms [`CHUNK_BUDGET`], and established the miss is structural: `ANALYZE` is one indivisible statement, `analysis_limit` damps its cost 3–4× without changing its shape, and `CommandKind::Analyze` is not on the exemption list — so `budget_violations()` names it after every call and always will.

That is the strongest case for an exemption anywhere in this crate, and it is refused for now.

**The exemption table has a `Bound` column and this kind cannot fill it.** `Checkpoint`'s bound is frames accumulated since the last one; `Archive`'s is the session's row count; `WriteBulkAtomic`'s is the batch the caller passed. The honest entry here reads "the size of the table, damped 3–4× by `analysis_limit`" — which is not a bound but the absence of one. A row that cannot state its bound is the table admitting the thing it exists to prevent, and the column would quietly become decorative for every row after it.

**And the kind is two callers wearing one name.** `Analyze` covers [`Database::optimize`] as well as [`Database::analyze`], and `close()` calls `optimize()` unconditionally. Exempting it would silence the **automatic** path — a ~19 ms hold on every handle close of a large ledger, reported nowhere — which is precisely the call nobody chose to make. Every current exemption has in common that a caller asked for the operation explicitly; half of this one has not.

**This is [D-152](s13-decision-register.md#d-152)'s finding arriving from the opposite direction, and that is why it is worth a decision rather than a comment.** There, `Rehydrate` reported as `Archive` and was exempt *by inheritance* — nobody had decided it, it fell out of the borrowed kind, and giving it its own variant would have silently flipped it. Here a shared kind would *launder* an exemption in the other direction: a judgement made about `analyze()` would land on `optimize()` without being made about it. The same defect — an exemption that is a side effect of naming rather than a decision — reachable from both sides of a kind split.

**What ships instead is a documented permanent violation, which is worse noise and better information than an undecided silence.** `analyze_is_not_budget_exempt_and_that_is_deliberate` pins the non-exemption so an exemption is an edit to a test whose message is the argument it must answer. `exempt_from_budget`'s rustdoc carries the reasoning beside `ShadowRebuild`'s, which is the existing precedent for a deliberate non-exemption. And [`ddl::ANALYSIS_LIMIT`](../../src/schema/ddl.rs)'s rustdoc says why lowering the limit is not the fix: it would buy the number by sampling too little to separate the two `source_id`-leading indices, which is the entire purpose of [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149).

W10.5 splits the kind, and only then is the question answerable per caller.

Rejected: *exempting `Analyze` now* (above — it silences `close()`'s `optimize()`, which nobody asked for); *lowering `analysis_limit` until the hold fits* (trades statistical quality, the one thing the pragma governs, for a counter); *splitting the kind inside 0.13.0* (`ALL`, `as_str()`, the exemption table and the Python string surface move together, and 0.13.0's acceptance is closed — a surface change made to tidy a counter is how an acceptance list stops meaning anything); *leaving it undocumented and letting the violation stand unexplained* (the next reader's cheapest fix is lowering `analysis_limit`, and nothing would stop them).

<a id="d-169"></a>D-169 — The feature-off build is a supported configuration that nothing built, and an example broke it for two releases (0.12.26). [D-154](s13-decision-register.md#d-154), [D-140](s13-decision-register.md#d-140), [D-166](s13-decision-register.md#d-166). Evidence: `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `Cargo.toml`.

`examples/analyze_hold.rs` shipped in 0.12.23 as [D-166](s13-decision-register.md#d-166)'s evidence. It calls `db.metrics()`, which is behind the `metrics` feature, and it had no `required-features` line — so `cargo test --no-default-features` and `cargo check --no-default-features --all-targets` both failed to compile from 0.12.23 to 0.12.25, while every gate stayed green.

**Three things had to line up, and each of them is ordinary.** The `[[example]]` convention exists and four other examples follow it; mine simply did not get one. `cargo build --no-default-features` passes, because `cargo build` does not build examples — so the obvious hand-check is the one that cannot see it. And CI runs `cargo clippy --all-targets --features "metrics property-tests"`, which builds every target *with the feature on*.

**The acceptance list is what makes this worth recording rather than just fixing.** §8 item 6 covers the feature-off build, and it was checked at 0.12.22 — one release *before* the example landed. A checklist item verified once and then invalidated by later work in the same wave is a specific hazard of long acceptance passes, and it is not addressed by checking more carefully; it is addressed by the check being a gate rather than an event.

So CI gains `cargo check --no-default-features --all-targets`. **`--all-targets` is the load-bearing half** — the break was in an example, and a plain `cargo check --no-default-features` would still have missed it. `check` rather than `test`, because what this guards is whether the configuration *compiles*; the behaviour the feature gates is already covered by the suite, and running the whole suite twice per push to catch a compile error is not the trade.

Note that `metrics` being a default since [D-154](s13-decision-register.md#d-154) makes this more likely rather than less: with the feature on by default, every ordinary command exercises the on-path, and the off-path is now the one nobody touches by accident.

Rejected: *dropping `--no-default-features` support* (it is the arrangement `ActorMetrics`-as-ZST exists for, and D-154 explicitly kept it: "turn it off with `--no-default-features` if the counters are genuinely not wanted — that path is still built and tested", which was true of the library and not of the examples); *`cargo test --no-default-features` in CI* (a second full suite run to catch compile errors); *`#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` inside the example* (an example that compiles to an empty `main` under a feature is worse than one Cargo declines to build, because it looks like it ran).

<a id="d-170"></a>D-170 — Visibility is enforced by whichever join happens to touch `concepts`, and the vector path never touches it (0.13.1, F-31). [D-051](s13-decision-register.md#d-051), [D-037](s13-decision-register.md#d-037), [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search), [§4.1](s4-schema.md#41-concepts-and-per-model-embeddings). Fix scheduled: W9.3, 0.14.0. Evidence: probe recorded below.

`keyword_search` excludes retired concepts and its rustdoc says why. `search_vector` does not, and cannot as written: it joins `vector_top_k(…)` to the per-model embeddings table and to nothing else, so `retired` is not a column in scope. `HybridSearch::execute` fuses the two lists with no post-fusion pass, so the vector arm carries a retired concept through the fusion and out to the caller.

Probed on a two-concept fixture — one retired and nearest the query, one visible and farther:

| surface | a retired concept | a concept expired in valid time |
|---|---|---|
| `search_vector` | **returned** | **returned** |
| `keyword_search` | excluded | **returned** |
| `hybrid_search` | **returned**, via the vector arm | **returned** |
| `search_filtered` | excluded | **returned** |

**`search_filtered` is safe by composition, not by decision.** Its candidates come from `TraversalBuilder::execute_ids`, whose SQL closes with `WHERE c.retired = 0`, and both strategy arms intersect against that set. Nothing in `FilteredVectorSearch` knows what `retired` is. A change that made the traversal return ids without hydrating would leak through the third surface too, and no test would say so.

**This is defect Z one module over, and the generalisation is the finding.** Wave 1 fixed a retired neighbour leaking through `links_current` into an `EdgeRef`, because `links_current` carries edges to retired concepts and only `hydrate` filters them ([§5.4](s5-modules.md#54-graphsubgraphrs-and-graphalgorithmsrs--native-in-memory-analytics)). The instance was fixed; the rule — *a path that never joins `concepts` enforces no visibility at all* — was not written down, and the vector path is precisely the path that never joins `concepts`. Per-instance fixes to a structural rule is the pattern [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059) and [D-064](s13-decision-register.md#d-064) already establish for the planner; this is its visibility counterpart.

**Recorded as a defect in a shipped release, which is the part worth stating.** 0.13.0 tagged with this present, and `tests_py/test_vector.py::test_a_retired_concept_is_not_a_search_result` exists — it exercises `keyword_search` only. A test that names the general property and covers one surface is how a gap survives review: the suite reads as though the property is held.

Rejected: *filtering in each caller* (three places to keep in step, and it is what produced the finding — `keyword_search` did its half and nothing propagated the obligation); *filtering at `upsert_embeddings`, so retired concepts hold no vector* (retirement is reversible and the embedding is expensive to recompute; it would also make un-retiring lossy); *documenting it as intended* (the intent was stated in `keyword_search`'s own rustdoc — *"a soft-deleted concept is not a search result"* — as a claim about search, not about one arm of it).

<a id="d-171"></a>D-171 — Search reads no instant, so `as_of` and retrieval cannot be combined (0.13.1, F-32). [D-170](s13-decision-register.md#d-170), [D-160](s13-decision-register.md#d-160), [§5.2](s5-modules.md#52-graphbuilderrs--traversal-valid-time-and-attribute-fidelity), [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search). Fix scheduled: W9.4, 0.14.0.

No search surface bounds a concept's own valid interval — the right-hand column of D-170's table. Uniform, therefore a gap rather than an inconsistency, and graded below F-31 for that reason despite being wider.

**The consequence is specific.** `TraversalBuilder::as_of(t)` bounds *edges* by `l.valid_from <= t AND t < l.valid_to`, so the graph half of `search_filtered` reads at an instant while the vector half reads today's corpus, and the concepts the traversal reaches are filtered on `retired` but never on their own valid interval. A caller asking *what was near this query, among what was true last March* cannot ask it, and the surface that looks closest to answering silently mixes March's topology with today's corpus.

**Why it is scheduled after W7.1 rather than before.** The instant search needs is the same parameter the road-to-1.0 plan's §3.1 splits `as_of` into. Adding a time parameter to search while the crate still has one word for two axes would ship a third spelling of the confusion W7.1 exists to end.

**And the sign is the trap for W9.5, recorded here so it is not discovered in review.** `search_vector` returns a distance and its results ascend; `hybrid_search` returns a fused score and its results descend, and both are pinned by tests. A decay factor in (0, 1] multiplied into a similarity penalises age correctly; the same factor multiplied into a distance makes stale rows look *nearer*. Decay is defined against similarity, and whatever the distance surface does about that is a test rather than a comment.

Rejected: *making the instant mandatory* (every existing caller breaks, and "now" is the overwhelmingly common question); *defaulting the instant to the current time* ([D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155) — an absent knob must leave the mechanism alone, and a default instant would silently start excluding rows with a future `valid_from`); *filtering valid time and leaving `retired` alone, or the reverse* (they are one join, and splitting them means paying for the join twice).

<a id="d-172"></a>D-172 — A branch is transaction time with a tree order, not a third axis and not a per-row tag (0.13.1, W12). [§4](s4-schema.md#4-schema), [§4.3](s4-schema.md#43-the-transaction-log), [D-029](s13-decision-register.md#d-029), [D-007](s13-decision-register.md#d-007). Scheduled: W12, 0.15.0.

Branching enters the plan for a named use — an agentic harness whose conversation tree forks at every turn — and the first decision is what the word means, because the alternative is a schema column that means nothing precise.

**Valid time answers *when was this true*. Transaction time answers *when did we come to believe it*, and a bitemporal ledger assumes belief arrives in one sequence.** A branch is a fork in that sequence: two divergent lineages of belief over the same valid-time domain. So branching does not add an axis; it changes the order on one, from a line to a tree.

The framing is worth this much space because everything else falls out of it and that is the test of whether it is right. Valid time is untouched. Doctrine III is *strengthened* — a branch never rewrites its parent, it appends elsewhere, which makes branching the most append-only operation in the crate. Doctrine II gains a clause rather than an axis: two clocks and a lineage. And `recorded_at` stays a total order within a branch while becoming a partial order across them, ordered by `(ancestry, recorded_at)`.

**The storage model follows Gancarski et al.'s Database Version model (1999)**, which the 2026 survey identifies as the only branching architecture in 54 papers: temporal state attached to complete database versions rather than to individual tuples, with logical versions decoupled from physical storage. A `branches` table, a nullable `parent_id`, a `forked_at`, and `branch_id` defaulting to `main` on the ledger tables. Reads resolve an ancestry chain; storage is shared; **a fork is one row and nothing else**, which is the requirement the use case imposes and the reason this design is chosen over the others.

Rejected: *a `branch_id` interpreted per row, with a fork copying inherited facts* (every index gains a lead column and every fork is O(rows) — and Rost et al. (2022) and Hou et al. (2023) both measure the analogous choice for time, timestamps as ordinary properties, and find query cost growing with history); *one file per branch, via `ATTACH`* (clean isolation, no shared storage, and cross-branch `diff` becomes a cross-database join); *treating a branch as a third temporal axis* (it has no duration, no interval and no ordering relation with the other two — it is a lineage, and calling it a clock would put a column in the schema that no predicate could honestly use); *shipping merge with it* (§19 — Doctrine III has no neutral answer to which assertion supersedes which).

<a id="d-173"></a>D-173 — An R\*Tree over these timestamps can bound a candidate set and can never be the authority (0.13.1, F-33). [D-029](s13-decision-register.md#d-029), [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059). Fix scheduled: W10.6, 0.14.0.

The standard prescription for bitemporal interval queries is a 2-D spatial index, and the 2026 survey's §4.2 is the usual citation for it. Recorded before anyone spends a wave on the port, because **the arithmetic blocks the drop-in version here**.

SQLite's `rtree` module stores coordinates as **32-bit floats**; `rtree_i32` stores **32-bit integers**. This crate's timestamps are fixed-width ISO-8601 text at microsecond resolution ([D-029](s13-decision-register.md#d-029)), chosen so that lexicographic order equals chronological order. A 64-bit microsecond epoch fits neither coordinate type: float32 carries a 24-bit mantissa, so epoch-seconds near 1.8 × 10⁹ quantise to roughly 128-second buckets, and int32 at second resolution overflows in 2038.

So an R\*Tree here is a **coarse bounding-box pre-filter with a mandatory exact recheck against the canonical text columns**. That is a legitimate design and is how the module is meant to be used. It is not the index the citation implies, and a version that forgets the recheck returns *nearly* the right answer — the worst failure available to a ledger, and the one least likely to be caught by a test that only asserts non-empty results.

**The survey's own evidence is more equivocal than the citation, and the equivocation is the useful half.** Kaufmann et al. (2015)'s Bitemporal Timeline Index explicitly declines to model the two dimensions spatially and keeps one 1-D index per temporal domain, outperforming the spatial structures on selection, join and aggregation; the survey's Fig. 9 concludes that no single index is optimal across bitemporal workloads. So the literature says *measure*, not *use an R\*Tree* — and W10.6 measures against W2.4's populated, analysed fixture before anything is built.

Rejected: *adding the R\*Tree now, ahead of the measurement* (an index added because a paper said B-trees are insufficient for a query nobody has timed — and this crate has paid three times for indexes chosen on structure rather than selectivity); *storing a parallel integer epoch column to feed the index* (a second representation of the one value [D-029](s13-decision-register.md#d-029) made canonical, and the two would drift under exactly the retroactive corrections the ledger exists to record); *dismissing spatial indexing outright* (it may still win as a pre-filter on a workload with many narrow intervals, which is what W10.6 is for).

<a id="d-174"></a>D-174 — `as_of` becomes `as_of_valid` and `as_of_recorded`, and the transaction-time half is **built** rather than renamed (0.13.2, W7.1, §3.1, F-32). [D-160](s13-decision-register.md#d-160), [D-085](s13-decision-register.md#d-085), [D-029](s13-decision-register.md#d-029), [D-039](s13-decision-register.md#d-039), [Doctrine VIII](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-viii), [§5.2](s5-modules.md#52-graphbuilderrs--traversal-valid-time-and-attribute-fidelity), [§5.6](s5-modules.md#56-temporalas_ofrs--valid-time-queries-and-attribute-hydration), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors). Closes the oldest open High in the plan.

[D-160](s13-decision-register.md#d-160) wrote down, without changing, what `as_of(ts)` actually did: bound `links.valid_from`/`valid_to` on the **valid-time** axis and `transaction_log.recorded_at` on the **transaction-time** axis, so one word asked two questions and the answer to neither was the one its name promised. This is the change that document existed to make reviewable.

**The rename is the small half.** `as_of_valid(v)` is *what was true*; `as_of_recorded(r)` is *what we believed*; neither defaults to the other, and setting both is the cell — *what did we believe at `r` about what was true at `v`* — that Jensen and Snodgrass's BCDM defines a bitemporal database as answering and that no surface in this crate could express.

**The large half is that `as_of_recorded` had nowhere to read from.** The 0.13.1 sharpening of W7.1 is what made this unavoidable: splitting a parameter is half done if the halves still route to two mechanisms that cannot compose. Valid time was reachable from a traversal and transaction time only from `reconstruct`, which folds the *whole* state and cannot be walked. So the walk now takes its edges from one of two relations exposing the same six columns — `links_current` under current belief, or a fold of `transaction_log` bounded at `r`. `links_current` is a projection of current belief, so the row that stood before a correction is not in it; the fold recovers it, and is exact because links are strictly append-only. Every assertion and every correction is an `INSERT`, logged `'I'` under `entity_id = source|target|type|valid_from`, so the last log row per entity at or before `r` *is* what `links_current` held at `r`. The fold partitions on `entity_id` alone, sound only because `table_name = 'links'` is in the `WHERE` — the discriminator applied by the filter rather than by the partition, which is why defect W's concept/link collision cannot arise here and why the four folds in `replay.rs` do it the other way.

**It reads the hot log and refuses rather than guessing.** `archive` removes superseded rows, and a traversal takes a `Connection`, not an archive path. [`DbError::RecordedInstantUnreachable`](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors) names the instant and names `reconstruct`, which does take the path. Conservative by one bit — the test is whether anything was *ever* archived, not whether this instant survived it, because the cutoff is not recorded hot-side ([D-132](s13-decision-register.md#d-132) refused that marker outright). It therefore refuses instants a fold might have answered. The alternative is answering some of them from a partial fold, which returns *nearly* the right topology, and on a ledger that is the worst failure available.

**The second, smaller mismatch [D-160](s13-decision-register.md#d-160) recorded closes with the same change.** `AtTime` consulted the payload's `retired` flag and never the concept's own valid interval, so a concept whose validity had ended still hydrated. It is now bounded against the interval **the payload recorded**, not the live row's — reading the live interval would answer today's belief about validity wearing the past's title, which is the conflation this entry exists to end.

**`AttributeMode` is kept, and the reason is worth stating because the split nearly dissolves it.** With two instants, `AtTime` no longer needs to mean *which clock* — the instants say that — so the enum reduces to *do the attributes follow the instants, or come from now, or not at all*. `Current` on a historical traversal stays a legitimate, stated request ([D-085](s13-decision-register.md#d-085)), `Omit` stays meaningful where the mode is a parameter, and `AttributeModeUnstated` now fires on **either** instant rather than on `as_of` alone — asking the question on one axis and not the other would be the same gap in a new place.

Rejected: *keeping `as_of` as a deprecated alias* (it has no honest target — it meant valid time for topology and transaction time for attributes, which is not a point in the new parameter space, so an alias would have to pick one and silently change the other); *making `as_of_recorded` affect attributes only* (cheap, and it leaves the topology permanently on current belief — the composition failure the 0.13.1 sharpening said must be fixed or refused by name); *refusing the pairing by name instead of building it* (permitted by W7.1's text, and it would leave W10.6 with nothing to measure and §14's acceptance item 11 unsatisfiable); *materialising a per-instant projection* (a second `links_current` per instant asked about, which is a cache keyed on a continuous domain); *storing the fold's cost as unknown* (§5.2 states it: window function plus a `json_extract` per column, materialised once and joined per hop, and W10.6 times it).

<a id="d-175"></a>D-175 — `load_subgraph_with` accepted a historical traversal and read the present, and the fix is one producer for the parameter list (0.13.2, W7.1, F-35). [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174), [D-073](s13-decision-register.md#d-073), [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030), [D-035](s13-decision-register.md#d-035), [§5.4](s5-modules.md#54-graphsubgraphrs-and-graphalgorithmsrs--native-in-memory-analytics). Found while splitting `as_of`; fixed in the same change.

The loader bound `now_ts` at `?3`, where `TraversalBuilder::execute_ids` bound the traversal's own instant. Both read the same CTE — [T0.1](s13-decision-register.md#d-073) had already deduplicated the SQL after D-073 found the two copies drifted — but the *parameters* were still built twice, agreed by a comment in each file saying the other pushes in the same order. So a builder carrying an instant, passed here, silently produced a live subgraph.

**This is F-31's shape in a third place and the generalisation is the same one.** A surface that accepts a qualifier and does not apply it is worse than one that refuses it, because the caller has evidence they asked. Defect Z was a retired neighbour leaking through `links_current`; F-31 was `search_vector` never joining `concepts`; this is an instant reaching a builder and not the query. Each was fixed as an instance. What is fixed structurally here is narrower and real: `bind_params` is now the only producer of the parameter list and `edge_type_base` the only computer of the placeholder offset, so the two call sites cannot bind different things at `?3` again — the same move D-039 made for the SQL, applied to what fills it.

**The Python binding could not reach the instants at all**, which is why `load_subgraph` gains `as_of_valid` and `as_of_recorded` in the same release rather than the next one — W6's finding that a binding gap opened in the release that created the feature never becomes a convention.

Rejected: *documenting the loader as live-only* (it takes a `TraversalBuilder`, and a parameter that is accepted and discarded is not documentation's problem to solve); *asserting in the loader that the traversal carries no instant* (turns a silent wrong answer into a refusal of a reasonable request, and the request is now served); *leaving `attribute_mode` ignored here too* (kept ignored, and stated: a `Subgraph` feeds six algorithms, none of which reads a title — that is a narrower gap and a deliberate one).

<a id="d-176"></a>D-176 — the one write path that returned the engine error raw, and the reason it looked safe was true of the guards and silent about the foreign key (0.13.3, W7.2, §3.6). [D-041](s13-decision-register.md#d-041), [D-058](s13-decision-register.md#d-058), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors).

`write_annotations_atomic` returned `DbError::Engine(e)` where every other write in the crate — `write_edges_atomic`, `write_concepts_atomic`, the archive delete — routes its failure through `error::classify`. §3.6 called this a bypass and predicted that *the same input produces different stored state depending on which entry point was used*. **That prediction is wrong and the finding is still real**, which is worth separating because the fix follows from the second half and not the first. There is no second entry point: `write_analytics_annotations` is the only way in, the chunk writer is the only thing it calls, and stored state was never at issue. What differed was what the caller was *told*.

**The reasoning that let it stand is the interesting part, because it was correct.** `analytics_annotations` carries no triggers at all — that is precisely why it is the cheapest bulk table and why its chunk ceiling is the largest of the four ([D-058](s13-decision-register.md#d-058)) — so no `RAISE(ABORT)` can fire on it, `abort_kind` would return `NotAGuard`, and `classify` would hand back the error it was given. Routing through it was, on that reading, a no-op. The reading is sound about guards and silent about the foreign key onto `concepts`, which SQLite enforces itself and which the table has carried since it was added. A guard vocabulary that covers every guard is not the same as one that covers every failure, and nothing in the code said which of the two the omission was resting on.

**The failure it hid is the one a caller actually causes.** An annotation naming a concept that is not there — an algorithm run against a graph read before an archive, or against ids that were never concepts — arrived as `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed` and nothing else, out of a rolled-back chunk of up to `chunk_rows::ANNOTATIONS` rows. The caller was told that one of fifty thousand annotations named something missing, and not which. It is now `DbError::NotFound(concept_id)`.

**The detector matches the result code, not the message, and that is a departure worth naming.** [`abort_kind`](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors) is documented as *the only place in the crate that matches on engine error text*, and its rustdoc names the cost it accepts: an upstream wording change silently degrades an unknown number of typed errors into opaque ones. It accepts that cost because SQLite flattens every `RAISE(ABORT)` into one generic constraint failure and leaves the message as the only discriminator. A foreign key is not in that position — libSQL reports statement failures through `SqliteFailure(extended_error_code(…), …)`, so `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_FOREIGNKEY` is available as a number. This classification therefore depends on nothing anyone can rephrase, and the doctrine `abort_kind` states is unweakened: text is still matched in exactly one place, and this is not a second one.

The **extended** code, not the primary. `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT` (19) also covers the CHECK, PRIMARY KEY and NOT NULL failures this same table can produce, and the canonical-timestamp CHECK on `computed_at` is one of them. Matching the primary code would report a malformed timestamp — a different bug, with a different fix, and one the actor's own clock reading cannot cause — as a missing concept. A test asserts that discrimination rather than asserting the constant, because the constant is not the claim.

Rejected: *a dedicated `AnnotationConceptMissing { concept_id, label }` variant* (the label identifies a row in the caller's own batch, which the caller already holds; the concept id is the fact the database knows, and `NotFound` is the §7 variant that carries exactly it — a new variant would cost a §7 reproduction, a Python class, a sample arm and a stub for a field nobody reads); *carrying `label` on `WriteOp::Annotation` anyway* (no arm reads it); *adding `AbortKind::ForeignKey`* (`abort_kind` is the text matcher, and putting a code-matched condition inside it makes the one function whose whole purpose is to isolate text matching into a mixed classifier); *extending the foreign-key arm to the edge and concept paths* (`links` and `concepts` have foreign keys too, and their failures deserve the same treatment — but that is a change to paths with their own guards and their own tests, and W7.2's scope is this one; recorded here so the next reader knows it was seen and left).

<a id="d-177"></a>D-177 — the actor's `Result` could not fail, and the branch that could had no test (0.13.4, W7.3, §3.5). [D-014](s13-decision-register.md#d-014), [D-153](s13-decision-register.md#d-153), [§5.1.4](s5-modules.md#514-the-actor-loop).

`run_writer_actor` returned `Result<()>` and its body contained one `return`-shaped expression: `Ok(())`. W7.3 offered two ways out — give it a failure it can actually report, or change the signature — and the first was examined before the second was taken.

**There is nothing for an actor-level `Err` to carry.** The loop has two exits: `LoopCtl::Break` from `HighPriCommand::Shutdown`, and the `else` arm when both channels are closed. Every candidate failure resolves into one of two places that already handle it. Per-command errors — a guard abort, a rolled-back chunk, a bad timestamp — go back on that command's own responder, which is the only place a caller can act on them and is [D-014](s13-decision-register.md#d-014)'s rule that a failed assertion must not kill the writer. A dropped responder is explicitly not an actor error and has been since `Turn::answer` was written: a caller who wrapped a write in `tokio::time::timeout` is doing something the write surface documents, and the command committed regardless. That leaves the `else` exit, which is reachable only after `Database` was dropped without `close()` — and dropping the handle drops the `JoinHandle` with it, so nothing is left to observe a status. An `Err` on that path would be unreadable by construction.

**What the unfireable branch cost was the readable one.** `close()` matched `Ok(res) => res?` beside `Err(e) => WriterStopped`, which reads as two failure paths. One of them was structural noise; the other — the actor **panicked**, so every write the caller believes is queued is going nowhere — was the entire content of the Wave 4.2 fix that replaced `let _ = handle.await`, and it had no test. A `Result` that is always `Ok` does not merely fail to inform; it stands where a reviewer expects the failure handling to be and reports that it is present.

So the mapping is now a named function, `writer_exit`, and the test hands it a **real** `JoinError` from a panicking task rather than a constructed one — `JoinError` has no public constructor, and a hand-built value would pin the mapping against something tokio does not produce. Cancellation is folded in with panics deliberately: `JoinError` distinguishes them and nothing in the crate aborts this task, so a cancelled writer means something outside the crate stopped it, which is not the gentler condition it might read as.

Rejected: *a `WriterExit { Shutdown, ChannelsClosed }` enum in place of the `Result`* (nothing discriminates it — the same defect in a new shape, and `close()` can only ever see `Shutdown` because it holds both senders); *detecting a poisoned connection at the end of each turn* (a real hazard, and the cheap probes for it are writes, so the detector would take the write lock on every turn to guard against a bug no path currently has); *making the `else` exit an error* (it is the documented consequence of `Drop` without `close()`, already warned about, and nobody is holding the handle to read it); *keeping the `Result` and adding `#[allow]`-style commentary* (the finding is that the signature makes a claim the body does not, and a comment saying so leaves the claim in place).

<a id="d-178"></a>D-178 — a stamp from the future is refused at open, because it is the one bad value in the ledger that spreads (0.13.5, W7.4, §3.4). [D-027](s13-decision-register.md#d-027), [D-062](s13-decision-register.md#d-062), [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155), [§5.1.2](s5-modules.md#512-handle-shape-and-the-clock-contract), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors).

`recorded_at_floor` took `MAX(recorded_at)` with no upper bound and the clock was raised to it, so one row stamped in 2087 became this process's floor and every stamp it issued landed at or after it. Those stamps are written, so the next open reads the same floor back from rows this crate itself produced. **That is what separates this from every other bad value in the file: it is inherited, and inheriting it manufactures more of it.** A corrupt timestamp, by contrast, cannot be parsed and therefore cannot be inherited — which is why [D-027](s13-decision-register.md#d-027)'s `warn!`-and-no-floor stays exactly as it is, and why extending it to this case would be answering the worse problem more gently.

**The refusal is at open, and open is the only place it can be.** `recorded_at` is never caller-supplied — every one is `clock.now()` from inside the write actor — so there is no write to reject. What there is, is a moment where a stamp the crate could not have issued becomes one the crate does issue, and that moment is `recorded_at_floor`. Refusing there costs nothing that was working and stops the propagation at its single entry point.

`FutureStampPolicy` is shaped like [`WalCheckpointPolicy`](s13-decision-register.md#d-155) — `Default | Tolerance(Duration) | Allow` — rather than as an `Option<Duration>`, for exactly [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155)'s reason: this guards an invariant, and an `Option` whose `None` means *off* switches it off for every caller who never heard of it. `Allow` exists because a database the crate refuses cannot otherwise be reached *by the crate that refuses it*, and inspecting a file requires opening it. It is documented as a reading path and not a repair: every write made under it inherits the floor, which is the condition being refused.

**The tolerance is a day, and generous on purpose.** What is being caught is a stamp no clock could have issued — a machine set to the wrong year, an import that carried foreign timestamps, a `FakeClock` fixture that escaped its test — and those are out by years. A tight bound would instead catch a timezone-confused host or a file carried across a daylight boundary by a tool that mishandled it, and refusing to open on that is worse than the disease. `Tolerance(Duration::ZERO)` is available where the host's time is known good.

**W7.4's second clause — "refuse writes stamped beyond a tolerance" — is answered by the first, and the other reading of it is rejected with its reason.** Read as *the writes already in the file*, which is what `recorded_at_floor` being the item's only cited location implies, it is the same fix: the floor absorbs them silently today, and now it does not. Read as *a per-`now()` skew guard*, it was examined and is not shipped. `Clock::now` returns a `String` and cannot refuse, so the check would have to sit in the actor's ten-odd `clock.now()` sites or in a trait method every arm then has to branch on. That is the mechanical objection. The substantive one is that a per-write guard needs a reference the wall clock cannot move, which means a monotonic anchor taken at open — and a monotonic clock does not advance across suspend on any platform this crate targets, so a laptop resumed after a week is indistinguishable from a host skewed forward by a week. Clamping there would write a stamp saying the transaction happened last Tuesday when it happened now, which is a lie about the transaction-time axis rather than a defence of it. Detecting the skew and *stopping* has the same problem in the other direction. Recorded here so the next reader knows the clause was read both ways.

**What a caller with a poisoned file actually does, since the guard makes the condition visible without making it repairable.** `Allow` opens it and does not fix it; `repair_recorded_at` is rejected below and stays rejected. The route back is to open under `Allow`, read the ledger, and write it into a **new** database, which has no floor and therefore stamps at the wall clock. Valid time survives that unchanged, because `valid_from` and `valid_to` are caller-supplied; transaction time does not, and cannot — the new file honestly records that these beliefs were entered when they were re-entered, and the old transaction-time axis is gone rather than corrected. That is the whole cost, and it is the same cost Doctrine II imposes on every other attempt to edit history: the ledger will not tell you it believed something at a time it did not.

This also names the one case the crate is itself the producer. A host clock that jumps forward *while a database is open* has no guard in front of it — `SystemClock` issues the jumped stamps, the actor writes them, and the next open refuses a file that was fine when it was opened. The refusal is still the right behaviour and the alternative is worse (see the per-write reading above), but it is worth being explicit that W7.4 makes that condition diagnosable rather than impossible.

Rejected: *clamping the floor to the wall clock instead of refusing* (every subsequent concept write then aborts on `trg_concepts_monotonic_ra`, so a diagnosable open becomes an undiagnosable failure on every write); *`Option<Duration>` with `None` meaning unbounded* ([D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155)); *refusing without an escape hatch* (the file becomes unreachable by the tool that reads this schema, and the crate is the only thing that understands it); *a tolerance measured in minutes* (catches clock hygiene, not the failure); *a `repair_recorded_at` method under `Allow`* (`recorded_at` is the transaction-time axis — a method that rewrites it is a method that rewrites what the database says it believed, and Doctrine II exists to make that impossible).

<a id="d-179"></a>D-179 — the within-batch overlap guard sorts and sweeps, and adjacent pairs are not enough to do it with (0.13.6, W7.5). [D-060](s13-decision-register.md#d-060), [D-014](s13-decision-register.md#d-014), [D-081](s13-decision-register.md#d-081), [§2.6](../Macrame%20Codebase%20Review%20v0.12.0.md), [§5.1](s5-modules.md#51-connectionrs--the-handle-the-pragmas-and-the-write-actor), [§9](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#9-performance-budgets).

`reject_overlaps_within` compared every pair in the batch. [D-081](s13-decision-register.md#d-081) rejected fixing that — "defensible and a larger change than this item" — and documented the cost instead, which was the right call for a documentation item and left the cost where it was. §2.6 lists it as Low for the same reason: it is measured, warned about, and deliberate. What makes it worth doing now is that the warning it earned is the mitigation for a hold of **18.1 seconds**, and a mitigation is not a fix. The batch is uncapped by contract ([D-014](s13-decision-register.md#d-014)), so the quadratic term has no ceiling other than the caller's patience.

**Sort by `(source, target, edge_type, valid_from)`, then sweep.** Same answer, `n log n`, and the expensive comparison — `Interval::new` twice and `overlaps` — now runs once per row rather than once per pair. Measured on the batch that cost 18.1 s: **2.2 s**, which is the fan-out shape's own cost. The two shapes are within 15% of each other at every size from 100 to 20,000 rows, so the shape term left [D-081](s13-decision-register.md#d-081)'s model at the same time, and `estimated_bulk_hold` was re-fitted to what remains — `rows · (7.4 µs + 7.24 µs · ⌊log₂ rows⌋)`, the insert path's own growth against a table the batch is loading.

**Adjacent pairs are not sufficient, and the roadmap's phrasing says they are.** "Sort by `(source, target, edge_type, valid_from)` and check adjacent pairs" is the standard construction, and its proof needs every pair to be eligible for comparison. Two are not. Identical `valid_from` is re-assertion — the last writer wins by `seq_id`, exactly as across batches — and two open intervals belong to `trg_links_single_open`, which fires during the insert with a more specific error. Skip an adjacent pair for either reason and a real overlap hides behind it: `[5,20)`, `[5,6)`, `[7,8)` has the first pair skipped for equal `valid_from` and the second separated by a clean gap, while `[5,20)` and `[7,8)` overlap plainly. The pairwise version found it; a literal adjacent-pair rewrite does not. So the sweep carries the widest `valid_to` reached so far rather than looking back one step, and carries a second one restricted to closed intervals — an open predecessor is ineligible for an open candidate and eligible for a closed one, and a single maximum cannot answer both. Equal `valid_from` is handled by advancing in runs: a run is checked against the maxima and only then folded into them, so its members never see each other.

**The refitted model's blind spot is stated rather than averaged away.** The `⌊log₂ rows⌋` term stands for the depth of a structure the batch is loading, and `estimated_bulk_hold(&[EdgeAssertion])` never sees the table. That is exact for the bulk import this warns about and optimistic for a small batch appended to an already-large one. The flat per-row model had the same blind spot; what changed is that the quadratic term used to dominate and hide it.

**The report now names the earlier interval as the existing one.** Within a batch neither is older in transaction time — they arrive under one stamp — so the pairwise version's answer was the caller's argument order, which carries no meaning. Valid-time order is the only ordering the two intervals have, and it is the one the words *existing* and *proposed* will be read as carrying.

Rejected: the literal adjacent-pair rewrite (wrong, for the reason above, and wrong in the direction that matters — it accepts an overlap rather than inventing one); sorting the batch in place (it is borrowed, and its order is the order the rows are written in, which is the caller's to choose — indices are sorted instead, for the reason [D-081](s13-decision-register.md#d-081)'s estimator groups rather than sorts); dropping the equal-`valid_from` exemption to make the sweep simpler (that is a change to [D-060](s13-decision-register.md#d-060)'s semantics wearing a performance argument, and re-assertion within a batch is Doctrine III's ordinary case); capping the batch so the quadratic term is bounded instead (breaks [D-014](s13-decision-register.md#d-014), and was already rejected at [D-081](s13-decision-register.md#d-081)); keeping the shape term in `estimated_bulk_hold` as a safety margin (a model that predicts a 7× spread that no longer exists warns loudly about a batch that is fine, which is how a warning gets filtered out — [D-081](s13-decision-register.md#d-081)'s own argument for the 250 ms threshold).

<a id="d-180"></a>D-180 — one overlap error, two guards, and only one of them was talking about the database (0.13.7). [D-060](s13-decision-register.md#d-060), [D-075](s13-decision-register.md#d-075), [D-179](s13-decision-register.md#d-179), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors), [§14](s14-python-bindings.md#14-python-bindings).

`DbError::OverlappingInterval` read *"edge a -> b (KNOWS) already holds [x, y), which overlaps the asserted [p, q)"*, and two guards raise it. `reject_overlapping_interval` compares an assertion against committed rows, where "already holds" is true. `reject_overlaps_within` compares a batch against itself **before the transaction opens** ([D-060](s13-decision-register.md#d-060)), where it is false twice over: the interval named is not in the database, and because the batch is refused whole it never will be. A caller whose bulk import failed was told to look for a row that was never written.

**Found by auditing [D-179](s13-decision-register.md#d-179) rather than by the change itself.** The wording predates it — this has been wrong since [D-060](s13-decision-register.md#d-060) added the within-batch guard — but D-179 is what made it load-bearing. That entry reasons at length about which of the two intervals the words *existing* and *proposed* should name, having taken on trust that the surrounding sentence was true. A decision about how a message will be read is worth no more than the message.

`Overlap::within_batch` carries the distinction and `Overlap::provenance` renders it, closing the message with *is already recorded* or *this same batch also asserts*. The variant is already boxed ([D-075](s13-decision-register.md#d-075)), so a `bool` costs `DbError` nothing. It crosses to Python as a `within_batch` attribute, since a caller writing `except OverlappingIntervalError` wants to branch on this rather than parse the message.

Rejected: *rewording to something true of both cases* ("conflicts with" would stop being wrong and would also stop being useful — which side of the write the other interval is on is the first thing a caller needs, and it is the thing only the crate knows); *a second `DbError` variant* (the two guards enforce one invariant with one remedy, and splitting the variant would break every `matches!(err, OverlappingInterval { .. })` call site to express something a field expresses); *leaving it, since [§2.6](../Macrame%20Codebase%20Review%20v0.12.0.md) and the reviews never raised it* (nothing in the suite asserted the message text either, which is why six releases passed — the absence of a complaint about a diagnostic is not evidence the diagnostic works); *making `provenance` return a `String` so the two messages could differ in shape* (this is a `Display` path, and a closing clause is enough to carry the fact).

<a id="d-181"></a>D-181 — the chunk loop knew how much it had written and told nobody (0.13.8, W7.6). [§4.2](../Macrame%20Road%20to%201.0.md), [D-011](s13-decision-register.md#d-011), [D-058](s13-decision-register.md#d-058), [D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors), [§14](s14-python-bindings.md#14-python-bindings).

`bulk_import`, `write_concepts`, `upsert_embeddings` and `write_analytics_annotations` are atomic *per chunk* and not overall ([D-011](s13-decision-register.md#d-011)) — that is the trade that buys the latency bound, and it is documented on every one of them. Its consequence was not: an import that failed at row 19,000 of 20,000 returned a bare `DbError` and the caller learned only that it failed. The count was three lines away. `low_chunked` accumulates `written` because it needs it to size the next chunk ([D-146](s13-decision-register.md#d-146)), and then threw it away at each of three `?`s. A caller who retried the batch rewrote 19,000 rows; one who skipped it lost the tail; one who wanted to resume had to go and count the table, which is only correct if nothing else has written since.

**A new error type rather than a new variant, and rather than a wrapper.** `BulkInterrupted { written, cause }` is what those four now return. The variant was rejected because it moves the cause behind a `matches!` arm nobody has written yet — every existing `matches!(err, DbError::SingleOpenViolation { .. })` would compile and stop matching, which is the worst available outcome for a change whose whole point is that callers should see more. As a distinct *type* it breaks the same call sites loudly, at compile time, and the fix is one field access. `From<BulkInterrupted> for DbError` is deliberately lossy and deliberately explicit: `?` into a function returning `Result<T>` still works and drops the count, which puts the decision to ignore it at the call site instead of inside the crate. Four call sites in this repo's own suite were the first thing the change found, and each of them wanted `.cause`.

**Cancellation is the boundary that already existed, given a name.** `CancelToken` is an `Arc<AtomicBool>` read once per chunk, and `BulkControl` carries it alongside an optional progress callback. Nothing rolls back and no transaction is interrupted: the loop stops sending, and the committed prefix is the same kind of prefix a failed chunk leaves. That equivalence is what makes cancellation free of new failure modes — it adds a reason to stop and no new state to be in. The check sits *after* the "no rows left" test, so a token tripped once the last chunk has committed reports success; a race between two of the caller's threads should not decide whether a finished import counts as one.

`BulkProgress` reports `written`, `total`, `rows` and `held` after every commit. `held` is the actor's own measurement — the number the chunk controller steers on — so a caller watching progress is watching the same thing [D-058](s13-decision-register.md#d-058) tuned against, not a wall-clock approximation of it.

On the Python side the exception class is still chosen by the cause, so `except NotFoundError` catches a missing concept whether it came from `upsert_concept` or from the middle of a 20,000-row `write_concepts`; what is added is a `written` attribute on the instance and a `BulkCancelledError`. `progress=` and `cancel=` are keyword-only on all four methods. Two Python-specific facts fall out of the binding and are worth stating: the call holds the GIL released for its whole run, so the cancelling thread must be a *different* thread — which is what a `CancelToken` is for and what a plain `bool` flag could not safely be — and a progress callback that raises cancels the write and propagates, because a progress bar whose failure silently reads as "the import finished" is worse than one that stops the import.

Rejected: *`tokio_util::sync::CancellationToken`* (a dependency, and its `cancelled()` future is for `select!` arms — this loop wants a synchronous read between transactions, which is one atomic load); *passing `&CancelToken` and `&dyn Fn` as two arguments* (four methods, two optional arguments each, and the next thing wanted here is a rate limit — `BulkControl` is where that goes); *checking the token inside the chunk transaction* (a partially applied chunk is exactly what per-chunk atomicity exists to prevent, and abandoning a `BEGIN` to honour a flag would trade a documented boundary for an undocumented one); *reporting a cancellation as `Ok(written)`* (indistinguishable from success, and the caller cannot tell a stopped import from a short one without knowing the batch size, which the error already carries as `total` on the progress side); *making `written` a field on every `DbError`* (only four paths can commit part of a batch, and `written: 0` everywhere else would be a claim about atomicity that no other path makes — the Python side went the other way at [D-182](s13-decision-register.md#d-182), because the argument rests on a type system Python does not have).

<a id="d-182"></a>D-182 — Rust says it in the type system, and Python has no such type (0.13.9). [D-181](s13-decision-register.md#d-181), [§14](s14-python-bindings.md#14-python-bindings).

[D-181](s13-decision-register.md#d-181) put the partial count on the four chunked writes and nowhere else, which is exactly right in Rust: those four return `BulkResult<usize>` and every other method returns `Result<T>`, so a caller cannot *reach* `written` on a path that has none, and cannot fail to notice one that does. The compiler carries the distinction. Python got the same asymmetry without the mechanism that made it safe — `written` existed on some exception instances and not others, discoverable only by raising one.

**What that costs is paid in an `except` block, which is the worst place available.** A handler written as `except MacrameError as e: log.error("failed after %s rows", e.written)` works against a `write_concepts` failure and raises `AttributeError` against an `assert_edge` failure — inside the handler, replacing the diagnostic the caller was trying to record with one about the recording. Nothing type-checks it, because a `.pyi` declaring the attribute on `BulkCancelledError` alone is correct and the checker has no way to know which exception a `try` block will produce.

**So `written` is on every Macrame exception, `int | None`, and `None` is a statement rather than a placeholder.** An `int` means this path applies in chunks and this many rows are committed and staying committed. `None` means partial application is not a concept here — a read, a single write, `write_bulk_atomic`, a closed handle — and the caller's real question, *is this database in a partially written state*, is answered by `e.written is not None` before any count is looked at.

**`0` was rejected for the not-applicable case because `0` is already taken.** On a chunked path it means *the first chunk failed*, which is a real outcome with a real remedy: the batch is untouched, retry it whole. Using the same value for *this path has no chunks* would make `e.written == 0` ambiguous between two different execution models, and the ambiguity would land on the one caller who wrote the check correctly.

**`getattr(e, "written", None)` was rejected as the alternative** because it collapses the same two facts one step earlier. It cannot distinguish *the attribute is missing* from *this path cannot partially apply*, so a caller using it is guessing, and every caller has to write the same guess.

**It is set centrally, at the two places an exception is constructed** — `raise()`, which every mapped `DbError` passes through, and `closed_error()`, the one exception that does not come from a `DbError`. Not in the arms: an arm-by-arm default is a rule that holds until the next variant is added, and the gate that would catch that omission cannot see it. `test_exception_attributes_match_the_mapping_layer` reads `errors.rs` by splitting on `raise::<Class,` and collecting each arm's `setattr` calls, so a `setattr` *outside* every arm is invisible to it by construction — which is why the 0.13.8 `to_py_bulk` could set `written` with no stub declaring it and nothing went red. `test_every_raised_error_carries_written` is the replacement, and it runs against raised instances rather than against source text.

This does not reopen [D-181](s13-decision-register.md#d-181)'s rejection of *making `written` a field on every `DbError`*. That argument is about the Rust surface, where the type system already separates the two kinds of path and a universal field would erase the separation; here there is no separation to erase, and the choice is between a universal attribute and an `AttributeError`.

Rejected: *a `PartialWriteError` base class the four paths raise under* (pyo3 fixes an exception's bases at compile time, and the class is chosen by the cause — a `NotFoundError` from a chunked path and one from `upsert_concept` are the same class by design, which is [D-181](s13-decision-register.md#d-181)'s decision and a good one); *a mixin injected at raise time* (same problem, and it makes `type(e)` unstable); *changing anything in the Rust core* (the asymmetry is correct there, and this is a binding-layer problem in full).

<a id="d-183"></a>D-183 — the axes were split and the error that exists to explain them still said `as_of` (0.13.10, W7.7). [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174), [D-085](s13-decision-register.md#d-085), [D-160](s13-decision-register.md#d-160), [D-180](s13-decision-register.md#d-180), [Doctrine VIII](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-viii), [§5.2](s5-modules.md#52-graphbuilderrs--traversal-valid-time-and-attribute-fidelity), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors), [§14](s14-python-bindings.md#14-python-bindings).

W7.7 asks for a decision recording *which axis `as_of` now means, and what a caller who wants the other one calls instead*. [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174) answered that in 0.13.2 and `TraversalBuilder::as_of_valid`'s own rustdoc answers it at length. Writing the entry meant going to check that the crate says the same thing, and in one place it did not.

**`DbError::AttributeModeUnstated` is the error a caller meets when they ask about the past, and it told them to call a method that no longer exists.** The variant carried `as_of: String`; the message rendered *"traversal as_of(2020-06-01) did not state an attribute mode"*. `as_of` was removed in 0.13.2. A caller who reads that message and goes looking for the call it names finds nothing, and the search that follows is for a method the crate deliberately deleted.

**The field was fed by an `.or()`, which is the more interesting half.** `resolved_mode` did `self.as_of_valid.or(self.as_of_recorded)` and put the winner in a field named after neither. So a caller who set only `as_of_recorded` was handed their transaction-time instant under the name `as_of`, and a caller who set **both** was told about the valid-time one alone — the bitemporal cell reported as half of itself. The comment defending it said a caller who set both "is asking a question the message reads correctly either way", which is true of the remedy and false of the report: the remedy is the same keyword either way, and that is exactly why the message can afford to name what was actually asked.

**This is [D-180](s13-decision-register.md#d-180) again, in the place D-180's own reasoning predicts.** That entry found `OverlappingInterval` claiming a row "already holds" an interval when the batch guard raised it, and recorded the general form: *a decision about how a message will be read is worth no more than the message*. Both were found the same way — by auditing a documentation item rather than by any test, because nothing in either suite asserted the sentence. Two releases apart, in the two errors whose whole content is an explanation.

**`StatedInstants` rather than two `Option<String>` fields.** `Valid`, `Recorded`, `Both` — three cases and never zero, because this error is constructed *because* an instant was set, so a fourth case carrying neither is a state no call site can reach. [D-177](s13-decision-register.md#d-177) is the precedent and the reason: it removed a `Result` whose `Err` could not be produced, on the ground that an unreachable branch stands where a reader expects real handling. `StatedInstants::new` returns `Option<Self>` and the `None` is the ordinary live traversal, resolved before the error type is involved at all.

Its `Display` renders `as_of_valid(…)`, `as_of_recorded(…)`, or `as_of_valid(…) with as_of_recorded(…)` — **named as the calls that produce them**, which is what makes the message answer W7.7's second clause in place: a caller who wanted the other axis can read what to call from the error they already have. Same shape as [`Overlap::provenance`](s13-decision-register.md#d-180), a method on a `Display` path rather than a second `#[error]` string, because one variant gets one format string.

Python gets `as_of_valid` and `as_of_recorded` in place of `as_of`, both always present and the unstated one `None`. A binding whose keyword is `as_of_recorded=` and whose exception attribute is `as_of` is the same conflation surviving one layer down, and Python has no compiler to notice it — the crate had already removed the keyword, so keeping the attribute would have left the wrong name in the only place a caller reads it.

**The rustdoc gate had been red since 0.13.2 and nothing said so.** `cargo doc` warned `unresolved link to Self::as_of` at [builder.rs:115](../../src/graph/builder.rs:115) — a fourth piece of the same residue, in the doc comment for `attribute_mode`, which is the method the error tells callers to use. CI runs that step under `RUSTDOCFLAGS: -D warnings`, so it was a failing build, not a warning; three more had accumulated beside it from 0.13.2 and 0.13.5 (a link to a re-export not in scope, and two public items linking to `pub(crate)` ones). All four are fixed here. The gate works; what did not work is that nobody ran it locally between the release that broke it and this one.

**The number in the plan is not the number here.** W7.7 says "Record D-155", written before D-155 was taken by 0.12.12's `Tuning` decision. Renumbering that entry would break every cross-reference pointing at it, several of which — [D-178](s13-decision-register.md#d-178) among them — cite it for its shape argument. So the item's content is recorded at the next free number and the plan's reference is stale, which is worth one sentence in both places rather than a silent mismatch.

Rejected: *`as_of_valid: Option<String>, as_of_recorded: Option<String>` as two fields* (representable-but-unreachable fourth state, for the [D-177](s13-decision-register.md#d-177) reason above); *keeping `as_of` as a deprecated alias on the Python exception* (the alias is the conflation, and an attribute nothing sets in Rust cannot be checked by the stub gate); *rewording the message without changing the field* (the field name is what the binding exposes, so half the defect would cross to Python untouched); *leaving it and recording only the prose entry W7.7 literally asks for* (an entry stating "`as_of` is gone, call `as_of_valid` or `as_of_recorded`" published in the same release as an error telling callers to call `as_of` is the documentation failure this project keeps finding, written down on purpose).

<a id="d-184"></a>D-184 — off the write connection was not off the thread (0.13.11, W8.1). §2.4, [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots), [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi), [D-053](s13-decision-register.md#d-053), [D-177](s13-decision-register.md#d-177). Evidence: the unit tests in `src/temporal/snapshot.rs`.
