**The design note that covered this was about a different axis, and read as though it covered both.** [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots) says snapshotting runs on the read side and never touches the write connection, "because the actor's loop must stay short enough that §5.1.5's latency bound holds" — true, deliberate, and about the *connection*. Nothing said where the CPU went. bincode over the whole state, zstd over the result, a `write_all` and an `fsync` are synchronous to the last instruction, and they ran on whichever tokio worker was polling `write_final`: the same pool the write actor's own I/O is scheduled on. §9 budgets that at two seconds for 100K edges. Two seconds of a worker polling nothing is not a smaller problem than two seconds of the actor's loop; it is the same problem one layer out, and the sentence that made the first one visible is what made the second one invisible.

**The read side is the half that was easier to miss.** `snapshot_anchor` runs on the async path of every composing `reconstruct` and decompresses and deserializes a whole `MaterializedState` to decide whether the file it just read is the one it wanted. That is the load half of §2.4, and it has no shutdown to hide behind — it is on the ordinary read path.

**One hop each, and the read side offloads the scan rather than the file.** The write is `save_snapshot` and the retention pass that follows it in a single `spawn_blocking`, because they run back to back and a scheduling point between them is a point nobody is waiting at; order and error behaviour are exactly what they were inline, including that a failed prune is still returned as an error after the snapshot is already on disk. The read is the whole directory scan in a single hop, because the loop is sequential by construction — it stops at the first snapshot at or before `ts` — so a hop per candidate would add scheduling to a path whose common case, `reconstruct(now)`, reads exactly one file.

**The two ends disagree about a lost thread, deliberately.** A `spawn_blocking` task cannot be cancelled once it has started, so the only way to see a `JoinError` is that the closure panicked. On the write side that closure is the file `close()` promises to have written, so it becomes `ReplayCorrupt` carrying the anchor — the class every other failure of `save_snapshot` already reports, which is the point: nobody handling *the snapshot did not get written* should need a second arm for *it failed by panicking*. On the read side the answer is `None` and fold from genesis, which is what the scan already does for an incompatible file and for an unreadable one. That is not leniency; it is what a snapshot **is** under [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi) — derivative and disposable, so ignoring one costs a slower reconstruction and never a wrong one.

**It also closes a panic path that had nothing to do with scheduling.** Inline, a panic in the loader unwound through `reconstruct` and took the caller's task with it: one corrupt file could stop a process that had a correct answer available the whole time. Offloaded, `spawn_blocking` catches it and the fold proceeds. W8.4 fuzzes the loader for exactly those panics, and this is what happens to the ones it has not found yet — which is worth having *before* the fuzzer rather than after it.

**What did not move, and why.** `newest_anchor_on_disk` stays on the caller's worker: one `read_dir` over a directory retention holds to about `RETAIN + RETAIN_DAYS` entries, no file opened, nothing decompressed, run once when the cadence task starts. `spawn_blocking` costs a queue and possibly a thread, and paying it to move a bounded directory listing would be applying the fix by habit rather than by argument.

**The number in the plan is stale again, and this is the second time.** W8.5 says "Record D-156", which 0.12.13's `checkpoint()` decision has held since long before this wave — the same collision [D-183](s13-decision-register.md#d-183) hit with D-155. The remaining "Record D-15x" items in the plan should be read as *record a decision*, not as an instruction about its number.

Rejected: *`tokio::task::block_in_place`* (it converts the current worker rather than moving the work, so the pool is still one worker down for the duration, and it panics outside a multi-threaded runtime — the Python binding builds one, but an embedder with a current-thread runtime would get a panic where they used to get a slow call; the unit test runs on a current-thread runtime precisely so a later rewrite reaching for it fails there); *`tokio::fs`* (moves the file I/O and leaves bincode and zstd, which are the expensive half, and does its own `spawn_blocking` per call — several hops to solve less of the problem); *making `save_snapshot` and `load_snapshot` `async`* (they are also what benches, tools and the test suite call from no runtime at all, so this would break every sync caller to spare two internal ones a wrapper); *an `async` twin of each* (a public name whose only difference is which thread it runs on, for a wrapper a caller can write in one line, when the sync function's own docs can say so instead).

<a id="d-185"></a>D-185 — snapshot format v3: two declared lengths, one checksum, and a third name for damage (0.13.12, W8.2). §3.3, [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors), [§14](s14-python-bindings.md#14-python-bindings), [D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043), [D-054](s13-decision-register.md#d-054), [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069), [D-184](s13-decision-register.md#d-184), [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi). Evidence: `tests/replay_snapshot_tests.rs`, unit tests in `src/temporal/snapshot.rs` and `src/util/crc32.rs`.

**The failure §3.3 names is not the one the phrase "unbounded loader" suggests.** `bincode`'s default limit is `Infinite`, which sounds like one catastrophic `Vec::with_capacity` off a forged length prefix. serde's cautious-capacity already blunts that: a sequence's hint is clamped, and the vector grows as elements actually arrive. What is left is slower and worse to reason about — a deserializer working through a corrupt stream, allocating as it goes, until the stream or the machine runs out. On the file the crash path reaches for first, that is a failure with no diagnosis attached: whatever it eventually reports is a symptom several layers below the fact that the bytes were wrong.

**So the fix is to know the bytes are wrong before deserializing any of them.** v3's header carries `payload_len` (the compressed byte count), `plain_len` (what it decompresses to) and a CRC-32 over the first 34 header bytes **and** the payload. The reader checks framing, then integrity, then size, in that order, because that is cheapest-first and because each answers a different question:

* `payload_len` against the bytes present catches truncation and appended junk without hashing anything. The atomic rename ([D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043)) was supposed to make a torn file impossible, and covers a crash during *this* process's write; nothing covers a filesystem that lost the tail of a file it had acknowledged.
* The checksum runs **before zstd is handed a byte.** This is the check that closes §3.3 — a corrupt stream is refused *as* a corrupt stream.
* `plain_len` is enforced *during* decompression rather than after it: the reader is bounded to `plain_len + 1` bytes, so a frame that expands further stops one byte over the line instead of expanding to whatever it wants. Checking afterwards would mean doing the work first, which is the work being defended against.

**The lengths sit under the checksum, and that ordering is the design.** A declared bound a reader cannot verify is a suggestion. Putting both lengths inside the checksummed range means the reader has already established they are the numbers this crate wrote before it acts on them — which is why there is no arbitrary cap beside them. A constant like *refuse anything over 4 GiB* would defend against a header the checksum has already vouched for, at the price of a number that either never fires or one day refuses a legitimate snapshot the same build wrote.

**What the checksum is not.** CRC-32 is detection, not authentication: anyone able to write a forged snapshot into the directory can compute the field, and can equally overwrite the database file beside it. That is the threat model — a local embedded ledger, where write access to the snapshot directory is write access to everything — and it is why the bounds after the checksum still have to hold on their own. The unit tests forge a *valid* checksum on purpose, so what they exercise is the reader's behaviour after integrity has been satisfied by something that computed it deliberately.

**Damage needed its own name, and this is [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069) in a place D-069 did not reach.** Every failure of `load_snapshot` was `ReplayCorrupt { seq: 0 }` — the variant meaning *the ledger is damaged*, which is the worst thing this library can say about a database, carrying a sequence number that cannot exist because `AUTOINCREMENT` starts at 1. That is the identical placeholder D-069 removed from `InvalidTimestamp` two years of releases ago; it survived here because nothing had cause to read it. There are three subjects and now three names: [`SnapshotIncompatible`](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors) (*another build wrote this*, ordinary after an upgrade), `ReplayCorrupt` (*the ledger is damaged*), and `SnapshotCorrupt` (*the cache is damaged and the ledger is not*). The third response is the cheap one — delete the file, pay a slower reconstruction — and under [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi) it is the only one available, because a snapshot is derivative and disposable. Nothing on the ordinary path raises it to a caller: the scan skips a damaged file exactly as it skips an incompatible one ([D-184](s13-decision-register.md#d-184)), so a caller who sees it either called `load_snapshot` directly or has no usable snapshot at all.

**CRC-32 is forty lines here rather than a dependency.** The polynomial has not changed since 1975, the table is generated at compile time so there is nothing transcribed to get wrong, and the standard check vector is asserted. `crc32fast` is a good crate and would be faster on long inputs, but the input is one payload per save and per load on a path already dominated by zstd and bincode, and a dependency has to be audited and kept compatible for as long as the project lives. The measurement that would justify it does not exist.

**§5.5's own description of the header had been wrong since 0.5.5, and this is the third time.** The paragraph said "ten bytes precede the payload" and its diagram stopped at offset 10; v2 had taken the header to eighteen in 0.5.5, and the [D-054](s13-decision-register.md#d-054) note further down the same section says so — "eighteen bytes per file" — without the paragraph above it ever being brought into line. A correction filed *beside* the passage it corrects rather than *into* it is [D-183](s13-decision-register.md#d-183)'s shape and [D-180](s13-decision-register.md#d-180)'s before it, found the same way: by writing the next version of a passage and checking the current one against the code.

**v2 files are refused, and that is the whole migration.** A v2 snapshot meets a v3 build as `SnapshotIncompatible`, the scan skips it and the fold runs from the log. This is precisely the case the versioned container was built for in [D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043); there is nothing to migrate because there is nothing in a snapshot that is not also in the ledger.

Rejected: *a checksum over the plaintext instead of the payload* (it would have to be verified after decompressing, which is after the work the bound exists to prevent); *an absolute size cap beside the declared length* (a magic number defending against a value the checksum has already vouched for, which either never fires or one day refuses a real snapshot — see above); *reusing `SnapshotIncompatible` for damage* (its meaning is *upgrade normally*, and a checksum failure is not normal); *reusing `ReplayCorrupt`* (it names the ledger, which is exactly the wrong subject, and it is the reason this entry exists); *`crc32fast`* (a dependency for forty lines on a path dominated by zstd); *xxhash or blake3* (stronger than the threat model, and neither is authentication either); *leaving `load_snapshot` unbounded and fuzzing it in W8.4* (a fuzzer finds panics in a loader that has no stated bounds; giving it bounds first is what makes its findings mean something).

<a id="d-186"></a>D-186 — the rename was atomic and the directory entry was not durable, and Windows gets a stated no-op rather than a silent one (0.13.13, W8.3). §3.7, [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots), [§5.1.7](s5-modules.md#517-shutdown-and-snapshot-coordination), [D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043), [D-184](s13-decision-register.md#d-184), [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185), [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi). Evidence: unit tests in `src/temporal/snapshot.rs`; CI's three-OS matrix.

**Two words that get used as one.** `save_snapshot` writes a temporary file, `fsync`s it, and renames it into place, and [D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043) is right that the rename is atomic: a crash across it leaves the old snapshot or the new one and never a splice. Atomic answers *which file is at the name*. It does not answer *whether the name is on the disk*, because the name lives in the directory and the `fsync` above covered the file. The window's shape is unhelpful in a specific way: the payload is already durable, so what a power loss takes is the pointer — a perfectly good snapshot sitting under a name nothing looks for, while the newest name that does resolve is an older file.

**The honest size of it: a slower start, never a wrong answer.** Losing the rename leaves the previous anchor and the whole log, and folding from the previous anchor is correct by construction. [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi) is what makes that true — a snapshot is derivative and disposable, and nothing in this document's correctness rests on any particular one existing. So this entry is not about a data-loss bug, and an entry that implied otherwise would be worth less than one that says so. What it *is* about is [§5.1.7](s5-modules.md#517-shutdown-and-snapshot-coordination): `close()` promises a final anchor, [§6](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#6-data-flows) tests for it, and the crash on which that anchor is the difference between a fast restart and a full fold is exactly the crash the guarantee was silently missing.

**A failed flush is reported, not logged.** The file is at its final name when `sync_directory` runs, so the error does not mean the snapshot is absent or damaged — which is why it is worth stating what it does mean: this function cannot promise the name outlives a power loss. It is the same class the file's own `sync_all` failure already returns, and the caller that hears it is `close()`, which is the caller whose promise it is. Swallowing it would leave the durability guarantee true on paper and conditional in fact, and *conditional in fact* is what this item was opened to remove.

**Deletions are deliberately left alone**, and the asymmetry is the argument rather than an oversight: a deletion undone by a crash resurrects a valid snapshot, which the next retention pass deletes again; a creation undone by a crash loses the anchor. Durability is owed to the name that has to be there, not to the name that has to be gone. `cleanup_expired_snapshots` says so where the deletions happen.

**Windows gets a no-op with a name, and the name is the deliverable.** There is no directory `fsync` on Windows. `FILE_FLAG_BACKUP_SEMANTICS` opens a directory *handle*, but `FlushFileBuffers` requires write access the handle does not carry; the call that covers directory metadata takes a volume handle, requires administrative privileges, and flushes every open file on the volume, which is not something a library may do to the machine hosting it. NTFS journals metadata operations, so a completed rename is recovered by the filesystem rather than by anything arranged here — a genuinely weaker statement, resting on NTFS or ReFS and saying nothing about FAT32 or an SMB share. All of that lives in the `#[cfg(not(unix))]` arm's own documentation, because a durability gap closed on one platform and silently open on another is worse than one that is open on both: the second is a known defect and the first is a false belief.

**These are the crate's first `#[cfg]` platform arms, which changes what "the tests pass" means locally.** The `unix` body does not compile on the machine this was written on, so it was checked by temporarily building it under `#[cfg(windows)]` — it compiles and passes clippy — and it is CI's three-OS matrix that runs it for real on two of three legs. Worth recording because the next platform arm will face the same thing and the answer should not have to be rediscovered.

**What is testable here is not durability.** No test in this repository can assert that a name survives a power loss; asserting it would need the power cut. What the two new tests *do* assert is the pair of things that can silently be false: that a directory descriptor on this platform accepts `fsync` at all — POSIX permits `EINVAL`, and if this platform took it up every `save_snapshot` would now fail — and that the non-unix arm really is inert, by handing it a path that does not exist and requiring `Ok`. A third pins the publish step as a rename by requiring no `.tmp` to survive a successful save. The untested part is named rather than implied.

Carried, not closed: `save_snapshot` still reports its I/O failures as `ReplayCorrupt`, the variant meaning *the ledger is damaged*, which [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185) had just finished arguing about on the read side. The write side is not the same defect — it carries a real `seq_anchor` rather than [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069)'s impossible `0` — so it is a naming question and not a lie, and it is left where it is rather than widening a durability item into an error-surface one.

Rejected: *logging a failed flush and returning `Ok`* (makes the guarantee conditional in exactly the way the item exists to end, and hides it from `close()`, the one caller who promised it); *syncing the directory before the rename as well* (durability is owed to the name a reader will look for, and the temporary name is one no reader may touch); *syncing after retention deletions* (above); *a volume flush on Windows* (administrative privileges, and it flushes every open file on the volume); *`FlushFileBuffers` on a `FILE_FLAG_BACKUP_SEMANTICS` handle* (needs write access a directory handle does not grant); *making the flush a `Tuning` knob* (a durability guarantee that can be switched off by default is the gap with a name on it); *skipping the item because [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi) makes a lost snapshot survivable* (true, and not the point — the survivable outcome is the one `close()` promised not to hand back).

<a id="d-187"></a>D-187 — the loader is fuzzed at three layers, because W8.2 made it a format a fuzzer cannot get into (0.13.14, W8.4). §5.4, §3.3, [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy), [D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043), [D-184](s13-decision-register.md#d-184), [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185), [D-186](s13-decision-register.md#d-186). Evidence: `fuzz/`, the `.github/workflows/ci.yml` fuzz job, unit tests in `src/temporal/snapshot.rs`.

**The obvious way to do this item produces a fuzzer that tests the checksum four hundred million times.** [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185) put a CRC-32 over 34 header bytes and the entire payload, checked before zstd sees a byte. That is exactly the shape coverage-guided mutation cannot solve — a 32-bit value that has to agree with everything before it — so a target that hands raw input to `load_snapshot` clears the four-byte magic in seconds and then spends the rest of its budget at step 2 forever. It would look like a working fuzz job. It would never reach `zstd` or `bincode`, which are the two components §3.3 named and the two where a defect would live. **W8.2 made this format fuzz-hostile on purpose, and W8.4's real content is getting past its own defences.**

**Three targets, one per layer of the reader, with the inner two handed a container built around their input.** `snapshot_container` takes a candidate file and explores framing: magic, versions, the declared lengths, the arithmetic on numbers read off a disk. `snapshot_payload` takes **plaintext** and has the harness compress, header and checksum it, so every case lands on `MaterializedState`'s deserializer with W8.2's `with_limit` in place. `snapshot_frame` takes eight little-endian bytes of declared plaintext length and an arbitrary payload, checksummed correctly, so every case lands on zstd and the `take(plain_len + 1)` bound. The split of the third target's input is deliberate rather than incidental: it lets the mutator move the declared length and the bytes it describes independently, which is the pair the bound has to hold across.

**The third target is the one the checksum cannot help with, and it is the reason the bound exists.** A decompression bomb's checksum is *correct* — the file is exactly what its author intended — so integrity says nothing about it and the only thing between the reader and the frame's full expansion is a number the header declares. This is precisely the case [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185) argued the bounds after the checksum must survive on their own, and it is where **"never an allocation storm"** stops being rhetorical. It is asserted by libFuzzer's `-rss_limit_mb` and `-malloc_limit_mb`, set deliberately low in the CI job: a storm becomes a reported finding with the offending input attached, which is what a fuzzer is *for* and what no assertion in a test can do.

**Building the container for the fuzzer is the same move the W8.2 unit tests already make, and it needs the same defence.** Those tests forge a *valid* checksum on purpose, because what is under test is the reader's behaviour once integrity has been satisfied by something that computed it deliberately. A harness that recomputes the header is that argument extended: it is not weakening the format, it is refusing to spend a fuzzing budget re-proving that CRC-32 works.

**Seeds are generated, never committed.** A directory of valid v3 snapshots in git is correct exactly until `SNAP_FORMAT_VERSION` next moves — after which every seed is refused at the version check, the session effectively starts from an empty corpus, and nothing says so, because the run's only visible output is a coverage number no one has a baseline for. `fuzz/src/bin/seed.rs` builds the corpus from the build under test in about a second, and derives the two inner corpora by reading the payload and the plaintext back **out of** a container `save_snapshot` genuinely wrote, so no part of the corpus is a second description of the writer. That is [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030) and [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047)'s rule applied to a test fixture.

**The doors are feature-gated and hidden, and the crate's public surface is unchanged.** `--features fuzzing` compiles a `#[doc(hidden)] pub mod fuzzing` with four functions; without it the module does not exist. It also required splitting `load_snapshot` into *read the file* and `parse_snapshot`, *understand the bytes* — worth doing on its own terms, since a parser reachable only through a filesystem path is a parser that can only be tested through the filesystem, at a syscall round trip per case on the one axis where cases per second is the whole measure of the tool.

**`fuzz/` is not a workspace member, and the empty `[workspace]` in its manifest is what keeps it out.** [D-098](s13-decision-register.md#d-098)'s property — every member is built by any command not scoped to a package — is pinned by `tests/packaging_tests.rs`, and a fuzz crate that joined would put libFuzzer and a nightly-only dependency into every `cargo test` at the root. The same manifest also keeps the directory out of the `.crate` tarball, by the mechanism that already keeps `bindings/` out of it.

**What is not closed: this runs in CI and nowhere else, and the deterministic half is the answer to that.** `cargo-fuzz` needs nightly and libFuzzer and does not support Windows, which is the platform this crate is developed on — so the targets here were type-checked locally and have never been executed locally, and CI's ubuntu job is the first place they run for real. A gate that only exists after a push is a gate nobody watches fail. So the same properties are asserted **exhaustively** in `src/temporal/snapshot.rs`, in every `cargo test` on every platform: every single-bit flip of a real snapshot is refused, every truncation and every extension is refused, every mutation of the plaintext behind a correct checksum is answered rather than survived, and a 64 MiB decompression bomb with a valid checksum stops 1,025 bytes in. Exhaustive is affordable because the fixture is small, and where it is affordable it beats sampling: the bit-flip test names the bit and the byte when it fails. Verified by mutation — disabling the checksum comparison turns it red at *bit 0 of byte 10*, which is inside `taken_at_micros`, a field nothing but the checksum guards.

**§8 had been using the word "fuzz" for something else since 0.4.0.** *"Fuzz tests attack the temporal machinery specifically"*, it says, and then describes a differential oracle over generated histories — which is a property test, is a good one, and is `doctrine_property_tests`. Nothing in the repository generated unstructured input for anything. The distinction is not pedantry: a property test explores the space its generator was written to explore and finds defects in shapes someone thought of; a fuzzer's whole value is the shapes nobody did. Named here because it is the fourth documentation finding of this wave and the same shape as the others — a claim that reads as true, in a section nobody had cause to check against the file list.

Rejected: *one target over `load_snapshot` alone* (the honest-looking option, and it would fuzz the CRC and nothing behind it — above); *committing a seed corpus* (goes stale silently on the next format bump); *`arbitrary` and a derived `MaterializedState`* (generates values the deserializer would have accepted anyway; the bytes are the input under test, not the struct); *a counting global allocator in an integration test to assert the allocation bound* (duplicates `-malloc_limit_mb`, needs its own single-test binary and a `#[global_allocator]`, and would still measure only the inputs someone wrote down); *making `parse_snapshot` public rather than feature-gated* (a real API commitment for a harness door, and [Appendix A](appendices.md#appendix-a--public-api-normative) is normative about that surface); *running the fuzzers to convergence in CI* (a session has no natural end, and a PR gate that never finishes is a gate that gets deleted — 90 seconds a target against a seeded corpus catches shallow regressions, and a long session out of band is what finds the rest); *adding `fuzz/` to the workspace* (puts nightly-only dependencies into every root command).

<a id="d-188"></a>D-188 — W8's decisions were all recorded and W8's own two additions were not, so the crate-layout tree becomes a gate (0.13.15, W8.5). [§3](s0-s3-foundations.md#3-crate-layout), [§5.11](s5-modules.md#511-util--ids-clocks-timestamps-checksums-and-engine-ceilings), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy), [D-184](s13-decision-register.md#d-184), [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185), [D-186](s13-decision-register.md#d-186), [D-187](s13-decision-register.md#d-187), [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089), [D-139](s13-decision-register.md#d-139). Evidence: `tests/doc_sync_tests.rs`.

**What the item asked for was already written, and that is the normal outcome rather than a surprise.** W8.5 says *record D-156: the v3 header, and why a format that a corrupt stream can walk to exhaustion is not acceptable in a file the crash path depends on*. That is [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185), written when the work landed in 0.13.12. The number is wrong for the second time in two waves — D-156 has been 0.12.13's `checkpoint()` decision since long before this one, exactly as D-155 was taken when [D-183](s13-decision-register.md#d-183) reached for it — and while this entry is open: **D-157 is `wal_autocheckpoint`, so W11.5's number is taken too.** The remaining *Record D-15x* items mean *record a decision*, and the register is the authority on which number that is.

**So the item is the audit it implies, and an audit's value is what it finds.** The wave's four items produced four entries, one each, all cross-linked; the coverage table's rows for §2.4, §3.3, §3.7 and §5.4 all name the release that closed them; [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors) and [§14](s14-python-bindings.md#14-python-bindings) both carry `SnapshotCorrupt`, which is what `doc_sync_tests` already guarantees for the error set. **What no gate covered was the crate's own shape.** [§3](s0-s3-foundations.md#3-crate-layout)'s tree lost `util/crc32.rs`, added in 0.13.12, and never gained `fuzz/`, added in 0.13.14. Three releases of green gates, in the document a reader opens to find out what exists.

**The tree says of itself that it is an inventory.** Its one exemption is explicit — *"the `tests/` and `bindings/` entries are shapes rather than inventories"*, written in 0.10.0 after it had listed five test files while the suite grew to twenty-eight — and an exemption stated that precisely is a claim that everything else is a list. It was not one. The same paragraph's fix had also been left half-done: the `tests/` entry still said **28** while the directory held **35**, so the entry converted to a shape was still carrying an inventory in its only number. The number is removed rather than corrected, because `scripts/run_rust_suite.py` enumerates the real set and a count in prose is a second copy of something already generated.

**This is the wave's fifth documentation finding and the first one a gate could have caught.** [D-184](s13-decision-register.md#d-184) found [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots)'s note about the write *connection* reading as though it covered the thread, and the plan's stale number; [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185) found the header described as ten bytes since 0.5.5; [D-187](s13-decision-register.md#d-187) found [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy) calling a property test a fuzzer since 0.4.0. Every one of them was found the same way — by writing the next version of a passage and checking the current one against the code — and that method works and does not scale, because it inspects only the passages someone happens to be rewriting. §3's tree is a passage nobody rewrites. It is also, unlike a paragraph of prose, mechanically checkable.

**`every_module_in_src_is_named_in_the_crate_layout_tree` walks `src/` and requires each module's file name to appear in §3's block.** It is deliberately shallow, which is `doc_sync_tests`'s own stated doctrine: a name is enough to catch *something was added and nobody added it here*, and a stricter check — the tree's shape, the comment beside each entry — would fail on a redrawn box, be relaxed the first time it cried wolf, and end up guarding nothing. That is [D-139](s13-decision-register.md#d-139)'s argument and [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089)'s: a gate is worth its false-positive rate or it is deleted. It runs in one direction only — a file with no name in the tree is drift, a name with no file behind it is not, because the tree names `checkpoint.rs` in a parenthetical about a 0.5.0 rename and should keep being able to. On its first run it printed `["util/crc32.rs"]`.

**It covers one of the two things this audit found, and saying so is the point.** `fuzz/` is a *directory*, and the gate says nothing about the top level. A rule there needs to know which directories are deliberately absent from the tree — `target/`, `scripts/`, `.venv/`, `docs/releases/` — which means an exemption list, which is a second place for the same drift to hide, and it would be guarding an event that has happened twice in fourteen months against one that happens whenever a module is added. The asymmetry is deliberate and the uncovered half is named here rather than left to look covered.

**W8.5 gets its own version, and the precedent it departs from is worth stating.** W5.7 closed as *"this item carried no work of its own and rides W5.6's bump"*, which was right: three decisions written at their commits, nothing left to do. This item found a code change, so it takes a version rather than a footnote — and W5.7's observation stands unchanged, that a wave item saying *record D-nnn* is planning for the decisions already foreseen, and every wave so far has produced more than it predicted.

Rejected: *closing the item as satisfied by [D-185](s13-decision-register.md#d-185) without the audit* (the item's remaining value **is** the check, and the check found two things); *correcting 28 to 35* (a count in an entry deliberately converted to a shape will be wrong again inside a wave — it was already wrong by seven); *extending the gate over the top-level directories* (an exemption list is a second place to drift, for an event that has happened twice — above); *fixing §5.11's heading and §3's tree and stopping there* (the instance and not the class, which is what each of the four previous findings did); *a parser that checks the tree's structure rather than its names* (fails on a redrawn box, gets relaxed, guards nothing); *a `#![doc]`-driven module list generated at build time* (the tree's value is the one-line comment beside each entry, which is a judgement about what the module is *for*, and nothing generates that).

<a id="d-189"></a>D-189 — `AttributeMode::AtTime` folds the same log a traversal folds, and only the traversal was asking whether the log could still answer (0.13.16, W9.1). [§5.6](s5-modules.md#56-temporalas_ofrs--valid-time-queries-and-attribute-hydration), [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots), [§5.7](s5-modules.md#57-temporalarchivers--cold-storage), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors), [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174), [D-132](s13-decision-register.md#d-132), [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069), [D-085](s13-decision-register.md#d-085). Evidence: `tests/temporal_tests.rs::hydrating_at_a_recorded_instant_refuses_once_rows_have_been_archived`.

**The finding, which is one sentence long.** `hydrate_at_time` reads `transaction_log` on the hot database. `archive` moves *superseded* rows out of it — [`LOG_ARCHIVABLE`](../../src/temporal/archive.rs) is `recorded_at < cutoff AND a newer row exists` — and a superseded row is precisely what a past instant asks for. The newest row per entity never moves, which is why `reconstruct(now)` is safe by construction and why this arm looked safe too. It was not: for a `ts` back in an archived generation the fold found nothing, the entity was dropped, and the return type is a `Vec` in which absent means retired, means never existed, and means *the answer is in the other file*. §3.2 of the review, and the review's own words for it were "silently degrades".

**The plan named two exits and this takes the one it said to take.** *Union the cold log into the fold, or return a named horizon error* — and **the error is acceptable and silence is not**, because [Doctrine III](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-iii) says assertions are superseded rather than deleted, so a reconstruction quietly omitting superseded state reports something the doctrine says cannot happen. The union is also not available at this signature: `hydrate_attributes` takes a `Connection`, the cold file is reached by `ATTACH` of a path nobody hands it, and adding an `Option<&Path>` to a public function would put the archive path into `TraversalBuilder::execute` and into the Python surface behind it — a wider change than the finding, to make one arm answer questions the crate already answers with `reconstruct`.

**The guard is the one that already existed, applied at the second reader.** `hot_log_answers_for` and [`DbError::RecordedInstantUnreachable`](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors) are [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174)'s, from 0.13.2, written for `TraversalBuilder::as_of_recorded`. Nothing new is invented here, and that is the shape of the finding rather than a convenience: the crate had the right refusal and one of the two surfaces that needed it. Both surfaces fold `transaction_log`, one for topology and one for the text, and the one that arrived first got the guard.

**It goes at the read, not at the caller.** `TraversalBuilder::execute_ids` keeps its own check because it folds the log itself for `links_at_tx`; `execute` therefore now runs the reach test twice, two aggregates over an indexed column on a path that is already a recursive CTE plus a window fold. That is the right way round. The two folds are separately reachable — `hydrate_attributes` is `pub` and re-exported from `macrame::temporal`, and a caller reaching it directly is not going through any traversal — and a guard that lives at the caller is one the next caller does not inherit. Which is the general form of what this entry is about.

**Conservative by the same one bit, and the arithmetic is unchanged.** `hot_log_answers_for` asks whether anything was *ever* archived, not whether this instant survived it, because the cutoff is not recorded hot-side ([D-132](s13-decision-register.md#d-132) refused that marker outright). So an archived database refuses every `recorded` hydrate, including instants a fold would have got right. The trade is [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174)'s and is not re-argued: a partial fold returns *nearly* the right answer, which on a ledger is the worst failure available.

**The refusal is scoped to the arm that reads the log.** `AttributeMode::AtTime` has four cells and only one of them folds; the other three read live `concepts`, which an archive cannot shorten — a concept is archivable only when `retired = 1` ([`CONCEPTS_ARCHIVABLE`](../../src/temporal/archive.rs)) and every live arm filters `retired = 0`, so the rows that leave were already invisible there. The test asserts that half too, because a guard that fired on all four would be the other way of being wrong about this.

**§5.6 had three stale claims and they are corrected here rather than noted.** It opened *"`as_of` is a filtered read of live tables; it never touches the log"* — true of the edge query, false of the module since attribute hydration was added; it gave the signature as `hydrate_attributes(conn, node_ids, ts, mode)`, which [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174) replaced with an `&AsOf` in 0.13.2; and it said `Current` *"emits a `tracing::warn!` when combined with `as_of`"*, which [D-085](s13-decision-register.md#d-085) removed in 0.6.0 in favour of a typed error. `quickref` carried the same stale signature and the wrong return type. Found by writing the next version of the passage and checking the current one — [D-188](s13-decision-register.md#d-188)'s method, which is also [D-188](s13-decision-register.md#d-188)'s complaint about the method, and none of the three is a name a gate can walk to.

Rejected: *threading an `Option<&Path>` into `hydrate_attributes` and unioning `cold.transaction_log`* (the plan's other exit — it widens a public signature, and through it `TraversalBuilder::execute` and the Python surface, to duplicate what `reconstruct` does with the path it already takes); *making the check cheap by running it only when the fold returned fewer rows than ids* (sound — the surviving hot rows for an entity are a suffix, so a complete answer cannot be short — but it derives the guard from an invariant of `LOG_ARCHIVABLE` and monotonic `recorded_at`, and a silence detector that is itself conditional on an invariant is the wrong shape for this defect); *relaxing `hot_log_answers_for` to answer `true` when `ts` is at or after the hot log's newest stamp* (sound by the same argument [`hot_log_reach`](../../src/temporal/replay.rs) already makes, and a real improvement to both callers — but it reverses [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174)'s stated conservatism and flips an existing assertion, which is a decision of its own and not a rider on a silence fix); *leaving the guard in `hydrate_attributes` rather than `hydrate_at_time`* (the dispatch arm is where the mode is read; the fold is where the log is read, and the obligation belongs to the fold); *documenting the limitation instead of raising* (it was already documented — the review is the documentation — and a `Vec` that is quietly short is not fixed by a paragraph).

<a id="d-190"></a>D-190 — An error that names another operation is a claim about that operation, and the claim is what gets tested (0.13.17, W9.2). [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy), [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots), [§5.6](s5-modules.md#56-temporalas_ofrs--valid-time-queries-and-attribute-hydration), [D-189](s13-decision-register.md#d-189), [D-026](s13-decision-register.md#d-026), [D-121](s13-decision-register.md#d-121), [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088). Evidence: `tests/temporal_tests.rs::what_the_hot_log_refuses_the_archive_path_still_answers`.

**The item asks for the demonstration, and names why.** *A test that archives, then reconstructs across the horizon, and asserts the chosen behaviour — this is the finding most likely to be "fixed" by a change nobody can demonstrate.* [D-189](s13-decision-register.md#d-189) took the plan's second exit: refuse, and name `reconstruct`. That is only the right choice if `reconstruct` actually answers, and 0.13.16 shipped without anything checking it. A refusal that redirects is a **claim about another operation**, and the claim is the testable part.

**The round trip, not the branch.** `hydrating_at_a_recorded_instant_refuses_once_rows_have_been_archived` proves the guard fires; it says nothing about whether the caller is better off. This test archives across the horizon, requires the refusal, and then requires `db.reconstruct(instant)` — same connection, plus the archive path the error says is the missing ingredient — to return the concept the refusal would not give.

**The expected value is read, not written.** It is taken from `hydrate_attributes` *while the hot log is still intact* and compared against, rather than spelled `"first"` in both assertions. A literal in both places passes when both readers are wrong the same way, which is [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088)'s lesson — a fixture that satisfies every clause at once cannot say which clause is working — applied to two readers instead of two clauses. Taking the live answer first makes the archive the only variable between them.

**Mutation-discriminated, because a test green on its first run has proved nothing yet.** Forcing `hot_log_reach`'s archive-present arm to `Covers` unconditionally — the plausible wrong fix, and the shape [D-026](s13-decision-register.md#d-026) warns about — fails it at the right assertion with `left: None`. That is §3.2's own failure one layer down: not a wrong answer, an absent one.

**The fixture is a `FakeClock` and could not be anything else.** The archive cutoff is a bound on *transaction* time, so three writes under the wall clock land inside the same microsecond, no cutoff falls between them, and the test would pass while demonstrating nothing — the same argument `archive_window_tests` opens with. Three generations an hour apart, cutoff between the second and the third, instant inside the first. It is now a shared helper, because W9.1's test and this one need the same shape and two copies of a fixture is [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030)'s failure class in a test file.

**The last arm is the horizon itself.** `reconstruct` without the archive path refuses too, so the boundary is a property of the ledger rather than of one reader. Without it the test would be consistent with a hydrate guard that fires on a database where nothing is actually unreachable.

**What it does not cover, stated rather than implied.** One concept and the transaction-time axis. Edges across the horizon are `reconstructing_before_the_archive_cutoff_keeps_every_entity`'s obligation and have been since 0.5.5; snapshot composition across the boundary is [D-049](s13-decision-register.md#d-049)'s. This test is about the redirection, and widening it would blur which claim failed when it goes red.

Rejected: *asserting the refused call and stopping there* (that is W9.1's test, and it leaves the error's own promise unchecked); *hard-coding the expected title in both readers* (above); *making it a property test over generated histories* (the claim is about one boundary and one redirection, and a generator would spend its cases re-proving [D-049](s13-decision-register.md#d-049)'s composition property, which already has one); *asserting the exact `ReplayCorrupt` reason string on the no-path arm* (the message is assembled by `archive_hint` and pinned by `the_unreachable_archive_error_says_how_much_went_and_how_far_back_the_log_reaches`; a second copy of that assertion here would break on a wording change that has nothing to do with this claim); *folding this into 0.13.16* (W9.2 is its own item, and a demonstration written in the same commit as the fix is one nobody has seen fail).

<a id="d-191"></a>D-191 — One visibility predicate, spliced from one constant, and there were three readers rather than the two the plan named (0.13.18, W9.3). [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search), [§5.3](s5-modules.md#53-graphvector_filterrs--strategies-and-the-byte-budget-cost-model), [D-007](s13-decision-register.md#d-007), [D-050](s13-decision-register.md#d-050), [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030). Evidence: `tests/vector_tests.rs::a_retired_concept_is_not_a_vector_search_result`, `tests/vector_filter_tests.rs::a_retirement_is_invisible_to_the_strategy_choice`.

**F-31, stated exactly.** `keyword_search` has carried `AND c.retired = 0` since the day it was written. `search_vector` never had it. So `hybrid_search` fused one ranked list that honoured a retirement with one that did not, and a caller who retired a concept saw it vanish from keyword results and stay in vector results — the same ledger, two answers. Retirement is what *stops belief* in a concept ([Doctrine III](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-iii)); a reader that returns one anyway is reporting something the ledger says is not there, which is [§3.2](s5-modules.md#56-temporalas_ofrs--valid-time-queries-and-attribute-hydration)'s failure at a different surface and the reason this wave holds both.

**The plan's design is taken as written: join `concepts` inside `search_vector`.** One predicate, one place, and the two surfaces that *compose* it — `hybrid_search`'s vector arm and `FilteredVectorSearch`'s `PostFilter` — are fixed by construction rather than by being remembered. The alternative it rejects is what produced the finding: filter after the fact in each caller, and `keyword_search` did its half while nothing propagated the obligation. The cost is *k* seeks on a `TEXT PRIMARY KEY` after `vector_top_k` has already reduced the corpus to *k*, and the inner join drops nothing on its own because the embedding tables carry a foreign key to `concepts`.

**There were three readers, not two, and this is the item's finding rather than its implementation.** `FilteredVectorSearch::run_pre_filter` scores candidate rows straight out of the embedding table and never touches `search_vector`. Fixing only `search_vector` would have left `search_filtered` honouring a retirement under `PostFilter` and ignoring it under `PreFilterCTE` — **a wrong answer selected by a byte estimate**, which reproduces on one machine and not the next and is strictly worse than both arms being wrong the same way. The predicate is therefore a spliced constant, `VISIBLE_CONCEPT`, and the third reader splices it too. `a_retirement_is_invisible_to_the_strategy_choice` is the gate; with the pre-filter arm reverted it fails at k=1 with the two strategies returning different concepts.

**A constant rather than two identical string literals**, because two copies of a predicate that must agree is [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030)'s failure class in SQL, and W9.4 is about to add a second clause to the same predicate. One edit or three is the whole difference.

**`top_k` is a count and stays one, which is the decision the plan said to take rather than discover.** `vector_top_k` chooses its rows before any predicate of ours can see them, so a post-hoc filter returns fewer than *k*; `top_k` quietly becoming a ceiling is a behaviour change for every existing caller. `search_vector` re-asks the index for a larger *k′* when a pass comes up short.

**The escalation is a loop, not an up-front estimate, and the reason is where the cost lands.** `CostEstimator::k_prime` computes `k × corpus / candidates` before running anything, which is right for `FilteredVectorSearch` because it needs the corpus size anyway to price two strategies. Here nothing else needs it, and computing it up front would put a `COUNT(*)` on **every** vector search — including the overwhelmingly common one where nothing is retired and the first pass fills *k* — to serve the case that almost never arises. The loop pays exactly nothing there: one query, no count. Termination is by exhaustion rather than a retry budget: *k′* doubles until the index has been asked for the whole table, and a *k′* at or above the row count means what came back **is** every visible neighbour, a complete answer rather than a truncated one. The row count is read at most once and only on the escalating path.

**What is deferred, and to where.** `hybrid_search` is fixed by construction and nothing asserts it here; that is W9.6's stated job — *all four search surfaces including `search_filtered`* — and pinning it in three places before then would be three copies of an assertion whose value is that it covers all four at once. The valid-time half of the same join is W9.4, and `VISIBLE_CONCEPT` exists in the shape it does so that arrives as one edit.

Rejected: *filtering in Rust after the join rather than in the `WHERE`* (it would make saturation directly observable and remove the row count entirely — but W9.4 adds an interval comparison to this predicate, and splitting one visibility rule across a SQL clause and a Rust closure is how it stops being one rule); *`top_k` as a ceiling* (a silent behaviour change for every caller, and the plan names it as the exit not to take); *the selectivity estimate up front* (above — it prices every search for the rare case); *fixing only `search_vector` and leaving `run_pre_filter`* (a wrong answer chosen by a cost model); *deleting embeddings on retirement* (an embedding is derived from content that still exists — [Doctrine VII](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vii) — and a rehydration or an un-retirement would then have lost it; the test asserts the row is still there).
<a id="d-192"></a>D-192 — Search reads at a stated valid-time instant, the predicate is a function of the parameter index, and a filtered search reads the traversal's instant rather than one of its own (0.13.19, W9.4). [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search), [§5.3](s5-modules.md#53-graphvector_filterrs--strategies-and-the-byte-budget-cost-model), [D-191](s13-decision-register.md#d-191), [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174), [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155), [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030). Closes F-32. Evidence: `tests/hybrid_tests.rs::each_search_arm_reads_at_the_instant_it_was_given`, `tests/vector_filter_tests.rs::a_validity_that_ended_is_invisible_to_the_strategy_choice`.

**The plan's design, taken as written.** `search_vector`, `keyword_search` and `HybridSearch` gain an optional valid-time instant, and with it set the `concepts` join [D-191](s13-decision-register.md#d-191) introduced also bounds `c.valid_from <= t AND t < c.valid_to`. Absent, behaviour is today's. That is the whole of F-32: `concepts` has carried a valid interval since v1 and no retrieval path had ever read it, so a caller could state an instant to a traversal and have the search inside it answer about now.

**One clause, parameterised by where the instant binds.** [D-191](s13-decision-register.md#d-191) made the visibility predicate a spliced constant and said W9.4 would add a second clause to it. It could not stay a constant: the instant is `?4` in the vector search, `?3` in the keyword search, and `?{chunk + 2}` in `PreFilterCTE`, whose ids are variadic. So the predicate is a function of the **parameter index** — not of the value. Splicing the timestamp itself would have kept the constant and put a literal into a statement that is already built per call: an injection surface on a path that takes caller strings, and a distinct statement per instant for the engine to prepare. The index is a number the crate chooses; the timestamp never leaves the parameter list.

**`keyword_search`'s copy folds in here, and this was the release to do it in.** It carried its own `AND c.retired = 0` from the day it was written; W9.3 wrote the shared constant for the two readers that lacked one and left this literal alone. Adding the valid-time bound to the constant and not to the literal is F-31 again with a different column — [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030)'s failure class arriving exactly one release after the constant was created to prevent it.

**`as_of_valid`, and transaction time is refused rather than deferred.** [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174) split `as_of` into `as_of_valid` and `as_of_recorded` because one keyword was asking two questions; naming this parameter `as_of` would ship a third spelling of the axis confusion §3.1 exists to end — which is the plan's stated reason for W9 following W7. Transaction time is not offered at all, and that is a decision rather than an omission: the DiskANN index holds one row per concept with no history, so "search the vectors as they stood at a past `recorded_at`" is a question this access path cannot answer, and offering a parameter that silently means something narrower would be F-35's shape.

**The fourth reader, again.** `FilteredVectorSearch` composes `search_vector` on one arm and scores embedding rows directly on the other, so W9.3's three readers become four the moment the predicate grows. It takes **no instant of its own**: it reads `TraversalBuilder::as_of_valid`. A separate knob would permit a filtered search whose filter is historical and whose ranking is not — a past neighbourhood scored against the present corpus — which is not a configuration anyone wants, it is the bug F-32 describes with a setting attached. And the instant is the *stated* one, not `execute`'s `now_ts`: `now_ts` is always present, so binding it would valid-time-bound every filtered search ever written, which is the silent behaviour change [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155) forbids.

**Both hybrid arms or neither.** RRF fuses two rank lists. An instant on the vector arm alone would fuse what was true then with what is true now into one ranked list that is neither, and the fused score cannot say which arm the anachronism entered by — which makes a half-applied bound worse here than on either arm alone.

**Red first, for the right reason.** The parameter was threaded through every surface while the predicate ignored it, and both gates failed on the value: the vector arm returned `both` at an instant after its validity ended, and the filtered search returned `c0000` where `c0003` was required. The instant *inside* the closed interval is the third arm and the discriminating one — a bound written as `valid_to = the sentinel` passes "absent" and "after" and fails only there.

Rejected: *a `search_vector_at` variant* (two functions that must agree is [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030)'s class, and the crate's precedent is [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174) widening `hydrate_attributes` outright rather than adding a second spelling); *filtering by valid time in each caller after the fact* (that is the shape that produced F-31, and it propagates an obligation rather than a predicate); *`FilteredVectorSearch::as_of_valid` as its own knob* (above); *defaulting the filtered search's instant to `now_ts`* (above); *bounding `links` as well from the search surfaces* (the traversal already does, and a search that re-bounds a topology it did not walk would be pricing a filter twice); *offering `as_of_recorded`* (above).

<a id="d-193"></a>D-193 — Decay is defined against similarity, the vector surface converts and the keyword surface does not, and a filtered search is not offered it at all (0.13.20, W9.5). [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search), [§5.3](s5-modules.md#53-graphvector_filterrs--strategies-and-the-byte-budget-cost-model), [D-192](s13-decision-register.md#d-192), [D-051](s13-decision-register.md#d-051), [D-050](s13-decision-register.md#d-050), [Doctrine II](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-ii). Evidence: `tests/hybrid_tests.rs::a_half_life_ranks_by_age_in_every_arm`, `::a_half_life_without_an_instant_is_refused`, `src/vector/search.rs::decay_tests`.

**The plan names the trap before the multiply, and the trap is real on one surface only.** `search_vector` returns a cosine **distance** and its results ascend; `keyword_search` returns bm25 and its results ascend too, but bm25 arrives *negative* with magnitude growing in relevance. A decay factor in (0, 1] multiplied into a distance makes a stale row look nearer — the bug. The same multiply applied to bm25 moves a hit **toward zero**, which is toward the far end of an ascending best-first list — the correct demotion. So the vector surface converts (`similarity = (2 - d) / 2`, multiply, convert back) and the keyword surface multiplies. **The operation that would have been the defect on one surface is the correct one on the other**, and that is the item's finding rather than its implementation: a single shared `decay(score, factor)` helper would have been written once, reviewed once, and been wrong in one of the two places.

**Non-negative before the multiply, on both.** `vector_distance_cos` ranges over [0, 2], so a raw `1 - d` similarity goes negative for an obtuse angle, and scaling a negative number toward zero *improves* it — the same trap wearing a second face. The vector conversion clamps to [0, 1] first. The keyword side leaves a non-negative rank alone rather than multiplying it, for the mirror reason.

**A half-life without an instant is refused rather than defaulted.** `HalfLifeWithoutInstant` names the fix in the sentence. Age is relative to an instant, and no read path in this crate reads a wall clock — which is what lets the suite pin these answers under a `FakeClock` at all. A default of *now* would make every decayed search silently a search about the present: F-35's shape, and [D-175](s13-decision-register.md#d-175)'s.

**Re-ranking a top-k is not the top-k of the re-ranking.** `HybridSearch::depth` has made that argument since 0.5.5 for fusion — a document ranked twelfth by both arms can outscore one ranked first by a single arm, and it is invisible if neither list was read past ten. Decay is the same argument with age in place of the second arm, so `max(5 × top_k, 50)` is promoted to `rerank_depth` and both use it. It is a **bound and not a guarantee**, stated as one: decay only demotes, so a row outside the pool enters the answer only if five times as many rows ahead of it were pushed below it. The alternative — decaying inside the SQL so the `LIMIT` applies to the decayed order — needs `pow`/`exp` over `julianday` arithmetic, which is not in the bundled amalgamation's guaranteed surface, and would put date parsing in the inner loop of every vector search to serve a parameter that is off by default.

**In `HybridSearch` the decay reaches the arms, not the fusion.** RRF adds *ranks*. A factor applied to the fused score would penalise a number that is already scale-free and would leave both arms' orderings — the only thing RRF reads — exactly as they were. So each arm decays its own similarity and re-sorts, and the fusion sees two lists that already price age.

**`search_filtered` is not given a half-life, and the reason is the property that makes it safe.** `PostFilter` holds the *k′* the cost model priced; `PreFilterCTE` holds every candidate the traversal returned. Decay reorders whatever pool it is handed, so pricing age inside each arm would make the answer a function of the byte estimate — precisely what [D-050](s13-decision-register.md#d-050) forbids, and what `the_strategy_never_changes_the_answer` exists to catch. Offering the parameter and letting that gate fail later would have been the worse outcome; offering it and *widening* `PostFilter`'s pool until the two agree is a real design with a cost model attached, and it is not this item.

**A zero half-life is the defined limit, not a refusal.** Everything with any age at all is fully decayed and only what became true at the instant survives. Strange to ask for, unambiguous to answer, which is the bar for not adding a second error.

**Mutation-discriminated on both surfaces, and the two mutations are different.** Multiplying the distance directly fails `decay_moves_a_hit_away_and_never_toward` in the unit tests and `a_half_life_ranks_by_age_in_every_arm` at the vector assertion, `["a_stale","b_fresh"]` where the reverse was required. Dropping decay from the keyword arm fails the same test at the keyword assertion — and the *sign* mutation there does not fail anything, because on that surface it is not a mutation at all. Finding that out is what corrected this entry's first draft.

**The fixture makes age the only variable.** Two documents with the same title and the same content, so bm25 cannot separate them and the keyword arm falls to its id tie-break; the older one is also the nearer vector and sorts first by id. Every undecayed list is therefore `[a_stale, b_fresh]` and every decayed one must be its exact reverse — required rather than "the order changed", because a decay applied with the wrong sign reverses it too.

Rejected: *one shared `decay(score, factor)`* (above — it is the finding); *decaying in SQL* (above); *defaulting the instant to now* (above); *a `half_life` on `FilteredVectorSearch`* (above); *decaying the fused RRF score* (above); *applying decay at write time or to the stored vector* (the plan's own scope line, and it would make a read parameter a schema migration); *`f32` seconds as the parameter type* (`Duration` is what a span is in Rust, and the Python surface takes `timedelta` or seconds because both are forms a caller already holds).

<a id="d-194"></a>D-194 — The visibility requirement is stated once per surface against one fixture, and the fixture ranks what it hides first (0.13.21, W9.6). [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search), [§5.3](s5-modules.md#53-graphvector_filterrs--strategies-and-the-byte-budget-cost-model), [D-191](s13-decision-register.md#d-191), [D-192](s13-decision-register.md#d-192), [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155), [D-050](s13-decision-register.md#d-050), [Doctrine III](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-iii). Evidence: `tests/search_visibility_tests.rs::every_search_surface_reads_the_same_visibility`, `::an_unstated_instant_reads_the_corpus_on_every_surface`.

**W9.3 and W9.4 were both correct by composition, and composition is the cheapest thing in this crate to change.** `hybrid_search`'s vector arm *is* `search_vector`; `PostFilter` calls it too. Each was fixed the moment the shared predicate was, and every per-surface test would stay green through an arm rewritten for speed or a second access path added for a planner. That is not hypothetical: `run_pre_filter` is exactly such a path, it was added for a cost model, and it is why W9.3 found three readers where the plan named two. So the requirement is now stated per surface rather than inferred from the call graph.

**Five lists, not four.** `search_filtered` is asked once per strategy. `PostFilter` and `PreFilterCTE` share no query, no access path and no ordering mechanism, and a visibility rule that holds under one and not the other is a wrong answer selected by a byte estimate — which reproduces on one machine and not the next, and which [D-050](s13-decision-register.md#d-050) exists to forbid.

**The fixture ranks both hidden concepts first, on every surface, before it hides them.** A test that only asserts absence is passed by a corpus that never had them within reach, and that is the failure mode of every "and it is not returned" assertion written after the fix. Here the first claim is that all five lists lead with `gone` and `ended` while both are believed and current; the hiding is the second claim and the count is the third.

**One total order across two ranking mechanisms, by construction rather than by luck.** Content is `TERM` repeated `8 - rank` times, padded with filler to a constant eight tokens. bm25 reads term frequency *and* document length, so holding length fixed makes tf the only variable and the keyword order the same total order the vectors give — which is what lets one expected list serve all five surfaces instead of five hand-maintained ones that can drift apart. It is asserted before it is relied on.

**The two are hidden by different mechanisms on purpose.** `retired` is a flag and an ended validity is an interval. They are separate terms of one spliced clause, and a surface can carry one and forget the other — which is literally F-31's history, where `keyword_search` had the flag and `search_vector` had neither. `top_k` is required to stay a count in the same assertion, because a filter applied after the index has already chosen its rows quietly returns `k` minus however many were hidden ([D-191](s13-decision-register.md#d-191)'s *k′* escalation is what prevents that, and this is where it is observed rather than described).

**The companion test is the absent knob.** With no instant stated the retirement still applies and the ended validity does not. Without it the gate above is also passed by a build that valid-time-bounds every search whether or not one was asked for — [D-155](s13-decision-register.md#d-155)'s rule, and a change that would break every existing caller while looking like a tightening.

**Mutation-discriminated three ways, each red at the value rather than at a count.** Dropping the valid-time terms from `visible_concept` returns `["ended", ...]`; widening `VISIBLE_CONCEPT` to admit a retirement returns `["gone", ...]` and fails the companion too; and dropping the instant from `run_pre_filter`'s splice **alone** — the mutation no per-surface test could reach, because it is invisible unless two strategies are compared under one instant — fails at `search_filtered/PreFilterCTE` and nowhere else.

Rejected: *asserting the four surfaces in the four files that already test them* (four fixtures drift, and the composition claim is precisely the one no single file can make); *asserting membership rather than the full ordered list* (a surface that returns the right set in the wrong order has lost the ranking, and `top_k`-is-a-count is an ordering claim); *reusing `tests/vector_filter_tests.rs`' 60-concept corpus* (it carries no text, so the keyword and fused surfaces cannot be asked of it at all).

<a id="d-195"></a>D-195 — Operation counts are gated as an `EXPLAIN` fingerprint, rows-scanned is not available and that was measured, and the fixture became shared (0.13.22, W10.1). [D-055](s13-decision-register.md#d-055), [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070), [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089), [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149), [D-150](s13-decision-register.md#d-150), [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042). Evidence: `tests/operation_count_tests.rs`, `examples/opcode_probe.rs`.

**D-055's argument is about timings, and this is what is left once timings are ruled out.** An absolute threshold asserted on a shared runner is an assertion about the runner, and [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070) measured the ~29% session-to-session spread that makes it a bad idea anyway. Neither reaches work *done*. The D-042 / D-059 / D-064 class — an index captures a query because it contains the columns, not because it discriminates — changes how many cursors a statement opens and how it enters each b-tree, and those are integers that do not move when the machine is busy.

**"Rows scanned" is not obtainable here, and that is a measurement rather than an assumption.** `sqlite3_stmt_scanstatus` reports rows visited per loop and requires `SQLITE_ENABLE_STMT_SCANSTATUS` at compile time. `PRAGMA compile_options` on the vendored engine lists fifty options and that is not among them; `examples/opcode_probe.rs` prints the list, so the negative is checkable rather than inherited. The substitute is the VDBE **program** — the thing that would do the scanning — read through `EXPLAIN`, which is a deterministic function of the chosen plan.

**Three integers: cursors opened, seeks issued, rewinds.** `opens` is one per table or index touched, so a covering index shows one where an index-plus-lookup shows two. `seeks` is entering a b-tree at a computed key. `rewinds` is entering it at an end.

**No single one of the three means what its name suggests, and the gate says so with an assertion.** An index range scan open at one end rewinds its cursor exactly as a full table scan does: the fold's `recorded_at <= ?1` is `(2, 0, 1)`, the control query no index serves is `(1, 0, 1)`. They differ only in cursors. So the triple is pinned as a **fingerprint**, and `a_full_scan_and_an_index_range_scan_are_distinguishable` requires that the two shapes agree on seeks and rewinds and disagree on opens — which stops the gate being "simplified" later to `rewinds == 0` on queries where a rewind is right.

**Strictly finer than the registry's assertion, demonstrated rather than claimed.** Take the traversal's recursive step and add `l.properties`, a column `idx_lc_traversal_cover` does not carry. The same index is still picked and the same seek still happens, so `index_plan_tests`' `plan.contains(name)` still passes; `opens` goes from 1 to 2 because the row must now be read. That gap is exactly D-042's class, and `losing_coverage_moves_the_count_and_not_the_registry_assertion` holds both halves in one test so neither can be read without the other.

**One honest qualification, because the first draft of this entry overstated it.** `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` prints `SEARCH l USING COVERING INDEX …` and drops the word when coverage is lost, so a plan assertion *could* catch this by matching on `COVERING`. The claim is therefore about the assertion [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089)'s registry makes — which keys on the index **name**, deliberately, because its question is "what reads this index" — and not about what the planner is willing to say. The count is still the better instrument for this: a word says a plan is covering, an integer says how many cursors are open when it is not, and only the second one moves by a measurable amount when something gets worse without getting different.

**The fixture is now `tests/common/plan_fixture.rs`, shared.** It was private to `index_plan_tests` until a second file needed the same database. Copying it would have produced two fixtures both called "the populated one" — [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088)'s defect, and the reason the plan and the counts pinned against it are comparable at all is that the rows and the `sqlite_stat1` behind them are identical by construction. `assert_has_statistics` moved with it: without that guard both files would pass while testing the empty-database planner under a name claiming otherwise ([D-150](s13-decision-register.md#d-150)).

**When this goes red on a dependency bump it is a plan review, not a bug.** The gate names the remedy in its own failure message — re-run the probe, read the sweep, change the number deliberately — because a fingerprint nobody re-derives is a fingerprint of whatever the code did last.

Rejected: *asserting total opcode count* (moves on engine changes that alter nothing about the plan, so it is brittle without being informative); *asserting `rewinds == 0` as a universal rule* (false for three of the six pinned queries, and the reason the control exists); *timing gates* ([D-055](s13-decision-register.md#d-055), unchanged — wall-clock benchmarks stay advisory); *deriving the counts from `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` text* (that is the coarser claim this file was written to be finer than).

<a id="d-196"></a>D-196 — The two-dimensional index question closes as *no index*, because the two axes never meet on a table (0.13.23, W10.6). Closes F-33. [§5.2](s5-modules.md#52-graphbuilderrs--traversal-valid-time-and-attribute-fidelity), [D-174](s13-decision-register.md#d-174), [D-195](s13-decision-register.md#d-195), [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089), [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042), [D-055](s13-decision-register.md#d-055). Evidence: `tests/bitemporal_plan_tests.rs`, `examples/bitemporal_index_probe.rs`.

**The question was measured before it was answered, which is the whole instruction the item carried.** W7.1 made a cross-axis read expressible and nobody had asked what one costs. The survey that raises the question is usually cited for spatial indexing of the two temporal dimensions; Kaufmann et al. (2015) declines to do that and keeps one 1-D index per domain, and the survey's own Fig. 9 concludes no single index is optimal. So the literature says *measure*.

**The transaction-time half is already done.** A traversal with `as_of_recorded` planned as `SEARCH transaction_log USING INDEX idx_txlog_time (recorded_at<?)` before this item started. That is option 1 for that domain, shipped in W3 and justified in the registry.

**Adding the valid-time instant changes the plan not at all, and that is the finding rather than the result.** The two bounds do not live on the same relation. `recorded_at` is a column of `transaction_log`. The valid-time bound is applied to the walk's join against `links_at_tx` — a materialised fold whose `valid_from` and `valid_to` are produced by `json_extract` over the winning payload — which is one derivation past the point where any index on the base table could be consulted. **F-33 asked which index should serve a cross-axis predicate, and there is no cross-axis predicate on a table to serve.** The question has no object rather than a difficult answer.

**Option 2 was built rather than argued about.** A composite over `(recorded_at, json_extract(payload, '$.valid_from'))` is created, `ANALYZE` is re-run, and the planner **does** take it in preference to `idx_txlog_time` — and the plan reads `(recorded_at<?)`. The second column is never consulted. Every other line of the plan is identical. So the concrete cost of the thing the paper is cited to recommend is: a wider index on the crate's highest-volume table, an index write per logged row forever, and no read that gets faster. This is [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089)'s unread index arriving as a *proposal* rather than as an archaeology finding, which is the better place to catch it.

**Option 3 is rejected on arithmetic the plan already stated.** `rtree` coordinates are float32 and `rtree_i32` is int32; neither can hold a microsecond epoch, and the exact recheck a bounding-box pre-filter requires would be against columns that do not exist on the table it would be built over.

**What the plans actually cost is not a temporal predicate.** `ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY entity_id ORDER BY seq_id DESC)` sorts the whole `recorded_at`-bounded slice — `USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY` — and that is the dominant line in every arm. The only index lever available is to force the fold onto `idx_txlog_entity`, which supplies the partition order and turns the seek into a full `SCAN`, still leaving `USE TEMP B-TREE FOR RIGHT PART OF ORDER BY`: strictly worse unless the `recorded_at` bound is unselective. Recorded as where a future item should look, and deliberately not fixed here — it is a different question from the one F-33 asked, and answering it inside this item is how a measurement wave turns into an optimisation wave nobody scoped.

**The closure is a gate, not a paragraph.** A decision that reads "we measured and there was nothing to build" is exactly the kind re-litigated by the next person, who did not. `tests/bitemporal_plan_tests.rs` pins the three claims — the seek, the plan equality, and the candidate index being used on its leading column only — and each failure message says the same thing: this is not a regression, it means F-33 is due for review.

**The fixture is written through the public API and analysed with `analyze()`, not built by hand.** `tests/common/plan_fixture.rs` inserts into `concepts` and `links` directly, so its `transaction_log` is empty — and the transaction-time half of every question here reads exactly that table. Stating which fixture and why is [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088)'s rule; using the wrong one would have measured an empty-table plan under a populated name, which is [D-150](s13-decision-register.md#d-150) one table across.

Rejected: *an R\*Tree pre-filter* (above — the coordinate types cannot hold the values); *a composite over both axes* (above — built, chosen, and used on one column); *an expression index on the valid-time JSON alone* (same reason, one column earlier); *rewriting the fold so the two axes do meet on a table* — i.e. materialising `valid_from` and `valid_to` as real columns of `transaction_log` — which is a schema migration and a write-path cost to serve a read nobody has timed, and is exactly the shape this item exists to refuse; *timing the fold* ([D-055](s13-decision-register.md#d-055) — and the plan is the part that generalises off this machine).

<a id="d-197"></a>D-197 — `CommandKind::Analyze` splits into `Analyze` and `Optimize`, and both stay budget-counted — for opposite reasons (0.13.24, W10.5). Settles [D-168](s13-decision-register.md#d-168). [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149), [D-152](s13-decision-register.md#d-152), [D-166](s13-decision-register.md#d-166), [§5.10](s5-modules.md#510-metricsrs--what-the-actor-holds-the-lock-for). Evidence: `examples/optimize_hold.rs`, `tests/actor_metrics_tests.rs`.
