**The split is the prerequisite, not the change.** [D-168](s13-decision-register.md#d-168) declined to decide whether `Analyze` should be budget-exempt, and the reason was not caution: the kind covered [`Database::optimize`] as well as [`Database::analyze`], and `close()` calls `optimize()` unconditionally, so any judgement made about the explicit call would have arrived at the automatic one without being made about it. That is [D-152](s13-decision-register.md#d-152)'s defect reflected — there a shared kind *granted* `Rehydrate` an exemption nobody had decided; here a shared kind would have *laundered* one. The exemption question was unanswerable until the kinds were two.

**Both answers came back "not exempt", and if the reasons were the same this entry would not be worth writing.** They are not, and the difference is the finding.

**`Analyze` cannot fill in the table's `Bound` column.** `ANALYZE` is one indivisible statement whose cost tracks the data — 5.26 ms at 10,000 edges, 19.1 ms at 40,000, against a 3 ms budget ([D-166](s13-decision-register.md#d-166)). The honest entry would be "the size of the table, damped 3–4× by `analysis_limit`", which is the absence of a bound rather than one, and a row that cannot state its bound is the exemption table admitting what it exists to prevent. Unchanged from D-168; restated because it is now a decision about one caller instead of two.

**`Optimize`'s violations are rare and they are the informative ones.** Measured on a 40,000-edge ledger (`examples/optimize_hold.rs`): **10.7 ms the first time**, on a database that has never been analysed — there is nothing incremental about the first call — and **90–220 µs every time after**, comfortably inside the budget, *including immediately after a bulk load that doubled the ledger*. It goes over budget only when it actually re-analyses something, and then by a lot: **460 ms**, once the table had grown 25×. So the hold distribution is bimodal by construction, and an `optimize` row in `budget_violations()` is not a complaint but a report — it names the handle closes that cost something. Exempting the kind would delete precisely that distinction, which is the one an operator wants.

**A measurement made for the exemption falsified the premise of a different scheduled item, and that is recorded here rather than acted on here.** `PRAGMA optimize` does not re-analyse because rows arrived; it applies **its own staleness ratio**, and the ratio is large. Read off `sqlite_stat1` across the call rather than inferred from a timing: growth of **2× and 5× both left the statistics untouched**, and only at **25×** were they rewritten. W2.2 scheduled `PRAGMA optimize` "after bulk load … when a run exceeds a threshold worth picking by measurement", and the measurement says a threshold on the *run* cannot make `PRAGMA optimize` do anything: below its ratio it declines whatever the caller picked, and above it it costs what `analyze()` costs. **W10.2 is where that gets decided**; putting the call site in this release would be answering W10.2's question inside W10.5's, which is how a wave stops being auditable.

**The mechanics of the split, stated because D-152 found them dangerous.** `Optimize` is appended at the end of the enum and of `ALL`, per the declaration-order contract — those positions are persisted in two languages and `#[repr(u8)]` pins them to whatever the order *is*, not to a safe one. `LowPriCommand::Analyze` keeps its `incremental` flag and `kind()` now reads it, which introduces exactly two regressions worth naming: both arms mapping to one kind, and the flag read backwards. Neither shows up in a total, so `analyze_and_optimize_are_counted_apart` checks each call against **both** counters at the moment it runs. `CHUNK_BUDGET`'s documented table names neither kind, so `the_budget_exemptions_and_their_documented_table_agree` holds across the split without an edit — which is the outcome that says the two lists really were one fact.

Rejected: *exempting `Optimize` because `close()` calls it and a close should be quiet* (the close that costs 460 ms is the one worth knowing about, and quiet is what D-168 refused); *exempting `Analyze` now that it is only the explicit call* (an explicit caller is necessary for an exemption and not sufficient — every existing row also states a bound); *lowering [`ddl::ANALYSIS_LIMIT`](../../src/schema/ddl.rs) until the hold fits* (it trades statistical quality, the one thing the pragma governs, for a counter — sampling too little to separate the two `source_id`-leading indices is the whole purpose of D-149); *folding `Optimize` into `RebuildFts` as generic maintenance* (their holds have unrelated shapes, which is the same argument `ShadowRebuild` is separate on); *adding the bulk-load call site here* (above — that is W10.2).

<a id="d-198"></a>D-198 — The bulk-load `PRAGMA optimize` call site is **not built**: statistics change no plan this crate has, measured (0.13.25, W10.2). Completes W2.2. [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089), [D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149), [D-150](s13-decision-register.md#d-150), [D-195](s13-decision-register.md#d-195), [D-197](s13-decision-register.md#d-197), [§5.10](s5-modules.md#510-metricsrs--what-the-actor-holds-the-lock-for). Evidence: `examples/bulk_optimize.rs`, `tests/statistics_effect_tests.rs`.

**W2.2 scheduled two call sites and 0.12.4 shipped one.** `close()` runs `optimize()` ([D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149)); the other — "at the end of `write_concepts` / the bulk edge path when a run exceeds a threshold worth picking by measurement rather than by taste" — was left to this item with an instruction to measure the threshold instead of guessing it.

**Measuring it turned up the question underneath it.** [D-197](s13-decision-register.md#d-197) had already established that a threshold on the *run* cannot make `PRAGMA optimize` act: the staleness test is SQLite's own ratio and it is large. But on a **fresh** database the first call is a full analysis and the ratio never applies — so the call site would fire exactly where a bulk load most plausibly matters, which made it look justified. The remaining question is the only one that decides anything: what does the caller get?

**Nothing, measured.** `examples/bulk_optimize.rs` loads a skewed graph into a fresh database at 90, 500, 5,000 and 40,000 edges, runs `optimize()`, and compares before and after. `sqlite_stat1` goes 0 → 7 rows every time, for between **0.26% and 21% of the import's own cost** — and **no plan and no opcode count moves**, across the six queries the registry justifies an index with plus a join whose order the planner is free to choose. **A maintenance call whose output nothing reads is [D-089](s13-decision-register.md#d-089)'s unread index in a different costume**, and this crate treats those as a red test rather than a harmless cost.

**Why that is structural rather than a property of one fixture.** `index_plan_tests` already asserts that every registry query gets its index on an empty database *and* on a populated, analysed one, which is the same fact from the other side: each index in `ddl::CREATE_INDICES` has exactly one reader and each reader is shaped to seek on it. Where there is one candidate access path, statistics have nothing to choose between. The join arm is in the query set precisely because the other six could not answer the question — a set made only of single-candidate seeks would be agreeing with itself.

**[D-149](s13-decision-register.md#d-149) is not overturned and the distinction matters.** `close()`'s call costs ~0.1 ms on an idle database, and what it buys is that the estimates are right for the query nobody has written yet; [D-150](s13-decision-register.md#d-150)'s two fixtures exist so the crate notices the day the two planners diverge. What is refuted is narrower and is the thing W2.2 actually scheduled: that a **second, automatic** call site buys a caller something today.

**A closure of this shape needs a gate or it is a paragraph.** `tests/statistics_effect_tests.rs` compares the plan text **and** [D-195](s13-decision-register.md#d-195)'s opcode triple — a plan is a summary and can hide a program change, and "statistics changed nothing" is exactly the claim that needs the finer instrument — across a **third fixture** added for it: the same rows with no `ANALYZE`. `migrated` against `populated_and_analysed` differs in rows *and* statistics, so agreement there cannot say which one did not matter; `populated_without_statistics` against `populated_and_analysed` differs in statistics alone. Both directions are guarded: the analysed side is checked to have statistics, the bare side is checked not to, and a second test confirms the fingerprint can see a difference when there is one, so the negative is not passed by a blind instrument. If it goes red, the message says W10.2 is due for review rather than inviting a fix.

Rejected: *adding the call site anyway because it is cheap* (0.26% of a large import is cheap and 21% of a small one is not, and neither number is the objection — an unread write is refused here on principle, per D-089); *calling `analyze()` instead of `optimize()` after a bulk load* (unconditional, 19.1 ms at 40,000 edges, and it would refresh statistics nothing reads for six times the cost); *a threshold on the run size* (D-197 — it cannot reach the ratio the pragma applies, and on the fresh-database case where the pragma does act, the result is still no plan change); *making it opt-in via `Tuning`* (a knob whose two settings are measurably indistinguishable is a question asked of every caller with no right answer); *deleting `close()`'s call as well* (its cost is ~0.1 ms on the idle path and its value is the estimates being right for a future query — this measurement is about a *second* call site, not about D-149).
